Housekeeping habit worth repeating periodically, especially after adding any new dataset: run `du -sh .git` and compare to the working tree size (`du -sh --exclude=.git *`). A large, growing gap between the two means old mistake-uploads are bloating history again — see the git-history entry in TECHNICAL-NOTES.md for exactly how that keeps happening and how to avoid it (verify a binary upload's destination and integrity before it's committed, not after).

Operational note for a fresh conversation: this repo's local checkout has repeatedly gone stale mid-session (not just between sessions) on past branches used this session, seen resetting to the exact same old commit more than once rather than drifting progressively further, which points to the session's container/disk occasionally being reset or reprovisioned mid-session, not ordinary "someone else pushed and this checkout didn't pull" drift. Don't assume the working directory reflects what's actually on GitHub — if a stop hook complains about untracked files, or before making any change generally, run `git fetch` against whatever branch this session is actually working on, confirm `git diff --stat HEAD` is empty (no real uncommitted work) before assuming anything's lost, then fast-forward (`git merge origin/<branch> --ff-only`). Safe to do without asking when there's no real uncommitted work.

What this project is

epiSTEMic is a browser-based platform for authentic computational science investigations targeting grades 6 through undergraduate. The core technical differentiator is Python running entirely in the browser via Pyodide (WebAssembly) — no backend, no login, no external dependencies. The platform is built-to-be-acquired, targeting educational publishers (Macmillan Learning, Cengage, Carolina Biological, HMH, Amplify) over a 5-7 year horizon. Funding path is non-dilutive: NSF STTR, DRK-12, Ben Franklin Technology Partners, KIZ tax credits. Penn State OTM invention disclosure is in progress. I-Corps regional cohort application is underway.

Current stage: this is bootstrapped, pre-funding proof-of-concept work ("vibecoding" with Claude) to produce lessons good enough to get real user/teacher feedback with, not production engineering. The whole codebase is expected to eventually be handed to a hired software engineer once funded, who will build the parts that are explicitly deferred below (accounts, LMS grade passback, any real backend). That handoff is a reason to keep new work as clean, static, and well-documented as the existing units, not a reason to start building backend infrastructure now — infrastructure decisions belong to that future engineer, not to this phase.

Collaborators: Kathy (Penn State, co-inventor, grantsmanship), Duke University biological anthropology researcher (provided primate datasets), Mr. Besong (high school physics teacher, climate investigation notebook), Wes and Becca (external, `reinhart-group/camel-2dcc` — built the CAMEL notebook catalog and Kathy's Data Literacy Summit session; see the "CAMEL folder" section near the end of this file).

Data licensing (confirmed with Duke, 2026-08-28): the MorphoSource specimen data used for MS1 (and the limb-length/CSG datasets more broadly) comes through Despina, Duke's deidentified-data platform, and is open source — no license is needed to use it in this platform. Not an open diligence item; don't re-raise it as a risk unless something changes.

Working principle throughout this project: The investigation is not a matching game. Mystery specimens are chosen so no single metric resolves the question. Students must synthesize multiple lines of quantitative evidence and make claims with explicitly stated uncertainty. This is intentional and must be preserved in all design decisions.

Current build state

The platform is multi-unit now. index.html at the repo root is the epiSTEMic splash page: logo, tagline, and a unit-selection grid. It is the live root at https://mmj125.github.io/epistemic-demo/. It links to two unit tiles, both now badged "Live" — Biological Anthropology (links to anthropology/student-landing.html as the primary action, with a secondary "Instructor resources" link to anthropology/instructor.html) and Meteorology & Climate Science (links to meteorology/investigation.html, the guided unit; the bare research sandbox, meteorology/energy-balance-sandbox.html, is embedded within it via iframe rather than linked separately from the splash page — no instructor resource page exists for meteorology yet, unlike anthropology's card). A non-functional "Sign In" placeholder sits in the top corner; accounts are not built yet.

Each unit's files live in their own top-level folder so additional subjects can be added the same way. The anthropology unit is under anthropology/:

anthropology/instructor.html — teacher-facing resource page. Contains four module cards with slide deck downloads, specimen entry links, a downloadable lesson plan, and an instructor reference table with specimen identities. Explicitly not linked from student-facing pages (banner + footer both say so) — the splash page respects this by routing its primary click into student-landing.html, not here.

anthropology/student-landing.html — student-facing landing page with "phone call from someone who found bones" narrative framing, six locomotor type cards with IMI ranges, four YouTube video embeds showing locomotion types, five specimen cards linking to the investigation, and a separate, visually distinct "After You Finish The Investigation" section linking to assessment.html (kept apart from the specimen grid so it reads as a later summative step, not a 6th mystery specimen). That section is locked by default (greyed card, no link) and only unlocks into a clickable assessment.html link once a localStorage flag (epistemic_argument_feedback_received, set by investigation.html's submitArgument() on the first successful feedback response) is present. There's no backend or login, so this is client-side state per browser, not enforced completion — a student can clear storage or switch devices/browsers to bypass it — but it replaces what used to be an always-clickable link with only a text note asking students to finish first.

anthropology/investigation.html — the main investigation tool, single file with URL parameter switching (?specimen=MS11 through MS14). Contains five tabs: Limb Proportions, Body Mass, Olecranon Index, Bone Geometry, Build Argument. Loads 478-specimen limb length dataset and 111-specimen CSG dataset as embedded JSON. Runs Pyodide for scatter/box plots via Plotly. AI formative feedback on student arguments calls the Gemini API (gemini-3.5-flash, free tier) through a Cloudflare Worker proxy (cloudflare-worker/worker.js) so the API key never sits in this public page. Reveal (Tab 5) is gated behind a revised argument submission, not just the first one — see Tab 5 detail below.

anthropology/lesson-plan.pdf, anthropology/slides-*.pptx.pdf, anthropology/MomentMacro_CSATS.txt — instructor-facing downloads, all linked from instructor.html.

anthropology/assessment.html — end-of-unit assessment, linked from instructor.html (opens in a new tab, not a download). Generates a randomized transfer specimen client-side on load using a seeded PRNG (mulberry32); the seed lives in the URL so a reload keeps the same specimen but a fresh visit gets a new one. No two students (or sections, or semesters) get the same underlying numbers by default — this is the deliberate fix for the standard problem of assessment answers getting passed down. Randomization is template-based across three archetypes (suspensory_ceboid, cursorial_cercopithecoid, leaping_lemuroid), each with its own genuine multi-metric tension (mirroring the mystery specimens' design), not naive independent-field randomization — that would risk generating a specimen that trivially resolves on one metric or is biologically implausible. Real embedded DATA and CSG_DATA (duplicated from investigation.html, not fetched) form the comparative backdrop in every chart; only the focal specimen's own numbers are generated. Five "data displays" (limb proportion scatter with a species/superfamily view toggle, IMI box plot, body mass table, an interactive click-to-mark OUI number line, CSG box plots with a CA/Imax-Imin toggle) plus ten questions across four parts: reading the displays (with an evidence-selection checklist per question — deliberately not every display is useful for every question), comparing to the student's own earlier mystery specimen, a full claim/evidence/conflicting-evidence/adaptation argument, and a reflection. Submission generates a printable summary (student responses + the exact specimen data they worked from) and a gated "reveal instructor grading reference" section that is auto-computed from that student's specific random values using the same classification logic as the rest of the platform (IMI category ranges, OUI_GROUPS from Drapeau 2004, the CSG interpretation heuristics), not written by hand per student. No backend or login; degrades gracefully if the Plotly CDN fails (each chart's render is independently wrapped, so one failure doesn't take down the body mass table, the OUI canvas, or the rest of the form). Operational note for instructors: the anti-sharing property only holds if students each open the assessment fresh — sharing the literal URL with a ?seed= already in it hands over the same specimen, so distribute the bare assessment.html link, not a URL a previous student generated.

logo.png at the repo root is the shared platform logo, referenced by both index.html and anthropology/instructor.html (via a relative ../logo.png path). Keep it at root rather than duplicating it per unit as more units are added.

The five investigation tabs in detail

Tab 1 (Limb Proportions): Configurable scatter plot and IMI box plot from 478 primate specimens across four superfamilies. Students enter four limb measurements (humerus, radius, femur, tibia), IMI calculates live, mystery specimen plots as a star. Grouping by superfamily/common name/genus. Axes fully configurable. For MS1 only, a "View & Measure (MorphoSource)" panel sits above the measurement form with a bone-switcher (humerus/radius/femur/tibia) driving a real MorphoSource 3D mesh viewer iframe, so the student measures the actual bone model with the viewer's own ruler tool instead of just typing in given numbers — see the MS1 paragraph below.

Tab 2 (Body Mass): Femoral head SI diameter input, five taxon-specific regression equations output simultaneously. Equations: Lemurs exp(2.548×ln(FHSI)+1.696)÷1000, South American Monkeys exp(2.729×ln(FHSI)+1.420)÷1000, Old World Monkeys exp(2.388×ln(FHSI)−4.560), Apes 10^(2.6465×log₁₀(FHSI)−2.4093), Humans 10^(1.7125×log₁₀(FHSI)−1.048). Contextual note auto-generates based on whether human estimate is biologically plausible. For MS1 only, the femur MorphoSource viewer reappears here so the student measures femoral head diameter on the same real mesh rather than the length measured in Tab 1.

Tab 3 (Olecranon Index): OL and UL inputs, OUI calculates live, plots as triangle on a canvas number line against six locomotor group ranges from Drapeau (2004). Groups staggered at three heights to prevent label overlap. Note explicitly states mid-range overlap means OUI alone cannot resolve classification. For MS1 only, a MorphoSource ulna viewer sits above the OL/UL inputs.

Tab 4 (Bone Geometry): ImageJ link bar at top. Drag-and-drop upload for femur and humerus log files (tab-separated ImageJ MomentMacro output: Name, CA, Imax, Imin, Imax/Imin, J). Three switchable charts: log-scale J scatter with 1:1 reference line, Imax/Imin box plots, log-scale cortical area box plots. Femur/humerus/both toggle on box plots. For MS1 only, a "Measure Directly (no ImageJ)" panel sits above the ImageJ upload path — see the MS1 paragraph below for what it does and why its numbers are explicitly estimated, not calibrated.

Tab 5 (Build Argument): Four structured prompts (claim, supporting evidence, conflicting evidence, adaptation/natural selection). Summary pills show all measurements student collected across tabs. Submission calls FEEDBACK_ENDPOINT (a Cloudflare Worker, see cloudflare-worker/worker.js) which holds the Gemini API key server-side and forwards to gemini-3.5-flash. Generates 240-290 word formative feedback that reads all four analyses, does not reveal identity, pushes back on single-metric arguments. The "Reveal identity" button and Identity Revealed panel live here (moved off Tab 1's sidebar so the unlock sits next to the action that earns it) and only arm after the student's second, revised argument submission gets feedback — the first submission's feedback alone does not unlock it, tracked via an in-page submission counter (argumentSubmitCount). A separate, smaller "assessment nudge" card also sits on this tab, always visible so it's discoverable even mid-demo: it starts in a greyed, locked state and flips to a live link to assessment.html once the first submission's feedback comes back, gated on the same epistemic_argument_feedback_received localStorage flag that unlocks the assessment section on student-landing.html. This was deliberately kept as a same-tab link rather than folded into investigation.html as a structural 6th tab, since the assessment generates an unrelated randomized specimen and is meant to be instructor-timed, not an automatic next step in the tab sequence.

The five mystery specimens

MS-11: Alouatta seniculus (Red Howler Monkey), Rio Verde Forest Reserve Colombia. IMI in arboreal quadruped range. Body mass 5-10kg distinguishes from larger species with similar IMI.

MS-12: Presbytis obscura (Dusky Leaf Monkey), Bukit Hijau National Park Malaysia. IMI ~90-91 in dense cercopithecoid overlap zone. Multiple locomotor strategies produce similar values.

MS-13: Indri indri, West African forest (deliberate misdirection — Indri is Malagasy). IMI ~66.5 clusters near humans and sifaka for entirely different evolutionary reasons. Two misdirections: field context wrong, IMI human-like but animal is a vertical clinger and leaper.

MS-14: Erythrocebus patas (Patas Monkey), Kiboko Savanna Sub-Saharan Africa. IMI overlaps with arboreal cercopithecoids despite highly terrestrial cursorial lifestyle. Fastest primate on ground ~55km/h. Habitat context is essential.

MS-1: Papio sp. (Baboon), Kalambo Escarpment, East African Rift Valley. IMI in the low-to-mid 90s sits squarely in arboreal quadruped range despite baboons being among the most terrestrial monkeys — habitat context alone can't resolve it; cross-sectional bone robustness is the deciding evidence. Unlike MS11-14, MS1 uses real specimen tooling rather than given numbers or an uploaded file: Tabs 1-3 embed real MorphoSource 3D mesh viewers (iframes onto morphosource.org manifests) the student measures directly with the viewer's own ruler tool, and Tab 4 adds a "Measure Directly (no ImageJ)" panel — an in-browser Otsu-threshold + pixel-moment CSG tool (canvas-based, algebraically the same math as the ImageJ MomentMacro) running on MS1's own real femur/humerus cross-section images (anthropology/sample-data/MS1_*_midshaft.png), as an alternative to the ImageJ-upload path the other four specimens use. That tool's px/mm scale (femur 9.08, humerus 10.89) is explicitly estimated — back-calculated from the image's own pixel dimensions against published baboon diameter ranges, not a lab calibration — which is an accepted tradeoff specifically because MS1 is a known teaching specimen the platform already runs full analyses on elsewhere, not a novel-research measurement; good for relative bone-robustness comparisons and supporting a claim, not for precise absolute values. Ported from anthropology/prototypes/investigation-ms1-preview.html into the live tool as a 5th specimen; all MS1-specific markup and JS is guarded on specimenKey==="MS1" and hidden by default so MS11-14 are unaffected, and the default specimen (MS13, via the ?specimen= URL param) is unchanged.

Datasets embedded in anthropology/investigation.html

Limb length: 478 specimens, four superfamilies (Hominoidea, Cercopithecoidea, Ceboidea, Lemuroidea), 21 common names. Fields: superfamily, genus, species, common_name, sex, humerus, radius, femur, tibia, forelimb, hindlimb, imi. Source: two Excel files (Limb_Length_Dataset_A and B) from Duke University collaborator.

CSG: 111 specimens, 11 species (Human, Chimp, Gorilla, Baboon, Orangutan, Siamang, Brown Capuchin, Sifaka, Squirrel Monkey, Howler Monkey, Crab-eating Macaque). Fields: common_name, femur_ca, humerus_ca, femur_j, humerus_j, femur_imax_imin, humerus_imax_imin. Log scale used throughout because size range spans two orders of magnitude.

Engineering unit (structural/civil — cantilever truss design, ground structure method) — very early, in progress

Source materials: Matt provided `Ground_Structure_3x6.mat` (a real MASTAN2 model file) and `Decision_Criteria_Cantilever_FINAL.xlsx` (his existing multi-criteria decision tool), both copied as-is into `engineering/prototypes/` for reference. This unit adapts a real classroom activity (most often taught middle-school tech ed, also used undergrad non-majors): students physically build cantilever trusses out of K'NEX, using MASTAN2 (a Windows desktop matrix-structural-analysis tool, cannot run in-browser) to predict deflection before building. Goal: minimize a weighted combination of deflection, cost, and mass under a fixed tip load — the "ground structure" method, where the full candidate member set is a fixed 28-node grid with 81 possible members, and the design problem is choosing which subset to keep.

The .mat file's model, decoded (do not re-derive this, it's already reverse-engineered and verified): 28 nodes, 7 columns × 4 rows, 4.1875in grid spacing. All 4 left-edge nodes (0-based indices 0, 6, 12, 18) are pinned supports — easy to miss, cost real debugging time (first pass only found 2 of the 4 and got wrong answers). Load is -1.5 lbf downward at node index 24 (bottom-right corner, 1-based node 25) — this is Matt's classroom-scale number, does NOT cleanly convert from "2kg" (2kg×g≈4.4lbf, not 1.5) so treat 1.5lbf as the real load, not a metric conversion. Material is K'NEX plastic: E=30800 psi, A=0.01284 in². All 81 candidate members (elem_info's first 2 columns, 1-based node pairs) are simultaneously present/active in this saved file — it's the full ground structure baseline, not a student's chosen subset.

Decisions locked in with Matt for this build (don't re-ask):
- Preserve the legacy numbers above exactly (not rebuilt with clean SI values) — keeps the browser tool cross-checkable against Matt's real MASTAN2/physical results.
- Auto-derive the bill of materials (rod colors/counts) from the truss topology a student selects, rather than requiring manual entry like the Excel tool. Important correction: the Excel's rod catalog (Black 1.5in ... Orange 20in) is for standard K'NEX and does NOT apply — the real activity uses mini K'NEX (purple 94mm, blue 138mm, both 3mm thick, per Matt directly). See the "Rod-length mapping" paragraph below for the resolved purple/blue assignment.
- Connector (hub) auto-derivation: the Excel's connector formula was written for standard K'NEX and doesn't apply — resolved with real mini K'NEX numbers from Matt directly, see "Connector mass and cost" below.
- Paired with a real physical K'NEX build (not a standalone virtual-only unit like the other two) — the browser tool is pre-build prediction; Tab 4 (planned) is where predicted vs. measured deflection get compared after building.
- No narrative/mystery framing needed (unlike anthropology/meteorology) — present the design brief directly, since the optimization tradeoff itself carries the investigation. Keep language accessible to middle-school-tech-ed level while still working for undergrad non-majors.

The real physical build is 3D, not 2D (clarified 2026-09-02, changes the tab plan below): the actual K'NEX structure is two mirrored copies of this exact 2D ground-structure shape, connected front-to-back with fixed purple/blue cross-bracing. The -1.5lbf modeled load is deliberately half the real hanging mass, since the two mirrored faces share it — this was already correctly baked into the legacy MASTAN numbers, it just wasn't explained anywhere in the UI (now fixed, see below). Decided NOT to make the interactive tool 3D: splitting a symmetric space truss into a planar sub-analysis at half-load is a standard, legitimate real engineering simplification, not a compromise — and a real 3D tool (WebGL/Three.js, camera controls, 3D hit-testing) is a large build for an audience (8th grade, see below) that can't use trig to interpret 3D force resolution anyway. This only holds as long as the cross-bracing between the two faces stays fixed/given rather than becoming its own student design choice — if that ever changes, revisit. `truss-designer.html`'s header now states this explicitly: it's one face of the real build, the load is half the real mass because both faces share it, and any member removed on screen should be removed from both physical faces (the cross-bracing stays as built).

Audience constraint (clarified 2026-09-02): this is currently taught in 8th grade (also works for undergrad non-majors, per earlier notes) — students can't do the method of joints, since it needs trigonometry they don't have yet. The software was always going to hide that math (same as MASTAN2 did), so this doesn't block the design-tool itself. What it does change is the scaffolding before it: without method-of-joints, students need a different, non-trig path to the qualitative structural intuition (why is this member stretching vs squishing, why does this shape hold its form, where does the load actually go) that lets them read the Element Report and make informed choices rather than clicking randomly. Matt confirmed the existing MASTAN-based version of this activity already works well for this audience (students reliably end up with non-collapsing designs) — the scaffolding redesign below is about making the reasoning more genuine and the tool more polished within that already-working format, not fixing something broken.

Planned tab structure (revised 2026-09-02 with Matt, replaces the earlier 5-tab plan — 2 tabs added specifically to replace what method-of-joints would have taught):
1. Push & Pull — tension vs. compression, qualitatively, no trig. Built, see below.
2. Why Triangles — static determinacy/stability taught interactively (toggle a few candidate members on a small toy structure, see live stable/unstable via whether the stiffness matrix stays non-singular) — this is where balanced-forces/Newton's-first-law reasoning gets introduced in plain language. Not built yet.
3. Load Path — a small fixed truss, student traces how the load travels from application point to supports using the Displacement/Axial Force modes, and sees the reaction forces push back (Newton's third law) — also the natural place to explain the half-load/mirrored-faces convention in context. Not built yet.
4. Design & Predict — the main tool, already built as the standalone `truss-designer.html`; this tab just links out to it for now (see below) rather than embedding it.
5. Iteration Log — today's decision-matrix functionality (already live inside truss-designer.html itself, see "Iteration workflow" below) as its own guided step once this tab shell is more built out.
6. Predict vs. Build — after the physical build, student enters measured deflection, compares to the model's prediction, reflects on the gap (real K'NEX joints aren't ideal pins, and the model is one face at half load, not the full 3D object).
7. Design Argument — claim/evidence/tradeoff/reflection, AI-graded via the same Cloudflare Worker the other units use, checking the argument doesn't lean on a single metric.

`engineering/investigation.html` — the tab shell now exists, with Tab 1 (Push & Pull) fully built and Tabs 2/3/5/6/7 as explicit "not built yet" stubs (not silently missing — each stub says so) and Tab 4 linking out to the existing `truss-designer.html`. Same dark-theme visual language as `truss-designer.html` (this unit's own established look, distinct from anthropology/meteorology's light theme) for consistency within the unit.

Tab 1 (Push & Pull), what it does: three small predict-then-reveal scenarios, each a tiny custom truss (2-4 nodes) solved by a generalized version of the same validated stiffness-method solver (now parameterized — takes nodes/elems/fixed/load as arguments instead of truss-designer.html's module-level globals — a cleaner shape worth backporting there later). Student predicts tension/compression per member (two buttons, "Pulled" / "Pushed"), then all predictions for that scenario trigger a real solve and reveal, color-coded with the exact same red/blue convention `truss-designer.html` uses, so the vocabulary transfers directly. Scenarios, in order: "Two Ropes" (a symmetric hanging sign, both members in tension — intuitive warm-up), "Shelf Bracket" (flat arm in tension, diagonal brace in compression — the classic textbook result, confirms the solver rather than assuming it), "Braced Bay" (one literal unit cell of the real 81-member ground structure, cantilever-loaded — reproduces standard cantilever beam bending, top chord tension/bottom chord compression, directly foreshadowing what students will see throughout Tab 4). Verified via the same independent-JS-mock Playwright pattern used throughout this unit: all three scenarios solve to stable, physically sensible results, wraparound (scenario 3 → back to 1) and tab-switching both work cleanly. Approved by Matt as-is.

Tab 2 (Why Triangles), what it does: a toggleable two-bay X-braced structure (6 nodes, 10 candidate members: bottom/top/vertical/both-diagonals per bay) — student clicks members on/off and gets live stable/unstable feedback. Deliberately redundant, not minimally determinate: exact determinacy only needs 8 members (2n=12 total dof minus 4 restrained at the 2 pins), so 2 of the 10 are genuinely optional — per Matt's direct feedback on the first version (a single exactly-determinate bay, where every member mattered and there was nothing to actually explore). The shared `analyze()` Python function (same one Tab 1 uses) was extended to handle two more cases beyond truss-designer.html's original: an isolated non-load node (reported by name, distinct from the load-isolated case) and, for a genuine mechanism, an actual mechanism-mode vector via SVD on the singular reduced stiffness matrix (`np.linalg.svd(Kff)`, last row of `Vt`) — the direction the shape can flex in without any member changing length. When unstable, the canvas doesn't just show a text warning: it animates the real shape wobbling back and forth along that computed direction (`requestAnimationFrame`, node positions offset by `mode * sin(t) * amplitude`), which is a far more visceral "why triangles" argument than a static message. Verified with a substantially extended JS mock (added a matrix-rank check and an inverse-iteration approximate null-vector solver, both reused from/built on the Gaussian elimination already in earlier mocks) confirming: default (all 10) stable, removing one diagonal stays stable, removing the *other* bay's redundant diagonal too still stays stable (now exactly determinate), removing a third essential member produces the mechanism with real frame-to-frame animation (confirmed by diffing canvas data URLs, not just checking status text), reset restores all 10 and stability, an isolated non-load node produces its own distinct message, and removing both members touching the load node correctly falls into the load-isolated case instead. Screenshots confirm both the full 10-member structure and the reduced-but-still-stable 8-member state render cleanly. Awaiting Matt's review.

What's actually built so far:

`engineering/prototypes/truss_stiffness_validate.py` — the from-scratch 2D direct-stiffness-method truss solver in Python (numpy), validated against the .mat file's own MASTAN2-computed deflection (~1e-14 agreement at every node). Reference oracle for any future changes to the physics.

`engineering/truss-designer.html` — a standalone, working interactive tool (not yet wrapped into a guided investigation.html — same pattern meteorology followed, sandbox first, guided wrapper later). Built after Matt specified the required functionality directly: students remove members and non-support nodes from the full 81-member ground structure to try to cut cost/weight, and can run four analysis modes over whatever subset remains:
- **Node Displacement** — dx/dy readout for the loaded node (bottom-right corner), always shown in the side panel regardless of mode.
- **Axial Force Diagram** — each active member drawn as two parallel offset lines, offset magnitude proportional to |force|, color-coded tension (red) vs. compression (blue) — this replicates MASTAN2's own force-diagram convention on purpose, since students already know it from the software.
- **Element Report** — a table of every active member (# / node i / node j / length / force / tension-compression tag), sorted by force magnitude ascending so near-zero-force members surface at the top as removal candidates.
- **Deflected Shape** — deformed truss overlaid on the undeformed one (dashed), with a student-adjustable exaggeration slider (1x-200x), since real displacements are small relative to the span.

Node/member numbering is preserved exactly from the .mat file (1-based, same order) so it stays cross-referenceable with MASTAN2 for anyone who's used both, and is now drawn directly on the canvas (node # bold near each node, member # along each member, both toggleable via a "Show member/node numbers" checkbox, default on) — added after Matt tested the first version and pointed out there was no way to identify which element was which. The 4 pinned support nodes and the loaded node are locked from removal (agreed with Matt); members touching them are removable like any other member. Unstable configurations are handled explicitly rather than crashing: a "mechanism" (singular stiffness matrix) and a "load node isolated" (all members touching node 25 removed) are detected and reported with distinct, specific messages.

Iteration workflow (added after Matt's first live-browser test): a "Run Simulation" button logs the current design as a numbered iteration (member/rod counts, total length, loaded-node deflection, and a combined score) and compares its score against the previously logged iteration, showing a clear better/worse banner — on worse, a "Revert to Iteration N" button restores that design. The full ground structure is auto-logged as Iteration 1 on load. An Iteration Log table keeps every run (click any row to restore that design), with adjustable weight inputs (deflection / cost-proxy / mass-proxy) that re-rank the whole table live — this is deliberately the same "no single best design, only best-for-a-weighting" lesson Tab 3 was designed around, now visible even before Tab 3 exists as its own guided step. Cost and mass in the score are still proxies (rod count and total rod length respectively) since real price/weight-per-rod numbers don't exist yet — labeled as such in the UI, not presented as real dollars/grams.

The Element Report's default sort was changed from ascending-by-force-magnitude to ascending-by-member-number, per direct feedback that surfacing near-zero-force members automatically "makes it too easy" — defeats the point of having students read and interpret the force column themselves rather than have the software hand them the answer.

One real bug caught by actually looking at a screenshot, not just checking the DOM: member-number labels for the two diagonals of an X-brace share the same geometric midpoint, so they rendered fully overlapping/illegible in the first labeling pass. Fixed by offsetting each label along that member's own perpendicular (the same trick already used for the axial-force offset-line rendering) rather than a fixed screen-space offset — separates the two labels since crossing diagonals have different perpendicular directions. Still tight in some cells at the default canvas size; worth another pass (bigger canvas, or hover tooltips instead of always-on labels) if it's still hard to read in practice.

Verification: this sandbox's dev environment can't reach cdn.jsdelivr.net (same network restriction that blocked testing the meteorology unit's AI feedback path against the live worker), so the real Pyodide/numpy path hasn't been driven in an actual browser yet — that still needs a real-browser check before trusting it in front of students. What *was* verified here: a second, fully independent JS reimplementation of the same stiffness method (used to mock Pyodide for Playwright testing) reproduces the MASTAN2 reference to displayed precision — the same value the Python version matched to 1e-14 — so the physics has now been cross-checked three ways (MASTAN2 itself, the Python port, an independent JS port). Playwright testing against that mock also confirmed: member/node click-to-remove works, all 4 analysis modes render without JS errors, the element report sorts correctly, and both instability cases (mechanism, isolated load node) are detected and produce the right message rather than crashing or showing garbage.

Rod-length mapping (resolved 2026-09-02, corrected same day): earlier read of the Excel's rod catalog as inches was wrong — it's centimeters. Purple is 9.5cm/0.9g, Blue is 13.5cm/1.3g (`Decision_Criteria_Cantilever_FINAL.xlsx`, "Ground Structure" sheet, rows 16-17) — converted to mm (95mm, 135mm) these are almost exactly the mini K'NEX numbers Matt gave verbally (94mm, 138mm), so the spreadsheet and Matt's direct statement corroborate each other once the unit is right. Purple pairs with the ground structure's horizontal/vertical members (106.36mm real length, ~11.6% off), blue pairs with the diagonals (150.43mm real length, ~8.3% off) — cross-pairing is 3-6x worse on both. There is no separate per-rod dollar-price column anywhere in the workbook; the workbook's own Summary sheet computes "Cost of members" as literally equal to "Length of members" (`F8='=E8'`), so `truss-designer.html` ports that exact formula (cost = total rod length in cm) rather than inventing a different one. Mass is a real number (grams) from the Mass Each column. The Iteration Log's Cost/Mass columns and the score weights now use these real values, not the earlier rod-count/length proxies.

Deflection is now displayed in cm everywhere in the UI (status bar, side panel, Iteration Log), converted from the solver's native inches at display time only — the physics itself still runs in inches internally, matching the validated MASTAN2 reference exactly; only the presentation changed.

One bug worth remembering the shape of: after changing the iteration-log weight *inputs'* HTML defaults, the JS `weights` object still had its own separately-hardcoded defaults, so the score silently used stale numbers until a student touched a weight field. Fixed by having the JS read its initial values from the input elements themselves rather than duplicating the defaults in two places — a good general lesson for this file (and probably others): don't let a default exist in two places that can drift apart.

Connector mass and cost (resolved 2026-09-02): mini K'NEX connectors are 0.81g and $10 each, one uniform type — unlike the old standard-K'NEX Excel sheet, which had five connector colors at different masses with a degree-dependent doubling rule, Matt gave one mass and one price for all connectors, meaning there's no degree-to-type mapping to derive for this kit — it's just a count. `truss-designer.html` now adds one connector per node that still has an active member touching it (a node fully cut off by removing all its members doesn't need one), added into both Mass and Cost. Total Cost = rod length (cm) + connector count × $10 — mixing a length-based proxy with a real dollar figure into one number isn't dimensionally clean, but it's exactly what Matt's own spreadsheet does (member cost = raw length, node cost = count × price, both summed into "Total Construction Cost"), so it's ported as-is rather than invented differently. The UI shows rod cost, connector cost, and the combined total separately so the blend isn't hidden.

Testing cost (clarified 2026-09-02, not literally wired into the score): Matt's actual point with the Excel's $1-Mastan vs $1000-physical split is pedagogical, not a number to fold into the per-design score — it's there to teach that engineers model before they build physical prototypes, because guess-and-check with real material is slow and expensive. `truss-designer.html` now has a "Modeling vs. Physical Testing" panel that counts every "Run Simulation" click as a $1 virtual test (running session total, not per-design) and states the $1000 physical-build reference cost for contrast. Deliberately kept separate from the per-iteration Cost/Mass/Score columns — testing cost is a property of the student's process (how many times they've iterated), not of any one truss design, so folding it into the ranking score would make an unrelated later design look artificially worse. This is a bigger digression from the source spreadsheet than most of this unit's other choices (the spreadsheet's own Total Construction Cost formula does fold testing cost into the same number used for ranking) — flagging it here in case Matt wants it combined after seeing it in practice.

Known unresolved items (don't guess, ask Matt): none currently open for the rod/connector BOM — rod length/mass/cost and connector mass/cost are all real numbers now (see "Rod-length mapping" and "Connector mass and cost" above).

Instructor-locked weighting (added 2026-09-02, per Matt directly): the deflection/cost/mass weights are an instructor decision made when assigning the tool, not something students should be adjusting live — Matt was explicit about this. The weight inputs now live inside a closed-by-default `<details>` disclosure labeled "Instructor: set weights for this assignment"; the main view shows the active weights as read-only text. A "Generate link for students" button builds a URL with `?wDefl=&wCost=&wMass=` baked in — opening a link that carries all three params locks the weights to those values AND hides the entire instructor disclosure (not just collapses it), so a student following that link has no UI path to change them. Opening the page with no params (or a partial set) leaves it in the open/default state, editable via the disclosure — that's the right behavior for Matt's own exploration or a general visitor, not for a distributed assignment link.

Splash page (added 2026-09-02, per Matt directly): `index.html` now links to `engineering/truss-designer.html` as a third unit card, badged "In Progress" rather than "Live" like anthropology/meteorology — deliberately different, since this is a single sandbox tool, not yet the full guided investigation.html, and hasn't been used with real students. Worth noting this also isn't quite "the same way" as the other two cards, which Matt asked for: both of those route through a landing/guided page (student-landing.html, investigation.html), never straight to a bare research-sandbox-equivalent tool — meteorology's own energy-balance-sandbox.html is deliberately NOT linked from the splash page for that reason. truss-designer.html is currently this unit's only page, so linking it directly was the only option; revisit once investigation.html exists, at which point the splash card should probably point there instead, matching the established pattern. This change is only live on this branch — the real site builds from `main`, so it won't appear on mmj125.github.io until merged.

Tab 3 (Load Path), what it does: the fully-triangulated end state of Tab 2's structure (single diagonal per bay, 8 members), fixed — not toggleable, this tab is about reading a result, not editing one. Shows the axial force diagram (same offset-line, red/blue convention as everywhere else in this unit) plus new arrows at the two pins sized and directed by the actual computed reaction force, with a text readout of both pins' reaction components. Needed a real engine addition, not just a UI change: neither analyze() nor truss-designer.html previously computed reactions at all, only member forces and displacements. Added it as `K @ u` read off at the fixed dofs — the full nodal-force vector consistent with the computed displacement field, which by construction equals the reaction at any fixed dof. Verified two ways: the reaction components sum to (0, 1), exactly canceling the (0, -1) applied load to floating-point precision — real static equilibrium in the actual computed numbers, not just asserted in the copy — and a screenshot confirms the arrows point in directions that match their component signs (e.g. a pure-leftward reaction renders as a horizontal arrow). Closes with a line tying this back to the half-load/mirrored-faces convention already stated in truss-designer.html's header. Awaiting Matt's review.

Tab 5 (Iteration Log), what it does: NOT a link back to truss-designer.html's already-built Iteration Log (that would just send students to the same page Tab 4 already links to) — instead, four fixed example designs with real-scale numbers (deflection/cost/mass), and three sliders for the same weights the real tool uses. Dragging a slider re-ranks the table live and highlights the winner. Deliberately chosen so three different sliders produce three different winners: default weights favor "Trimmed", maxing out the deflection weight favors "Full Ground Structure" (stiffest), maxing out cost or mass weight favors "Overtrimmed" (lightest/cheapest) — verified this by hand calculation first, then confirmed the browser reproduces the exact same scores and winners for all three weight extremes. This lets a student build the "no single best design" intuition before they've necessarily used Tab 4 for real, and doesn't require the solver at all (pure arithmetic over fixed numbers, no Pyodide dependency for this tab).

Tab 6 (Predict vs. Build), what it does: two number inputs (predicted deflection from the tool, measured deflection from the physical build), live percent-difference readout, and a reflection textarea with three suggested (non-exhaustive, not multiple-choice) explanations for the gap — real K'NEX joint play, the 2D/half-load model simplification, or measurement error. No solver dependency, no AI grading here (that's Tab 7); this is just the numeric comparison plus a prompt to think about it. Verified the percent-difference math against hand calculation.

Tab 7 (Design Argument), what it does: the same claim/evidence/tradeoffs/reflection four-field pattern as the other units, wired to the same deployed Cloudflare Worker (`FEEDBACK_ENDPOINT`, `cloudflare-worker/worker.js` — no worker changes needed, it's already fully generic) rather than a new endpoint. The system prompt is specific to this unit's epistemics: explicitly told there's no single correct design, so it should push back on arguments that lean on one metric alone, don't cite the student's own actual numbers, or treat one iteration as proof rather than one data point among several — and it's told to check that a predicted-vs-measured deflection explanation (if given) is a plausible physical reason, not hand-waved. Verified end-to-end with a mocked `fetch` (this sandbox can't reach the deployed worker, same limitation as every other unit's AI-feedback path) covering three cases: empty fields correctly block the request with an alert rather than calling fetch, a mocked successful response displays correctly and re-enables the button, and a mocked network failure falls back to the same graceful "try again" message the other units use rather than showing a raw error. The actual Gemini output quality is unverified, same caveat as everywhere else this pattern is used — needs a real-browser check before trusting it in front of students.

This completes the originally-planned 7-tab structure — every tab now has real content, no stubs left. Tab 5 and Tab 6 don't depend on the solver at all; only Tabs 1-4 (and Tab 5's underlying concept, if the student later applies it in Tab 4) touch Pyodide.

Truss bridge activity (new, in progress, separate from the cantilever above — same engineering unit, same K'NEX kit, different classroom activity)

What it is: a second, separate design activity within the same engineering unit. Students design and build a truss BRIDGE (simply supported, not cantilevered) with mini K'NEX, to minimize cost and mass while holding a 1kg mass at the center of a 1.3m (130cm) span. Unlike the cantilever, there is no real MASTAN file or existing decision-criteria spreadsheet for this activity — everything below came directly from Matt in conversation, including one real back-and-forth worth remembering the shape of (see "Connector confusion" below).

Full rod catalog (all 7 colors matter here, unlike the cantilever which only ever used purple/blue): Black 1.5cm/0.1g, Yellow 2.5cm/0.3g, Gray 4cm/0.5g, Red 6.5cm/0.7g, Purple 9.5cm/0.9g, Blue 13.5cm/1.3g, Orange 20cm/1.9g.

Connector confusion (resolved, worth remembering the shape of): Matt's first pass at giving me connector data listed 5 colors with masses (black 0.9g, yellow 0.5g, gray 1.7g, red 0.8g, blue 1.3g) but flagged gray's 1.7g as "a mistake from an earlier reference." His next message, meant to give the corrected rule, instead described a *different* connector system entirely — a 6-color degree/angle table (orange, purple, yellow, gray, red, green, plus a separate "3D exception" pair of gray/blue) that didn't overlap cleanly with the first list at all (missing black, introduced orange/purple/green with no mass anywhere). Rather than guess which list was real, this was flagged back to Matt directly — the fix: only two connectors are actually used in this activity, both 3D: Gray (4-way, 1.7g — confirmed correct) and Blue (7-way, 1.3g). The rest of that degree/angle table was a different, broader K'NEX reference that doesn't apply here. Lesson: when two of Matt's own messages about the same data contradict each other, that's a signal to stop and ask, not to average or guess between them — same category of issue as the earlier inches-vs-cm and standard-vs-mini-K'NEX mix-ups on the cantilever side.

Connector-type-selection rule (my inference, not yet confirmed by Matt — flag before relying on it): since gray and blue are described only by max capacity (4-way vs 7-way), a degree-based rule is proposed — gray for any joint with ≤4 members meeting, blue for 5–7. This is a placeholder default like the cantilever's early connector simplifications were; correct it if Matt gives an actual rule.

Six named triangle-module scales, each a (leg rod, hypotenuse rod) pair a student can build with: Small (black leg/yellow hyp), Medium (yellow/gray), Large (gray/red), X-large (red/purple), XXL (purple/blue), Huge (blue/orange). Checked the leg×√2≈hypotenuse relationship for each: X-large (+3.3%), XXL (+0.5%), and Huge (+4.8%) are all close to a true right angle; Small/Medium/Large are 13-18% off, consistent with small bracing pieces that don't need to be geometrically precise the way a load-bearing span does. X-large divides the 130cm span into exactly 20 bays (20×6.5cm=130cm, not approximately — exactly), which is almost certainly the intended primary span scale, though this is inference from the numbers, not something Matt stated outright.

Two real pedagogical failures Matt wants students to discover in the model instead of through physical build-fail-rebuild cycles: (1) smaller module scales need far more members to span the same distance and get expensive fast (Small needs ~87 bays for 130cm vs. X-large's 20 — the tool needs to let students actually try a small scale and feel that cost, not just default to X-large), and (2) a single layer (2 rows of nodes) is never stiff enough regardless of bracing, and needs real added depth (3+ rows) to work. Point (2) is now numerically confirmed with the real validated solver, not just assumed true: a fully-braced 2-row (1-layer) truss at X-large scale deflects 12.32cm under the load — the *stiffest that depth can ever be*, since every candidate member is already present — while a 3-row (2-layer) truss deflects only 2.87cm, a 4.3x improvement from one added row. Both configurations checked against real static equilibrium (reactions split evenly at 4.905N each, exactly half the 9.81N load, as symmetry requires, summing to cancel it exactly to the sixth decimal place).

Architecture decision (confirmed correct, not just simpler): a parametric ground-structure generator — student picks a module scale and a depth (number of rows), which generates a candidate grid, then the same proven toggle-member interaction, 4 analysis modes, and BOM/cost/mass/iteration-log pattern from the cantilever tool apply on top. This was initially an open question (a much bigger free-form node/member placement tool was the alternative) but Matt's direct fact that real mini K'NEX connectors only allow 45°/90°/135°/180° angles settled it — a square grid with diagonals produces exactly those angles and no others, so the ground-structure approach isn't just easier, it's the physically correct one; true free-form placement could produce angles the real kit can't build. Per-cell candidate members confirmed directly by Matt: horizontal/vertical (leg length) plus BOTH diagonals (hypotenuse length, independently toggleable X-brace) — the same redundancy pattern already built and proven in Tab 2 ("four purples and two blue cross braces... stronger but more expensive... than one cross brace").

What's actually built and validated so far: `engineering/prototypes/bridge_ground_structure_validate.py` — a generic `generate_ground_structure(cols, rows, spacing)` function (horizontal + vertical + both diagonals per cell), cross-checked against the real, MASTAN2-validated cantilever structure rather than trusted on faith: parametrized to match the cantilever's regular 6x4 grid, it reproduces the exact same 68 members and exact same node coordinates as the real file. `engineering/prototypes/bridge_solve_validate.py` — the first real bridge configuration (X-large scale, 20 bays, pin+roller supports, 1kg at midspan), producing the depth-comparison numbers above. Material properties (E=21235.86 N/cm², A=0.08284cm², converted from the cantilever's real MASTAN E=30800psi/A=0.01284in² into consistent metric units since the bridge's own span/mass/rod data are all metric) are carried over from the cantilever on the assumption it's the same physical K'NEX plastic — reasonable, but not explicitly confirmed by Matt the way every other number in this section was, so flag it if the bridge tool's numbers ever need to be cross-checked against a real physical test. Supports (pin at one end, roller at the other) are the standard, statically-determinate choice for a simply-supported span — an engineering default, not something Matt specified, but not really a judgment call either.

`engineering/bridge-designer.html` — first working interactive piece, scoped deliberately narrow (core grid + physics only, per Matt directly) before layering in the rest. Fixed at X-large scale, 20 bays, 3 rows (2-layer) — the full 182-member ground structure, same click-to-toggle interaction and 4 analysis modes (Node Displacement, Axial Force Diagram, Element Report, Deflected Shape) as `truss-designer.html`, generalized to pin+roller supports (a roller only restrains vertical displacement, unlike the cantilever's all-pin supports — needed a small change to the fixed-dof logic, not just reused as-is). Added zoom (mouse wheel) and pan (click-drag) over the base view transform, since the bridge is far too large to work with at a fixed scale — a drag-vs-click guard (`canvas._didDrag`) stops a pan gesture from also being read as a member click. Reference span lines mark the required 130cm at the pin and roller x-positions, to scale, with a labeled bracket underneath.

Verified two ways: the baseline (full ground structure, nothing removed) deflects -2.8671cm at midspan, matching `bridge_solve_validate.py`'s already-computed number to the full displayed precision — a real cross-check that the browser port of the physics is correct, same discipline as the cantilever's original MASTAN2 match. Playwright confirmed click-to-toggle, both instability messages (mechanism, load-isolated), locked nodes (pin/roller/load can't be removed), zoom in/out/reset, and that dragging to pan does not also toggle a member.

Fixed a real bug caught by Matt from a screenshot, not by me: the load arrow at midspan was drawn pointing up and away from the node instead of down into it — a copy-paste inversion when adapting `truss-designer.html`'s arrow-drawing convention (which correctly extends below the node). One-line sign flip, verified visually after.

Scale/depth selector added (the next round after the core grid): student picks a module scale (Small through Huge) and a layer count (1-5), then "Generate Ground Structure" rebuilds the grid at that configuration. Bay count is `round(130 / legLength)`, so most scales don't divide the 130cm span evenly — the tool says so explicitly (e.g. "33 bays × 4cm legs = 132.0cm span, 2.0cm over") rather than silently fudging the span to hit 130 exactly. This required generalizing the Python `analyze()` function to take nodes/elems/pin/roller/load as arguments instead of baking them in as globals at Pyodide-init time (the same shape already used in `investigation.html`'s Tabs 1-3), since the whole point is that the geometry now changes at runtime.

Verified: the default configuration (X-large, 2 layers) still solves to the exact same -2.8671cm already cross-checked against the Python reference — confirms the refactor to parameterized analyze() didn't change the physics, only how it's called. Also re-confirmed the depth lesson at a *second* scale, not just X-large: Large scale (gray/red) deflects -7.67cm at 2 layers vs. -33.58cm at 1 layer, a 4.4x difference — the same "one layer is never enough" pattern holds regardless of which scale a student picks, which is exactly what needs to be true for the lesson to generalize. Stress-tested Small scale (87 bays, 264 nodes, 785 candidate members) — regenerates and solves in about a second, no performance concern even at the far end of the scale range. `autoFitView()` rescales zoom on every regenerate so a much bigger or taller configuration is immediately visible without manual zooming first.

Real cost/mass and the cross-configuration iteration log are now built (continuing directly from the scale/depth selector above, per Matt's "continue to cost/mass, and tie that to the iteration log"). `generateGroundStructure` now tags every candidate member with its rod type (`'leg'` for horizontal/vertical, `'hyp'` for diagonal) at generation time, since the BOM needs to know unambiguously which of the current scale's two rod colors applies to each active member — this was the one prerequisite change needed before the BOM math could work. A `ROD_CATALOG` (all 7 colors, length+mass) and `CONNECTOR` (gray 1.7g/≤4-way, blue 1.3g/5-7-way) table back a `computeBOM()` function: counts active leg/hyp rods by the current scale's colors, computes each node's degree from the active member set to classify its connector (skipping removed nodes and nodes with degree 0, which need no connector at all), and totals mass (rods + connectors) and cost (total rod length in cm — the same length-as-cost convention ported from the cantilever's real spreadsheet, since no per-rod dollar price exists for this catalog either, stated as such in the UI). The connector degree-to-type rule stays flagged in the UI as an inference, not a rule Matt's explicitly confirmed.

A genuine physical-constructibility finding came out of building this: the full ground structure at the *default* configuration (X-large, 2 layers = 3 rows) already has 19 nodes needing more than 7 connections at once, exceeding blue's stated 7-way max capacity — not a rare edge case, the very first thing a student sees. A `capacity-warning` panel now surfaces this by name (listing the actual over-capacity node numbers) whenever it applies, rather than silently producing a BOM for a design that can't really be built — confirmed this isn't a bug by checking a 1-layer (2-row) configuration, which has no over-capacity nodes at all, so the warning correctly tracks depth/bracing density rather than firing unconditionally.

Iteration log: mirrors the cantilever tool's pattern (instructor-locked weight disclosure via a closed-by-default `<details>`, live weight-display text, a "Generate link for students" button that bakes `?wDefl=&wCost=&wMass=` into a URL and hides the whole disclosure when a student opens a link carrying all three params) with one real difference — each logged iteration snapshots not just the active-member/removed-node selection but also the scale key and layer count, since a student can regenerate an entirely different grid between runs, not just trim one fixed grid the way the cantilever tool's single grid worked. Clicking a history row calls `regenerate()` for that snapshot's scale/layers first, then overrides the resulting full-grid `activeMembers`/`removedNodes` with the snapshot's actual saved sets. Score is `weights.defl*|deflection| + weights.cost*cost + weights.mass*mass`; the table re-sorts live on every weight-input change; a "Run Simulation" click compares the new entry's score against the previously *logged* iteration (not the best-so-far) and offers a one-click revert when it's worse. The full ground structure at the default configuration auto-logs as Iteration 1 on page load, same as the cantilever tool. Weight inputs are read from the HTML elements themselves at startup rather than hardcoded a second time in JS — a deliberate callback to the cantilever tool's own stale-default bug (documented in this file's cantilever section), avoided here by construction rather than by remembering not to repeat it.

Verified via Playwright with the same independent-JS-mock-of-Pyodide pattern used throughout this unit (this sandbox still can't reach the CDN for a real Pyodide/numpy check): BOM leg/hyp/connector counts and total mass hand-checked against the default X-large configuration's actual rod colors (red legs at 0.7g, purple diagonals at 0.9g); the capacity warning confirmed present at the default 3-row config and absent at 1 layer; the auto-logged Iteration 1 confirmed present on load; removing a member via direct state manipulation (canvas-click hit-testing proved too coordinate-fragile for a scale-independent test) then running a second iteration produced a lower, "better than Iteration 1" score with the correct banner; maxing the cost weight live re-sorted the history table; clicking a history row restored its exact scale dropdown value; URL params `?wDefl=&wCost=&wMass=` correctly hid the instructor disclosure and applied the locked values; and "Generate link" produced a URL carrying the current weights. The actual Gemini-free numeric/UI logic is what's verified here — no AI feedback path exists in this tool, unlike Tab 7 of `investigation.html`.

Real bug caught by Matt in a live browser (not by this session's testing, since the JS-mock-of-Pyodide pattern used throughout this unit never actually exercises real Python tuple-unpacking and so couldn't have caught this): the page hung forever on "Loading solver…" with an uncaught `PythonError: ValueError: too many values to unpack (expected 2)` in the browser console. Root cause: the JS-side `generateGroundStructure` was changed (for the BOM work above) to tag every member with a 3rd rod-type element (`[i, j, 'leg'|'hyp']`), but the embedded Python `analyze()` function still did `i, j = elems[idx]`, which unpacks fine for a 2-tuple and throws for a 3-tuple. Two call sites had this (main stiffness-assembly loop and the post-solve force-recovery loop); both fixed to `i, j = elems[idx][0], elems[idx][1]`. Worth remembering the shape of: this session's whole verification approach for this unit (an independent JS reimplementation of the solver, used to mock Pyodide for Playwright) is blind to bugs inside the actual embedded Python string itself, since the mock never runs it — a change to the JS-side data shape that both the mock and the real Python consume needs the real Python path checked by hand, not just by Playwright-against-mock passing.

Node/member numbering added (per Matt directly, after using the tool live): the Element Report table and the capacity-warning message both referenced node/member numbers that were nowhere visible on the canvas, making them useless for actually finding the flagged node. Ported `truss-designer.html`'s exact numbering approach: node # in bold near each node, member # offset along that member's own perpendicular (so two diagonals of an X-brace, which share a midpoint, don't draw on top of each other), both toggleable via a "Show member/node numbers" checkbox, default on. At this tool's default configuration (69 nodes, 200 members) the numbers are dense enough at the full zoomed-out view that reading a specific one requires zooming in on that region first — confirmed via screenshot at both zoom levels; the existing scroll-to-zoom/drag-to-pan already supports this, no new UI needed for it.

Abutment overlap (added same day, per Matt directly): a real bridge needs to physically rest on/overlap each abutment, not just touch it at the single point where the required clear span ends — so the truss now extends one extra bay (`OVERLAP_BAYS = 1`, not yet student-adjustable) past each support onto the abutment. The pin and roller supports moved from the truss's own end nodes to one bay in from each end; the 130cm reference span line still marks the distance between the two supports (the actual clear span requirement), not the truss's full physical length, which is now `130cm + 2 overlap bays`. Midspan (the load node) is recomputed as the midpoint between the two supports, not the midpoint of the whole truss including overhangs. Verified two ways: a standalone Python re-run of the already-validated `bridge_solve_validate.py` generator/solver with the overlap bays added reproduces the exact same -2.8655cm the browser tool shows (up slightly from -2.8671cm without overlap — expected, since the ground structure is redundant/indeterminate, so the extra overhang bracing does slightly change the load path, not a bug), and reactions still split evenly at 4.905N each and sum to exactly cancel the 9.81N applied load. Matt separately confirmed the connector over-capacity message (rather than blocking construction outright) is the right call here — "they'll find that out anyway if they try to build" — so the capacity-warning behavior from the previous round stays as-is, including surfacing it on the very first thing a student sees at the default configuration.

Real timing bug caught by Matt (not by testing here, since this sandbox can't run real Pyodide against the real CDN): clicking "Generate Ground Structure" updated the span-note text immediately (synchronous) but didn't redraw the canvas until the async solve finished — for a slow solve (a big grid, or just real network/Pyodide latency), the picture kept showing the *previous* configuration while the text already described the new one. The initial page load never had this problem (it already called `draw(null)` right after the first `regenerate()`); the regenerate button just didn't mirror that. Fixed by calling `draw(null)` immediately after `regenerate()` in the button handler too, plus an explicit "Solving…" status in between so it's clear a computation is in flight rather than looking frozen. Verified by artificially delaying the mocked solve by 1.5s and confirming the picture already shows the new node/bay/layer count well before the solve resolves.

Resolved: Matt's "10 bays on the third row" question turned out to need no new architecture at all — he hadn't realized the existing click-to-remove interaction (same ground-structure method as the cantilever, present since the very first round of this tool) already covers it. Generate the full grid, then click off that row's members over the bays you don't want; click them back on if the trim proves unstable. If Matt himself missed this, students would too, so the discoverability of remove/restore was worth fixing regardless: the header copy now states the workflow explicitly (including this exact "shorter row" case), and a prominent highlighted callout ("Click any member or node below to remove it") sits directly above the canvas instead of the small muted note that used to live below it, easy to miss.

Also fixed while in there: the zoom-control buttons shared one `width:32px` CSS rule meant for the small +/- icon buttons, which squeezed "Reset view"'s text onto two lines. Split the rule so only the +/- buttons get the fixed square size.

Connector model simplified to gray-only, capacity warning removed (per Matt directly, 2026-09-04): the blue connector, the degree-based gray/blue classification, and the whole over-capacity-node warning panel are gone. Matt's reasoning: assume everyone ends up using gray connectors anyway, and simplify the model rather than keep tracking a distinction that mostly wasn't earning its complexity. `computeBOM()` now counts one connector per node with at least one active member touching it, full stop — no degree check, no warning text, no `capacity-warning` DOM element at all (removed, not just hidden). The Bill of Materials panel shows a single "Connectors (gray)" line.

Cost model changed to real dollars, applied to BOTH tools (per Matt directly, 2026-09-04): cost is now explicitly $1 per cm of rod plus $10 per connector, stated as real dollar prices rather than the earlier "length as a cost proxy" framing. Numerically this was already the cantilever's formula (rod cost was already `= total length in cm`, connector cost was already `count × $10`) — what changed there is just the labeling (added explicit `ROD_COST_PER_CM = 1` constant, `$` prefixes on the displayed values, removed the "not dimensionally clean" / "not a real dollar figure" hedging in the UI notes). For the bridge tool this was a real formula change: it previously had no connector cost at all (cost was rod length only) — now it's `$1/cm × rod length + $10 × connector count`, matching the cantilever exactly. All "Matt's own spreadsheet" mentions were removed from both tools' visible UI text per his direct request (the underlying provenance — `Decision_Criteria_Cantilever_FINAL.xlsx` — is still noted in code comments as engineering history, just not surfaced to students as attribution).

Comparison figure: three "ideal" cantilever designs (2026-09-04, one-off demonstration deliverable, not built into either tool): Matt asked to see the cantilever's ground structure optimized under three weighting extremes (deflection-heavy, mass-heavy, cost-heavy) as a static figure. Built as a standalone Python script (not committed to the repo — a scratch deliverable, sent directly as a PNG) using the exact same 28-node/81-member ground structure, the validated stiffness solver, and the real BOM (post cost-model-change) as `truss-designer.html`. Optimization is a greedy local search: from the full 81-member structure, repeatedly test every single-member removal AND every remaining non-locked node removal (which drops all of that node's active members and its connector at once), keep whichever single move improves the weighted score most, stop when nothing improves it — explicitly a demonstration heuristic, not a claim of global optimality. Genuine finding, not a bug: mass-heavy and cost-heavy weighting converge on the *identical* 36-member design. Checked why: rod cost (\$1/cm) and rod mass (g) are nearly perfectly proportional across both rod colors (about \$10.4-10.6 per gram of rod either color, vs about \$12.35 per gram for a connector) — with this specific catalog and pricing, minimizing cost and minimizing mass are nearly the same objective, and only deflection-heavy weighting pulls toward a genuinely different (stiffer, heavier, 75-member) design. This mirrors what the fixed-example numbers in `investigation.html`'s own Tab 5 already show (maxing cost OR mass both favor "Overtrimmed" there) — not a new phenomenon, now confirmed with the real solver and real BOM instead of hand-picked example numbers.

Third scoring criterion changed from mass to force concentration, in both tools plus the cantilever's guided Tab 5 (2026-09-04, per Matt directly): prompted by the comparison-figure finding above (mass and cost are nearly the same objective with this BOM), Matt asked whether a genuine third criterion exists. First proposal from this session (raise connector price so minimizing connectors tracks cost while minimizing members+connectors tracks mass) was tested computationally before being recommended and found not to work: swept connector price from $10 to $5000 in the greedy optimizer and the "cheapest" and "lightest" designs stayed byte-for-byte identical at every price point. Root cause is structural, not a tuning problem: in this fixed grid a member only ever connects adjacent nodes, so removing a joint always means removing all its members too, and that combined move is essentially always worth it regardless of the relative price of rods vs. connectors — joint count and material quantity aren't actually separable axes here. The idea that *did* work: force concentration, the largest force carried by any single active member. It's computed for free from the solver's existing per-member force output (no failure-load data needed, sidestepping Matt's own valid concern that part-to-part fit variation makes modeling real failure loads impractical), and it ties directly to the real failure mechanism Matt described (a member popping out of a connector under compression, or a connector separating under tension, both happen where force peaks, not wherever material is thinnest). Verified computationally that it produces genuinely different designs from pure mass/cost minimization before building it in. Renamed "load concentration" to "force concentration" per Matt directly ("to prevent giggles in the classroom").

Implementation, both `truss-designer.html` and `bridge-designer.html`: `computeMetrics()`/`computeBOM()` now also return the peak absolute member force and which member carries it; the iteration-log score is `weights.defl*|deflection| + weights.cost*cost + weights.force*peakForce`, replacing the old mass term. Mass is NOT removed from the UI — the Bill of Materials panel still shows it for build reference (how heavy the physical structure will be) — it's just no longer one of the three weighted/scored criteria, and a note now says so explicitly. A new "Force concentration (peak member force)" + "Highest-loaded member" stat pair sits next to the displacement readout, with a note telling students to check that exact member first after a physical break — directly supporting the workflow Matt described wanting instructors to use (physical test breaks something → open the model → check whether the break location matches the model's own highest-force member). All `w-mass`/`wMass` ids and URL params renamed to `w-force`/`wForce` throughout (weight inputs, display spans, history table columns, instructor-lock URL scheme, generate-link button) — no `mass` scoring path left in either tool.

Real bug caught during verification (a test-harness bug, not a product bug, but worth remembering the shape of): the Playwright mock's regex for parsing `E` and `A` out of the embedded Python init string (`/E\s*=\s*([\d.]+)/`) matched the *first* substring in the whole string that looked like `E = <number>` — and in the cantilever's init code, the line `LOAD_NODE = 24` contains exactly that pattern (the "E" at the end of "NODE"), so the mock silently set its stiffness constant to 24 instead of 30800. Displacement came out ~1283x too large while member forces stayed correct (forces in a truss with one uniform E across all members don't depend on E's absolute value, only displacements do) — that mismatch (wrong displacement, right force) was the tell that led to the actual root cause rather than the real solver. Fixed by anchoring the regex to line start (`/^E\s*=\s*([\d.]+)/m`). This class of bug is specific to this session's test mocks, not to the shipped product — the real embedded Python does plain unambiguous variable assignment — but it's a reminder that a regex scanning a whole multi-line code string for a short pattern like a single capital letter needs anchoring, or it can match inside an unrelated longer identifier.

`investigation.html`'s Tab 5 (Iteration Log) fixed example table was updated to match: "Mass (g)" column and slider replaced with "Force conc. (lbf)", and — since this table was being touched anyway — all four example designs were replaced with real, solver-verified numbers instead of the old hand-picked placeholders (the old "Full Ground Structure" row's cost, 913.5, was actually missing connector cost entirely — a pre-existing bug now fixed to the correct $1193.5). The deflection and cost sliders' `max` attributes had to be raised (150→4000 for deflection, 2→10 for cost) since the old ranges were calibrated for the old placeholder numbers and couldn't reach the thresholds needed to flip the winner with the new real ones — computed the exact thresholds (defl weight ≈2520, cost weight ≈6.36) before setting the new ranges rather than guessing. Tab 7's AI-feedback system prompt updated to say "force concentration" instead of "mass" throughout.

First pass at the four example designs (Full Ground Structure / Trimmed / Lean / Overtrimmed, all just greedy subsets at decreasing deflection-weight strength) had a real gap Matt caught: maxing the deflection slider and maxing the force-concentration slider converged on the same winner (Full Ground Structure), leaving only 3 distinct winners across 4 sliders. Investigated whether that was fixable or an inherent property of this ground structure by searching much harder for a genuinely force-minimizing (not just deflection-minimizing) design: 30 random-restart greedy searches explicitly minimizing peak member force (not deflection) turned up a real one — 38 members, deflection 1.06cm, cost $645, peak force 1.89lbf, found via `random.seed(23)` in a randomized variant of the same greedy optimizer, tie-breaking randomly among near-equally-good moves instead of always taking the single best one (that randomization is exactly what let it escape the same local optimum the deterministic greedy search kept landing in). This design is strictly better than the full 81-member structure on peak force (1.89 vs 2.07 lbf) *and* far cheaper (38 members vs 81), at the cost of noticeably higher deflection (1.06cm vs 0.64cm) — real proof that "spread the load out" is a genuinely different design strategy from "keep more material," not just a side effect of stiffness. Replaced the "Lean" row with this design, named "Load-Balanced" — now all four sliders (default/balanced, max deflection, max cost, max force concentration) produce four distinct winners (Trimmed / Full Ground Structure / Overtrimmed / Load-Balanced respectively), verified via Playwright at each weight extreme.

Force unit changed from lbf to N in the cantilever tool, matching the bridge tool and the rest of the metric UI (per Matt directly, 2026-09-04): the solver still runs internally in lbf/inches (preserves the real MASTAN legacy numbers for cross-checkability, per the original "preserve legacy numbers exactly" decision), converted to N only at display and score time — the same treatment deflection already got for inches→cm. Added `LBF_TO_N = 4.44822` to `truss-designer.html`; every place a raw force number reached the page (the peak-force stat, the Element Report's Force column, the Iteration Log's history column and score calculation) now multiplies by it before display. Weight defaults and thresholds were rescaled to match: since score = weight × force, and force values got ~4.45x bigger switching from lbf to N, the force weight had to get ~4.45x smaller to preserve the same score balance (default 25 → 5.6). `investigation.html`'s Tab 5 mirrors this exactly (force column and weight slider both converted the same way, default weight 25→5.6, slider max 500→150) — verified via Playwright that all four weight regimes (default/max-deflection/max-cost/max-force) still pick the same four winners as before the unit change, confirming this was a pure relabeling with no change to the underlying comparisons. The bridge tool was already fully metric (E in N/cm², load in N) and needed no change.

Tab 6 and Tab 7 copy sharpened to match Matt's stated view of how this lesson should read as a whole (2026-09-04, no structural changes — he confirmed the existing 7-tab order already matches how he wants the lesson to flow: forces, then triangles, then load path, then design/build, then compare weightings, then compare to the model, then argue). Tab 6 (Predict vs. Build) now states explicitly that the predicted-vs-measured comparison is a test of the model's quality (a 2D, half-load simplification), not of the student's building accuracy. Tab 7 (Design Argument)'s claim/evidence prompts were reframed around Matt's point that a real engineering argument justifies a design as the best choice for the *specific, assigned* weighting, not as universally best — "which design best balances the three criteria, and for what weighting" (implying the student picks the weighting) became "given the weighting your instructor assigned, why is your final design the best one you found for it," and the evidence prompt now explicitly asks for Iteration Log numbers compared against that assigned weighting. The Tab 7 AI-feedback system prompt was updated to match: it now also pushes back if a student's argument never names or uses the assigned weighting, and checks that a predicted-vs-measured discussion treats the gap as a model-quality question, not just a build-accuracy one.

Tab order corrected: Iteration Log now comes before Design & Predict, not after (per Matt directly, 2026-09-04 — a real correction to a misreading on my part, not a new idea he floated). He wants students to understand how the weighting/scoring works *before* they design anything, not discover it only after building something — so the tab sequence is now Push & Pull, Why Triangles, Load Path, **Iteration Log**, **Design & Predict**, Predict vs. Build, Design Argument (tabs 4 and 5 swapped from the original plan). Implementation was a pure swap of the outer `id="tab-4"`/`id="tab-5"` container divs and their nav buttons/labels — none of the tabs' own internal element ids (`tab5-body`, `tab5-w-defl`, etc., named for the *feature*, not its position) needed to change, so this was lower-risk than it might sound. Cross-references updated to match (Iteration Log's closing note used to say "try it for real in Tab 4," now correctly says "Tab 5"; the page's own intro sentence and the Design & Predict stub's copy both now acknowledge the new order explicitly). Verified via Playwright that the 4th nav button shows Iteration Log content and the 5th shows Design & Predict, and that Iteration Log's own weighting demo still functions correctly in its new position.
