End-of-unit assessment (not started, scoping only): Matt wants an anthropology-assessment.html-style practical assessment for this unit eventually — tension/compression identification on a given structure, explaining/fixing an unstable structure, modifying a design for a new weighting — randomized per student with an auto-computed instructor answer key, same pattern as `anthropology/assessment.html`. One design question already answered directly: randomizing the *starting structure* and/or the *assigned weighting/criteria* per student is fine, as long as whatever's being tested was actually taught and practiced during the unit (don't randomize into skills outside what the tabs cover). Still open before building: whether "explain why unstable" reuses Tab 2's mechanism-detection/SVD engine directly or is AI-graded free text, and whether this is meant to be instructor-administered in one sitting or take-home. Do not start building this without a real scoping pass first (structures/archetypes, question types, randomization scheme) — same discipline anthropology's assessment needed before any code got written.

Load correction: both tools were modeling the wrong load, caught by Matt directly (2026-09-04). The real classroom loads are 1.5kg total for the cantilever and 1kg total for the bridge. The bridge is built the same way as the cantilever — two mirrored parallel truss faces connected front-to-back, sharing the load — but this had never been stated for the bridge before now; only the cantilever's mirrored-faces construction was previously documented. `bridge-designer.html`'s `LOAD_N` was `-9.81` (the full 1kg), not halved — changed to `-4.905` (half of 9.81N). Verified: the standalone `bridge_solve_validate.py` re-run at the halved load gives exactly half the old deflection at every configuration checked (linear-elastic truss, deflection scales exactly with load), and the browser tool's own computed deflection matches that Python re-run to full displayed precision. Header text now states the same "one face, mirrored, half the load" convention `truss-designer.html`'s header already stated, so both tools explain it identically.

For the cantilever, a real unit-conversion mistake happened in this exchange worth remembering the shape of: half of 1.5kg is 0.75kg, and the first instinct was to write that directly into the lbf-based solver as `-0.75` — but a kilogram of mass and a pound-force are not numerically interchangeable (1 kgf ≈ 2.20462 lbf), so `-0.75 lbf` would have modeled a load of about 0.34kg, roughly a third of the intended 0.75kg. Caught before it shipped. The correct value goes through Newtons explicitly, per Matt directly: 0.75kg × 9.81 = 7.3575N, ÷ 4.44822 N/lbf = 1.65403 lbf. Added as `const LOAD_LBF = -(0.75 * 9.81) / LBF_TO_N;` in `truss-designer.html` (previously a bare `-1.5` hardcoded directly in the embedded Python init string) so the derivation stays visible in the code rather than being a magic number. This is a deliberate, informed break from the earlier "preserve the legacy MASTAN load exactly" decision — Matt chose physical precision over exact-match-to-the-.mat-file precision, having been shown both the option to keep -1.5lbf and the option to correct it.

Every deflection and force number computed under the old loads is now stale, including plenty already written into this file — the ratio is exact and simple if a number ever needs re-deriving by hand rather than re-running the solver: cantilever numbers scale by 1.65403/1.5 = 1.102688×, bridge numbers scale by exactly 0.5×. Concretely re-verified and updated: `investigation.html`'s Tab 5 table (all four designs re-solved fresh at the new cantilever load, not just rescaled from the old rounded numbers, since rescaling compounds rounding error) and the "Load-Balanced" design specifically — the old 38-member design found by the seed=23 random-restart search no longer holds at the new load (peak-force values shifted enough that the search's absolute tie-breaking window explores a different path from the same seed), so it was re-found fresh via the same randomized-restart method: seed=30 now gives a 41-member design, 1.23cm deflection, $679.50, 9.82N peak force (vs. the full structure's 10.15N) — still a genuinely different, verified-better-on-force design, just a different member count than before. The four weight regimes (balanced default, max deflection, max cost, max force) still each pick a different one of the four designs, re-verified via Playwright; the force weight needed to demonstrate the "max force" case moved from ~56 to ~90-120 (comfortably inside the existing 0-150 slider range) since the Load-Balanced design's margin over the full structure is thinner at this load than the old one was. Not yet done: the earlier one-off comparison-figure PNG (the three-weighting-extremes demonstration image sent directly to Matt) was generated at the old -1.5lbf load and has not been regenerated — flag this if it's referenced again, since its numbers no longer match the live tools.

Meteorology / Energy Balance unit (guided investigation live and linked from the splash page, matching anthropology's structure)

Adapts a Colab notebook a high school physics teacher (Mr. Besong) has used for years — NASA CERES satellite energy-balance data, originally requiring an 800MB NetCDF file plus Cartopy/matplotlib for mapping, both incompatible with Pyodide — into a browser-only tool matching the platform's no-backend/no-external-dependency design. Lives under meteorology/ (promoted from meteorology/prototypes/ to match anthropology/'s flat top-level convention) and is linked from index.html's meteorology tile, badged "Live" — index.html's primary click now goes to meteorology/investigation.html (the guided unit, see below), not straight to the bare sandbox, mirroring how anthropology's card routes through student-landing.html rather than investigation.html directly.

meteorology/investigation.html — the guided unit, source content is Mr. Besong's "Energy & Earth" Honors Physics worksheet (2 parts: EM spectrum/blackbody/atmospheric-absorption conceptual content, then the classic textbook global energy-budget diagram, ISR=342 etc., sourced in turn from Penn State's "Lecture-Tutorials for Introductory Astronomy"), digitized and made interactive rather than ported as a static paper form. Five tabs, mirroring anthropology's investigation.html arc (build foundational vocabulary, verify against something real, open into genuine investigation):
- Tab 1, Light & Energy: an interactive blackbody-curve tool (simplified Planck's law, canvas-drawn, Sun 5778K vs Earth 288K vs a free-entry custom temperature, each curve normalized to its own peak so both fit one chart despite radiating wildly different total energy — a teaching visualization of where each curve peaks, Wien's law, never named as such, matching the worksheet's own non-formal treatment) and a click-to-reveal atmospheric absorption spectrum (5 wavelength bands, click to reveal the responsible molecule) replacing the worksheet's static figures with something students manipulate. Both "Student 1 vs Student 2" debate prompts ported as pick-a-side-then-reveal, no live data involved — there's no natural real-data pairing for wavelength-resolved content on this platform, so none was forced.
- Tab 2, Radiation & the Energy Budget: ISR/RSR/ASR/OLR/DLR/NAR/albedo definitions, a live calculator (enter ISR/RSR, watch ASR and albedo compute; enter ASR/OLR, watch NAR compute and get a plain-language balance read), and an interactive, checked version of the worksheet's fill-in-the-diagram exercise using its exact numbers (ISR=342, RSR direct=30, etc.). Framed around NGSS Science and Engineering Practices (analyzing/interpreting data, using mathematics and computational thinking) rather than AAAS Vision and Change, unlike anthropology's lesson plan — this audience is genuinely K-12 (Mr. Besong's Honors Physics class), so NGSS is the right frame here specifically.
- Tab 3, Predict & Compare (redesigned from an original "Verify With Real Data" version — see the dedicated paragraph below for why): two predict-then-check comparisons, each asking the student to write a prediction grounded in the physics *before* they touch real data, deliberately not a "does the computed number match the textbook" exercise. "Same Place, Across Time" (Arctic ISR, winter vs. summer, using the Map display type, Compare mode, region pre-set but months left for the student to pick and run themselves) and "Same Place, Across Variables" (ISR vs. ASR as a Scatter plot, var1/var2/chart type pre-set but region left open for the student to choose). Each comparison deliberately uses a different display type than Tab 4's Trend Map, so by Tab 5 the student has had guided practice with three of the sandbox's four display types, not just one.
- Tab 4, Beyond the Global Average: a Compare-mode deep link showing real Arctic albedo and real Arctic sea-ice-cover trend maps side by side, 2000-2025, turning the worksheet's sea-ice albedo question (a photo + "which parts would have higher albedo, explain your reasoning") into an actual measured comparison.
- Tab 5, Investigate: iframes the plain sandbox unmodified, full open investigation, notebook logging, AI feedback. Below it, an optional, collapsed-by-default "Stuck? Here's What Real Research Has Found" panel (five `<details>` entries: the ice-albedo feedback, the Arctic/Antarctic sea-ice asymmetry, polar amplification, clouds' dual and still-uncertain radiative role, land-vs-ocean warming contrast), each stating plainly how settled vs. actively-researched the explanation actually is and pointing to which variables/regions would let a student check it themselves. Deliberately not required reading and not gating anything — for a student who doesn't know where to start with open-ended investigation, not part of the guided sequence everyone goes through. This is also where the Arctic/Antarctic asymmetry ended up: floated as a possible Tab 4 addition, deliberately rejected for the guided tabs specifically because it's a genuine, uncurated anomaly (not a staged "discrepant event") that would derail attention from the basics Tab 4 is still building for students not yet ready for it — moved here instead, where encountering it is optional and self-paced rather than forced on the whole class at once.

Living inside Tab 5 is explicitly a stopgap, not the intended permanent home — Matt flagged this directly. Don't be surprised to be asked to relocate it; no destination has been decided yet (candidates worth asking about when that conversation happens: a differentiated/accounts-gated surface once accounts exist, matching the "shown only to students who need it" intent better than a manually-expanded panel everyone sees; or its own standalone resource page, not nested in the guided sequence at all).
Tabs 3-5 embed energy-balance-sandbox.html via iframe rather than duplicating its ~1900 lines of data-loading/computation code into a second file — kept as the single source of truth. This works via a small deep-link param scheme added to the sandbox itself: `?var=&var2=&region=&type=&run=1` (plus a "B" suffix for Compare mode columns) pre-selects a variable/second-variable/region/display-type and auto-runs it on load, silently ignoring anything unrecognized — `run=1` is deliberately omitted on Tab 3's two comparisons so the student has to actually configure the remaining choice (which months, which region) and click Run themselves, rather than landing on a pre-computed answer. `embed=1` hides the sandbox's own header ("Earth's Energy Budget · Research Sandbox") so it doesn't announce itself as a separate app when nested in an iframe — added after direct feedback that seeing the sandbox's own branding nested inside the guided page broke the sense of being one continuous investigation; applied to all of Tabs 3-5's iframes, not just the one that prompted it. Tab 5's iframe passes only `embed=1`, otherwise landing on the sandbox's own defaults exactly as a standalone visitor would see it.

Tab 3 was originally built as "Verify With Real Data" (compare a computed global mean against Tab 2's textbook numbers, 342 W/m² etc.) and then redesigned after direct feedback: comparing two numbers for closeness isn't asking the student to reason about anything, it's a lookup task, exactly the single-metric-resolves-it shape this project's own design principle exists to avoid. Rebuilt as "Predict & Compare" — see the Tab 3 description above — matching the predict-then-check pattern Tab 4 was already using well. General lesson worth not re-learning: a "does this match a reference value" framing is an easy trap to fall into when real data happens to be available to check something against, but availability of a check isn't the same as the check being pedagogically meaningful.

Sandbox now has a third derived variable, albedo (RSR ÷ ISR), needed for Tab 4. Unlike the existing asr/cre derived variables (pure subtraction, always defined for any input), albedo divides by ISR, which is genuinely zero during polar night — its `op` returns NaN rather than Infinity/garbage when ISR is below 1 W/m², and both aggregation call sites (computeSpatialGrid's per-pixel path, filteredYearlySeries' per-year averaging) were updated to skip a NaN result the same way they already skip ERA5's missing-value sentinel, rather than letting it poison an entire cell/year's average. Verified against a real full-year Arctic trend map (the exact case that would have broken without the guard) before merging.

The standalone-prototype disclaimer banner ("Prototype — Energy Balance research sandbox, not yet part of a full guided unit") and the matching title-tag suffix were removed from energy-balance-sandbox.html once the guided investigation made that framing inaccurate. The file itself, its URL, and its behavior as a standalone page are otherwise completely unchanged — anyone holding a direct link to it from before this (Matt shared it with early testers for feedback) is unaffected.

meteorology/energy-balance-sandbox.html — single-file research sandbox. Students pick a display type (time series, scatter/correlation, spatial trend map, or a non-trend spatial map — see below), a variable, a year range, a month selection (All/Winter DJF/Summer JJA quick-picks or custom), and a lat/lon region (Global/Arctic/Antarctic/Tropics/Hemisphere presets or a custom box), run the analysis, and log a "what do you see / what does it mean / what questions does it raise" notebook entry (localStorage key epistemic_energy_balance_notebook). A "Get AI Feedback" button now reviews that entry — see the dedicated paragraph below for how it differs from the anthropology unit's Tab 5 feedback and why. Trend maps and Maps also have a "Color scale" dropdown (per teacher feedback, students were being pointed at matplotlib's colormap reference page and wanted the same freedom here) — "Auto" keeps the physically-motivated default (diverging, zero-centered for trends and signed variables; sequential, zero-to-max for physically non-negative variables), or a student can force any specific palette, including a diverging one onto a non-negative variable or vice versa, which is left in deliberately since seeing a badly-matched colormap waste half its range is itself the lesson matplotlib's own docs make about choosing colormaps appropriately. Three palettes (Viridis, Cividis, Purple-Orange) are labeled "colorblind-safe" for deuteranopia/protanopia specifically — the rest are left unlabeled rather than guessed at, after an initial version of this feature shipped that label on Pink-Yellow-Green in error (pink carries enough red to sit near the exact red-green confusion axis those conditions involve), caught before merge by direct user feedback.

An "Analysis mode" selector (Explore / Compare) sits above the display panels, added per direct teacher feedback that comparative research — one variable across different seasons, or two variables over the same time/place — is a typical way this kind of investigation actually gets done. Explore is the original single-panel behavior, unchanged. Compare renders a second, fully independent "Build a Display" + "Display" panel pair (its own display type, variable(s), years, months, region, projection, colormap) side by side with the first, both generated from one HTML template function (`buildAnalysisPanelHTML`) keyed by an id suffix ("" for column A, "B" for column B) so the two can never drift apart in markup. Every function that touches these panels' DOM elements takes that same suffix, defaulting to "" so single-column call sites are unaffected. The shared "What Do You See" reflect panel below both columns adapts automatically: if only one column has been run, it behaves exactly as before; if both have, "Log This Entry" and "Get AI Feedback" combine both runs' computed results, and the AI feedback prompt is told explicitly to check the student's description of the *difference* between the two, not just each one in isolation — same grounding-in-computed-numbers approach as the single-run case, just extended to two.

Each display panel also has a "Save PNG" button. For the Plotly-based displays (time series, scatter) this is just `Plotly.downloadImage`, since Plotly already bakes axes/legend/title into the rendered figure. For the canvas-based maps (trend map, map), the legend and title are separate DOM elements, not part of the canvas pixels — exporting the bare canvas would hand a student an unlabeled color map, useless for "save it for a later presentation," so it composites title + map + a redrawn legend gradient (from the same color stops used on screen) onto one offscreen canvas before triggering the download.

The "Map" display type (as opposed to "Trend map") shows a plain spatial snapshot — each cell's average over the selected years/months, not its rate of change — sharing the same fine/coarse tile pipeline and raster renderer (`computeSpatialGrid`'s `mode` argument is "trend" vs. "mean"; `drawSpatialMapCanvas` takes a `colorFn` so it doesn't need to know which). Color scale depends on the variable's `zeroAnchor` flag: physically non-negative variables (ISR, RSR, OLR, cloud fraction, clear-sky OLR, ASR) get a sequential scale from 0 to the region's max, since centering the diverging trend-map scale on 0 would waste half the palette on values that never occur; net flux and cloud radiative effect (legitimately signed — a region can be a net energy source or sink) keep the diverging scale even in Map mode. Unlike a trend map, Map mode works with a single selected year.

Six CERES raw variables (isr, rsr, olr, net, cld, clrolr — incoming solar, reflected shortwave, outgoing longwave, net flux, cloud fraction, clear-sky OLR) plus two derived (asr = isr−rsr, cre = olr−clrolr), matching real CERES EBAF-TOA product variables (solar_mon, toa_sw_all_mon, toa_lw_all_mon, toa_net_all_mon, cldarea_total_daynight_mon, toa_lw_clr_c_mon). Plus five ERA5 reanalysis variables (t2m, sst, siconc, tp, strd — 2m air temperature, sea surface temperature, sea ice cover, precipitation, downward longwave/DLR) — see the dedicated "Second dataset" paragraph below for why these were added and what they needed that CERES didn't.

Two-tier resolution architecture: one always-loaded coarse dataset (2°, 90×180 grid, full globe, every month) for time series/scatter work and for scanning the whole globe on a trend map; separate fine-resolution (1°, native CERES resolution) tiles (30°×30° each, 72 tiles cover the globe) fetched only for whichever tiles a selected region overlaps, capped at 12 tiles (~17MB) before automatically falling back to the coarse dataset. This exists because a single global dataset at 1° was empirically ruled out (≈183MB, too large to load on every visit) and because trend maps at 2° were confirmed too coarse to read (a 30°×40° custom region is 300 cells at 2° vs 1200 at 1°).

Trend maps render via hand-rolled spherical projection math (equirectangular, orthographic, stereographic — Snyder's standard forward/inverse formulas), not Plotly's geo traces or Cartopy: Cartopy doesn't work in Pyodide (see above), and a scattergeo-marker approach was tried first and abandoned — no fixed marker size can correctly tile a lat/lon grid under a curved, non-cylindrical projection (confirmed by a visibly broken "pinwheel" render under stereographic at global extent). The working approach inverse-projects every output canvas pixel back to a lat/lon and looks up its cell, which is correct by construction for any projection. A bundled ~76KB world coastline file (110m resolution, decoded offline from the world-atlas npm package via topojson-client, not fetched live) draws over the raster for geographic orientation, clipped to the same region bounds as the data itself — an earlier version clipped coastlines only to the projection's own visibility, not the selected region, so coastlines from well outside the analyzed area could render past the edge of the data.

Data: real CERES EBAF-TOA Edition 4.2.1, monthly, 2001-2025 (25 full years — the record starts March 2000 and the available subset ends April 2026, so both partial-coverage years were dropped). Quantized to int16 with a single global scale factor, gzip-compressed. Getting this data in is worth remembering for next time: this sandbox has no route to fetch anything from NASA Earthdata or Google Drive, so the conversion has to run in a Python environment that already has the file (Colab, in this case), sending back only the small processed output — and even that small output had real trouble getting from a local computer into GitHub. The ~39.5MB coarse file was too large for GitHub's web upload flow; a subsequent attempt got mangled into garbled text after the .gz extension triggered the OS's own archive handler to silently decompress it, and the resulting garbage got pasted through GitHub's "create new file" text editor instead of uploaded as binary. What finally worked: strip the .gz extension entirely before the file leaves Colab (a plain unlabeled binary blob nothing tries to "helpfully" touch), then push via GitHub Desktop rather than the website upload widget, which also silently drops files into the wrong folder when uploaded in separate batches. Verified end to end afterward: decompressed byte counts match the manifest exactly, and the tool's own area-weighted global mean for incoming solar radiation comes out to 340.5 W/m², matching the well-known published value.

Second dataset: ERA5 reanalysis (2m Air Temperature, Sea Surface Temperature, Sea Ice Cover, Precipitation — t2m/sst/siconc/tp), added per teacher feedback that Max's own lesson uses these alongside CERES. Same manifest+gzip pattern, same Colab-conversion pipeline, and — deliberately — regridded during conversion onto CERES's exact lat/lon grid and trimmed to the exact same 2001-2025 year range (ERA5's own record actually starts a full year earlier, at full-year 2000, but CERES's usable range doesn't, and matching CERES rather than ERA5's fuller range is what lets the two datasets share one year index for cross-dataset comparison). This pairing is a deliberate pedagogical complement, not just "more variables": CERES is the energy budget itself (radiation in/out at the top of atmosphere); ERA5's four variables are the observable surface-level consequences of that budget (does the radiation imbalance actually show up in temperature/ice/SST/precipitation trends). The Scatter display type can mix one variable from each dataset directly (e.g. Net Flux vs. 2m Air Temperature) since both share the same grid and year index.

Two things ERA5 needed that CERES didn't: (1) ERA5's four variables span wildly different magnitudes (°C, %, mm/day) so each carries its own quantization scale factor in its manifest, unlike CERES's one shared scale — computeSpatialGrid/regionalMonthlyMeans read a variable's own GRID entry either way, so this needed no shared-format migration, just per-variable scale support in the ERA5 load path. (2) sst and siconc are only defined over ocean (confirmed: ~26.9% of the global grid is missing, matching Earth's land fraction) — a reserved int16 sentinel (-32768) marks missing cells, and every grid-reading function (computeSpatialGrid, regionalMonthlyMeans) skips them rather than averaging in garbage; verified visually (a global SST map correctly renders land as blank, not a corrupted color) and numerically (global mean 2m temperature comes out to 14.57°C, matching the well-known published value, the same kind of sanity check used for CERES's 340.5 W/m²). ERA5 has no fine-resolution regional tiles (unlike CERES's 1° tile system) — the trend-map/map fetch logic checks each variable's `dataset` tag and always uses ERA5's coarse 2° grid regardless of region size, rather than attempting a fine-tile fetch that would 404. One known data quirk worth not mistaking for a bug: ERA5's SST field dips slightly below the real seawater freezing point (~-1.8°C) under sea ice, since ERA5 uses a diagnostic proxy value there rather than a real measurement — this is inherent to the source data and would show up identically in any tool reading the same ERA5 file, including Max's own Colab script.

Important distinction: the Colab notebook referenced above (Mr. Besong's original lesson, used as the source for the sandbox's data/analysis logic) predates public generative AI availability and contains no AI/API calls of any kind — there is no exposed key in it and nothing to route through the Cloudflare Worker. This was previously misdocumented in this file; if AI feedback ever gets asked about for the meteorology unit, it means a new feature to design, not a fix to something already in that notebook.

Terminology note: when this unit's design talks about a "notebook," across every mention in this file it means a pedagogical science notebook — a student-facing log of what was studied, why, what was found, and what it led to (claim/evidence/reasoning), the same spirit as Tab 5's argument-building in the anthropology unit. It does not mean a Jupyter-style code notebook, and Pyodide is not involved in it.

AI feedback: a "Get AI Feedback" button next to "Log This Entry" sends the student's three notebook responses to the same deployed Cloudflare Worker the anthropology unit uses (cloudflare-worker/worker.js — no worker changes were needed, since it's already fully generic: it just proxies {systemPrompt, userPrompt} to Gemini and holds the key server-side, with no idea which unit is calling it). The prompt design is deliberately different from anthropology's Tab 5, because the underlying problem is different: Tab 5 checks an argument against one of four known mystery specimens with a fixed answer key, so it can safely hand the model the correct identity and grade against it. Here, a student can run any variable/region/period combination, so there is no fixed answer key — instead, the prompt hands the model the run's own computed result (the same summary string already shown in the annotation, e.g. "Trend 0.0071 W/m²/yr, r=0.431, mean 340.50 W/m² (...)") as the ONLY ground truth, explicitly forbids inventing additional numbers/facts, and scopes the model to three things: whether the student's "what do you see" matches what was actually computed, whether their "what does it mean" is a plausible mechanism without overreaching what one run can establish, and pushing them toward a concrete next analysis rather than accepting a single metric as sufficient (the same "not a matching game" principle stated at the top of this file, applied here instead of to primate specimens). Verified end-to-end against a mocked worker response (request payload, loading/error states, button text) since this sandbox's own dev environment cannot reach the deployed worker over the network — the actual Gemini output quality has not yet been reviewed by a human and should be checked in a real browser before relying on it.

Planned but not started: an instructor resource page mirroring anthropology/instructor.html (slide decks, lesson plan, answer/reference materials) — the guided investigation itself (meteorology/investigation.html) is now built, see above, this is specifically the teacher-facing page that doesn't exist yet. Decided but not yet built: that page should include the pedagogical rationale for the tab sequence itself (why predict-then-check before open investigation, what each step is building toward) — deliberately kept out of the student-facing tabs, which should read as content, not as a stated curriculum design, but worth explaining to teachers.

ERA5's `strd` (surface thermal radiation downwards / DLR) is sourced and wired in as a 5th ERA5 variable (dataset:"era5" in RAW_VARS, `energy-balance-sandbox.html`), byte-appended onto the existing monthly_era5.bin.gz/manifest rather than a separate file, loaded automatically by the already-generic ERA5 loading loop in loadData() with no loader changes needed. Tab 3 of the guided investigation now has a real DLR verification panel (global mean, ERA5 reanalysis, compared against the worksheet's 324 W/m² textbook figure) alongside the existing ISR and net flux panels. Verified three independent ways before merging: Colab's own computed global mean, an independent from-scratch Python recomputation off the actual committed binary, and the sandbox's own production JS computing the same value through its real loading/aggregation code — all three agreed to many decimal places (~340.88 W/m²), and a byte-equality check confirmed the other four ERA5 variables were untouched by the append.

Getting this one variable actually right took several rounds worth remembering for next time: (1) the first CDS download turned out to be the wrong dataset shape entirely (a single-point hourly time series of `fdir`, direct solar shortwave, not `strd` at all) — twice, the second attempt was the identical wrong file again, traced to stale extracted files left in Colab's `/content` from the prior run, not a second bad download; (2) a suspicious-looking `06:00:00`-only timestamp on an otherwise-correct file turned out to be a harmless fixed reference-time CDS attaches to true monthly-mean accumulated fields, not evidence of the wrong "by hour of day" product — resolved by checking the actual physical value (a global-mean sanity check against the worksheet's own 324 W/m² figure) rather than trusting metadata interpretation alone; (3) a legitimately correct file was still missing a clean 4-year block (2004-2007 entirely) from an apparent CDS delivery hiccup, caught by explicitly checking for missing year-months rather than just checking total count. General lesson: for any accumulated ERA5 field, verify with the actual computed physical value against a known reference before trusting metadata, and always check for date-range gaps explicitly, not just a total record count, a partial delivery can still add up to a plausible-looking number.

Agricultural unit (crop/soil modeling, adapting the real CYCLES agroecosystem model) — very early, in progress

Source material: a Colab notebook (`Copy_of_Cycles_CONUS_using_Cycles_utils.ipynb`, committed to `main`) that runs the real CYCLES model (Penn State, Kemanian et al.) for a user-defined field boundary. The notebook downloads gSSURGO soil data, NLDAS-2 weather, and generates Cycles input files via the `cycles-utils` pip package, then executes a compiled Cycles binary and plots results. Matt has a direct working relationship with the CYCLES developers and expects them to be receptive to this use.

Licensing (the central constraint on this whole unit, read before doing anything else here): Cycles is released under **CC BY-NC-ND 4.0** (verified directly from `LICENSE.md` in github.com/PSUmodeling/Cycles) — noncommercial only and no derivatives, both in tension with this platform's eventual commercial-acquisition model and with "modify Cycles for classroom use" specifically. Getting explicit permission from the devs is in progress (Matt reaching out directly) but not yet confirmed in writing — don't treat it as resolved. Separately, the input-data pipeline (gSSURGO/GADM/NLDAS) reads from a private "Cycles global simulation database" Google Shared Drive Matt has access to but this sandbox does not — any static data this unit ships needs Matt to run the Colab preprocessing himself and export the result, same pattern as CERES/ERA5.

Four technical paths for getting the real model working have now been investigated; three are closed off or set aside:
- **WASM compilation of the real source**: not attempted, because there is no public source to compile. The public `PSUmodeling/Cycles` GitHub repo and every other repo in that GitHub org ships only compiled binaries and sample input/output files — confirmed by downloading the actual v1.4.4 release zip and inspecting its contents (one ELF binary, no `.c`/`.h` anywhere), and the repo's own README says outright "this repository is used to release Cycles executables," not source. Emscripten itself installs and works fine in this sandbox — the blocker is purely the missing source, not the toolchain.
- **v86 (run the real unmodified Linux binary inside a browser-based x86 emulator, no source needed)**: built and tested a real headless boot harness (Node.js + the `v86` npm package + a real bootable Linux ISO, `linux4.iso`, committed to `main` at Matt's request after the correct host, `i.copy.sh`, was tracked down from v86's own CI pipeline — an earlier guess at `k.copy.sh` was wrong and got corrected). The real Cycles binary was actually pushed into the emulator's 9p virtual filesystem and executed — and failed with a BusyBox "applet not found" error. Root cause, confirmed directly: `uname -a` inside the booted guest reports a 32-bit i686 kernel, and v86's own Readme states plainly "64-bit kernels are not supported" as an explicit, permanent project limitation, not a missing image to hunt down. Cycles only ships 64-bit Linux builds (checked: no `Cycles_i386` release exists). This path is dead for this specific binary, conclusively, not just difficult — don't re-attempt it without a fundamentally different emulator.
- **Small backend running the real binary server-side**: prototyped and proven to work (`POST /simulate` → real Cycles execution → parsed JSON, tested against two different real rotations with physically distinct results, not a canned response). Deliberately not deployed anywhere — this breaks the platform's stated no-backend architecture (see "Current stage" and "What is deliberately deferred" above), and Matt chose to pursue it only as a fallback/comparison, not the primary path. The prototype code was not committed to this repo (it lived in the sandbox only) since it was explicitly exploratory.
- **Blink (x86-64 emulator) compiled to WebAssembly, running the real unmodified binary client-side**: unlike v86, Blink supports 64-bit guests, so this path actually works — see the dedicated "WASM-Cycles investigation" paragraph below for the full technical account and why it's set aside for the live tool anyway (speed, not correctness).

The path actually being pursued now: a **from-scratch reimplementation**, independently built from Cycles' own published methodology rather than its code — legally clean regardless of the licensing outcome (copyright protects the specific code expression, not the underlying published science; 17 U.S.C. §102(b)), and it doesn't need source, a backend, or an emulator. Matt supplied both the primary methods paper (Kemanian et al. 2024, *Computers and Electronics in Agriculture* 227, "The Cycles Agroecosystem Model: Fundamentals, Testing, and Applications") and its Supplemental Information document, which together give real, complete, numbered governing equations for water/energy balance, canopy growth and yield, curve-number runoff, and soil carbon/nitrogen saturation dynamics — not just descriptions. Two likely-systematic sign errors were found in the SI's typeset equations (the runoff denominator and the wet-curve-number formula both show a `−` where the external standard methods they cite, and basic algebraic derivation, require `+` — implemented with the corrected sign in both cases, flagged in code). Per-crop numeric parameters (radiation/transpiration-use efficiency, phenology thresholds, harvest-index bounds) come from the real `GenericCrops.crop` file bundled with the public binary release itself — genuine input data the software reads at runtime, not literature guesses.

Scope for v1 (decided directly with Matt): all three of Cycles' bundled sample rotations (continuous corn, corn-silage-soy-wheat, corn-soy-wheat-pasture — the last one pulls in winter wheat and cut-and-regrow perennial forage species, real added complexity worth having named). Water balance and yield only — full nitrogen/carbon cycling (the model's own claimed core innovation) is explicitly deferred; real Cycles output confirms zero nitrogen stress in the validated reference scenario anyway, so skipping it isn't costing accuracy there. Nitrogen rate is kept as a student-facing knob via a simplified mineral-N balance (fertilizer applied minus crop uptake minus basic leaching) rather than the full six-pool soil C/N system. Arbitrary KML field upload (the feature that originally motivated this whole investigation) is explicitly out of scope for now, deferred to a later pass once a validated engine exists — not to be silently assumed back into v1.

What's built and validated so far: `agricultural/prototypes/cycles_engine_validate.py`, an independent Python engine (no Cycles code or binary in it, and deliberately no Cycles-generated reference data committed alongside it either — see the file's own docstring for why: republishing Cycles' *output* may need the same permission as the model itself, an open question for the devs that hasn't been asked yet). Validated against real Cycles v1.4.4 output for the Rock Springs, PA continuous-corn sample scenario (37 years, 1980-2016):
- Reference evapotranspiration (FAO-56 Penman-Monteith, a standard external public formula): matches real Cycles' own output to 0.008 mm/day mean absolute error across the full record — essentially exact, as expected of a correctly-implemented standard method.
- Curve-number runoff: right order of magnitude, not a clean match — the model's own moisture-adjustment factor (`fwc`) is described only in words in the SI ("1 for soil saturated to 0.6m depth... depth-weighted toward the surface"), not given as an exact formula; the current implementation is a defensible interpretation, not verified exact.
- Soil-temperature-triggered planting: no soil temperature formula is disclosed in either source (the SI's own process flowchart marks it as covered, but the actual equation isn't there — same gap as soil evaporation, below). A standard lag-filter proxy of air temperature was used instead and validated directly against real per-year planting dates: 2.65 days mean absolute error across all 37 years.
- Crop growth and yield: went from actively backwards (a first, ad hoc proxy model — not the one in this file — had *negative* year-to-year correlation with real output, rating real drought years as the best years) to 0.744 correlation once real soil-moisture stress was coupled into transpiration (the model initially used pure atmospheric-demand-based potential transpiration with no soil-moisture limit at all, so it could never be more water-stressed than the atmosphere alone implied, no matter how dry the actual soil was). After that fix, four candidate explanations for a remaining ~20-25% absolute-level overestimate were tested and none panned out: canopy cover trajectory checked out fine against real daily output; real nitrogen stress is zero in this scenario so isn't the cause; CO2 scaling of radiation-use-efficiency is undisclosed in both sources and implausible to be large for a C4 crop like corn; cold-temperature transpiration stress is a real, disclosed mechanism (now implemented, using real crop-file thresholds) but has negligible effect at this site. Rather than keep guessing, a disclosed calibration factor was applied to bring the absolute mean in line with real output — it does not affect year-to-year correlation, only scale. That factor was later found to be covering for a real, separate bug (see the update further below, from validating the other two rotations) — after fixing it, the correct residual calibration factor is smaller (0.876, not 0.645). Current validated numbers: mean yield 10.97 vs. real 10.93 Mg/ha, mean absolute error 0.52 Mg/ha, correlation 0.759.
- Bare-soil evaporation: no disclosed formula in either source (again, the flowchart marks it as covered; the actual equation isn't there) — implemented as a standard proxy (ETo scaled by non-intercepted radiation fraction, limited by available surface-layer water), not a verified Cycles formula.

Update from validating the other two rotations (in progress): most of the ~20-25% level gap noted above turned out to be a real, fixable bug, not an unidentified missing mechanism. Real Cycles' own `HARVEST_INDEX` column is defined as grain ÷ *aboveground* biomass (verified exactly: 11.61 / (26.17 - 3.48 root) = 0.5117, matching the real reported value; 11.61 / 26.17 does not), but the model had been applying harvest index to *total* biomass the whole time. Fixed by adding real shoot/root partitioning (Eq. SI.8-11, using the crop file's real INITIAL/FINAL_PARTITIONING_TO_SHOOT values; the equation's own TTf50 half-max parameter has no disclosed numeric value in either source, so the literal reading of its name, 0.5, was used, flagged in code). After the fix: grain corn's pre-calibration ratio dropped from 1.55 to 1.14 and correlation improved 0.744 → 0.768; soybean similarly improved (ratio 0.78 → 0.90, correlation 0.83 → 0.85). Calibration factors are now applied per-crop (`crop["calibration_factor"]` in code), not as one global constant, since soybean's residual runs the opposite direction from corn's — confirmed this isn't one uniform missing mechanism.

Winter wheat and silage corn, by contrast, are still rough. Wheat's correlation got *worse* after the AG-biomass fix (0.535 → 0.362) — diagnosed properly rather than guessed at: thermal-time accumulation was checked directly against real Cycles output and ruled out (ours: 1783.6, real: 1740.76 by harvest, within 2.5%), but canopy cover was not: at the point real Cycles shows 0.746 canopy cover, the paper's default shape constants (calibrated for corn, explicitly stated to represent "a normalized plant density of 1" for that case) predict 0.886, a real, verified gap. A wheat-specific refit of those shape constants (fit against 13 real data points from one real season) improved the level bias (yield ratio 1.51 → 1.40, now calibrated to match the mean exactly) but did NOT fix correlation (0.362 → 0.253, still worse than before the AG-biomass fix) — the actual driver of wheat's weak year-to-year tracking remains unidentified after two real diagnostic attempts.

Silage corn got the same treatment and turned up something more general. Every individual mechanism checks out: thermal time matches real Cycles within 1%, canopy cover matches within a few percent at every checked date, harvest date (triggered at `HARVEST_TIMING=85` → 85% of thermal time to maturity, confirmed directly from the crop file and the operation file's own comment explaining why forage crops aren't given a fixed harvest date) matches real dates to 1.1 days mean error across 13 years, and the aboveground-biomass-to-forage-yield ratio is a rock-solid 0.9499-0.9500 across all 13 real harvests. None of that explains correlation stuck at 0.503. A day-by-day residual check (the same technique that found the AG-biomass bug) found the real, if small, cause of the *level* bias: the paper's canopy-cover defaults run about 5% high at peak even for corn itself (real 0.943 vs. predicted 0.990) — negligible for grain corn, since by the time this matters the crop is already senescing and growth has stopped, but silage corn is harvested mid-peak-growth (85% of maturity, not 100%), so the same small daily overestimate compounds for weeks longer before harvest (AG biomass ratio to real output grows from 1.00 at mid-season to 1.25 by harvest). A corn-specific canopy refit (6, -20, -12, 12, also applied to grain corn, nudging its correlation up to 0.771) fixed silage corn's level bias but barely moved its correlation (0.503 → 0.501) — confirming the level bias and the correlation problem are two separate things, and the correlation problem in both wheat and silage corn is still genuinely unresolved. What the two failing-correlation crops share, that corn/soybean don't: neither is harvested at full senescence (silage stops at 85% of maturity while still actively growing; wheat crosses a real winter) — logged as a specific, evidence-backed question rather than continuing to guess.

Follow-up diagnostic session (2026-09-10, correlation numbers unchanged — this was pure diagnosis, no fix landed): went looking for the actual driver behind wheat's and silage corn's stuck correlation by directly comparing this model's day-by-day water stress against real Cycles' own `WATER STRESS` output column (never previously checked directly) — and found two genuinely different problems hiding under one symptom.
- **Silage corn**: stress *magnitude* is right (real layer 1-3 soil moisture during the 2016 peak is genuinely down near wilting point, 0.13-0.17 m3/m3 — a real drought, nothing anomalous), but *timing* is off by about 11 days (model onsets 2016-07-11, real onsets 2016-06-30), and the within-event shape differs. Three independent, principled fixes were swept across all four validated crops to see if any single recalibration closed this without breaking corn/soybean: raising the stress-onset threshold (0.5→0.8), slowing root growth rate, and widening bare-soil evaporation's extraction depth to FAO-56's standard 0.10-0.30m (a real public constant, not a guess). None meaningfully moved silage corn's correlation, and all three modestly hurt corn/soybean as the parameter increased — ruling out simple recalibration and pointing at the soil water *redistribution* physics itself (the paper's real Eq. 1-2 is a sub-daily, capacitance-weighted flow calculation; this engine approximates it with a same-day cascading bucket, flagged as such in the module docstring since the first validation pass). Implementing the real physics needs soil hydraulic parameters (pore-size distribution exponent, air-entry potential, saturated hydraulic conductivity) that Saxton-Rawls (2006) reportedly also provides — but every source tried to verify the exact secondary formulas (four different mirrors) was blocked by this sandbox's network egress policy, not confirmed as paywalled. Added to the dev-questions list as a real, disclosed-but-unverifiable-here item rather than guessing at coefficients.
- **Winter wheat**: a stranger, structurally different anomaly, not a timing problem. Real wheat yield correlates -0.89 with real Cycles' own max-per-season water stress across all 9 harvested years — confirming stress is genuinely the dominant driver, not a red herring. But this model's max-per-season stress only correlates 0.37 against real Cycles', and shows *zero* stress in three years (2003, 2009, 2012) where real Cycles reports 27-60%. Traced the worst case (2012, real peak 59.5% on 2012-03-16) directly against real Cycles' own soil-moisture output for that date: layer-1 moisture is 0.305 m3/m3 against a field capacity of 0.339 — only mildly depleted, ~83% of field capacity — while the crop is still early vegetative growth (thermal time 42% to maturity). No standard depletion-fraction stress function would produce 60% stress from that mild a deficit. First suspected a winter-dormancy desync in the thermal-time engine; ruled out by comparing day-by-day (not just season-total) thermal time against real Cycles' own column across a full season — every checkpoint matches within 1-3%, including both going flat in December-January. So wheat's real water-stress mechanism is doing something this model isn't modeling at all (a shallower/slower real root-growth curve specific to wheat, a much more nonlinear stress response, or an early-season restriction that isn't moisture-driven in the way assumed) — logged as a sharp, evidence-backed question rather than more blind tuning. Full detail on both findings, including the ruled-out fixes so they aren't re-attempted, in `agricultural/prototypes/QUESTIONS_FOR_DEVS.md` items 6-7.

Classroom-workable bar (agreed with Matt 2026-09-10): a rotation/crop is only a student-selectable option once its correlation against real Cycles output is roughly 0.6-0.7 or better, matching what corn and soybean already achieve — not shipped with caveats below that. Current standing: **corn 0.771 and soybean 0.846 pass; winter wheat 0.253, silage corn 0.501, and the pasture perennials (near-zero/negative) do not.** A running, specific list of open questions for the CYCLES developers is being kept at `agricultural/prototypes/QUESTIONS_FOR_DEVS.md` rather than asking piecemeal — intent is one short list sent once, after independent effort is exhausted, not an ongoing back-and-forth. Matt has reached out to the devs for a meeting as of 2026-09-10; timing unknown, so independent work continues in parallel rather than pausing to wait.

Perennial pasture species (Lotus corniculatus, white clover, orchardgrass) — attempted, not resolved, real finding worth not re-deriving from scratch next time: real per-cut harvest data confirms these genuinely are cut-and-regrow on one persistent stand (same PLANT_DATE repeats across multiple cuts within a year and across years — verified directly, e.g. orchardgrass planted 1982-03-16 shows cuts on 1982-06-10/07-08/08-09/09-20 and again 1983-06-08, all carrying that same planting date). The 6-year rotation only grows pasture 4 of those 6 years (confirmed from real harvest.txt: years present in blocks of 4 with 2-year gaps, e.g. 1982-1985, then a gap, then 1988-1991) — each new block is a fresh establishment, not a continuation of the prior block's mature stand; an initial implementation that ignored this got correlation 0.109, fixing it (reset state at each gap) actually made correlation worse (-0.276), which is itself informative: the remaining error isn't about state carryover, it's about the cutting trigger itself.

Follow-up session (2026-09-10): pushed the diagnosis further by checking the real `GenericCrops.crop` values directly (`CLIPPING_BIOMASS_THRESHOLD_UPPER=4` Mg/ha, `CLIPPING_BIOMASS_THRESHOLD_LOWER=1` Mg/ha, `HARVEST_TIMING=60%`, `STANDING_RESIDUE_AT_HARVEST=50%`, `RESIDUE_REMOVED=80%`) and comparing against real Cycles' own daily crop-output file (`Orchardgrass.txt`, not just the harvest summary) day by day around all four 1982 cuts. Four separate trigger hypotheses are now ruled out with direct evidence, not just two: a fixed absolute biomass threshold (real pre-cut AG biomass across the four cuts is 2.24/1.27/2.25/2.10 Mg/ha, none near the 4.0 upper threshold); a fixed thermal-time-since-last-cut threshold (real Cycles' own thermal-time column resets at each cut, but the accumulated amount between resets varies 442-802 degree-days, not a fixed value consistent with 60% of the 1500 maturity target); a growth-rate-plateau trigger (daily aboveground biomass climbs steadily right up to the cut day every time, no slowdown); and the crop file's own `STANDING_RESIDUE_AT_HARVEST=50%` read literally as the post-cut carryover fraction (real numbers don't support it). What the data does support: comparing real pre-cut aboveground biomass (from harvest.txt) against the very next value in the daily crop file (already post-cut) gives a strikingly consistent ~25% carryover ratio in 3 of the 4 real cuts — for both biomass and thermal time simultaneously, suggesting cutting resets the plant's physical size and its phenological "age" together, not just one or the other. The harvest.txt `AG RESIDUE` column, which an earlier pass mistook for this carryover, actually nets out to ~5% of aboveground biomass and turns out to be a completely separate "litter left on the ground" accounting (FORAGE YIELD + AG RESIDUE already sum to 100% of aboveground biomass in every real record, leaving no room for a third "still standing and growing" pool in that column at all). One of the four cuts (1982-07-08, notably the one with by far the lowest pre-cut biomass) breaks the ~25% pattern in both measures at once, hinting a different rule may apply near the `CLIPPING_BIOMASS_THRESHOLD_LOWER=1` boundary. Implementing the corrected 25% reset (replacing the earlier 50% guess) on the existing dual-threshold trigger did not fix correlation (-0.276 → 0.156, still far from workable) and made the level bias worse (ratio 1.825 → 3.305), since a larger residual regrows faster and triggers more frequent cuts than reality shows. With all four mechanical hypotheses now ruled out by direct evidence, further progress on this item most likely needs the devs' input on the actual trigger logic (a much sharper, specific question now — see `QUESTIONS_FOR_DEVS.md` item 4) rather than more independent guessing; real annual yields vary meaningfully year to year (5.25-7.94 Mg/ha at this site) and this model's output still doesn't track that variation. Deliberately not committed to the repo in this state — it doesn't meet the bar every other validated piece in this file was held to, and documenting an honest, well-diagnosed failure here is worth more than a calibration factor that would hide it.

`agricultural/prototypes/engine-demo.html` — first browser/Pyodide wiring, proving the validated engine actually runs client-side (no server, matching this platform's architecture), not the classroom tool itself. Lets a visitor pick corn or soybean (the two crops that pass the classroom-workable bar) and a real weather year (1980-2016), then runs the actual validated engine (embedded as a Python string, loaded into Pyodide, cross-checked to produce identical output to the standalone Python harness before committing) against real NLDAS-2 weather for Rock Springs, PA (real public federal reanalysis data, embedded inline, not Cycles-generated output, so distributing it doesn't raise the same licensing question as the model itself or its output would). Also deliberately does not display real Cycles reference values alongside the model's output, for the same reason real Cycles output isn't bundled elsewhere in this unit.

Nitrogen knob (built this session): the fertilizer-rate slider decided earlier in this unit's planning ("nitrogen rate is kept as a student-facing knob via a simplified mineral-N balance... rather than the full six-pool soil C/N system") is now actually wired into `simulate_season()` (both `cycles_engine_validate.py` and the embedded copy in `engine-demo.html`, kept in sync) as an optional `n_rate_kg_ha` parameter -- `None` (the default when not passed) skips it entirely, byte-identical to the pre-nitrogen behavior, verified by re-running `run_validation.py`/`run_validation_rotation2.py` unchanged (corn 0.771, soybean 0.846, wheat 0.253, silage corn 0.501, all exactly as before). Two real, disclosed mechanisms, grounded in each crop's actual `GenericCrops.crop` values (corn: N_MAX_CONCENTRATION=0.055, N_DILUTION_SLOPE=0.4; soybean: 0.07, 0.4), not literature guesses:
- **N demand**: a standard critical-N-dilution-curve model (Justes et al. 1994 / Lemaire dilution theory -- a widely used simplification, not a Cycles invention), flat at N_MAX_CONCENTRATION below 1 Mg/ha biomass, declining as biomass^-dilution_slope above it. Caught a real bug before shipping: naively multiplying that whole-plant *average* concentration by each day's new growth overcounts (it double-counts already-accumulated tissue's N on every day), inflating total seasonal demand to ~668 kg N/ha for a ~30 Mg/ha corn crop -- verified wrong by checking against realistic total-uptake references. Fixed by using the actual *marginal* rate (the derivative of total plant N content with respect to biomass, which carries a `(1 - dilution_slope)` factor), confirmed correct by checking that an unconstrained run (`n_rate_kg_ha` very high) exactly reproduces the no-nitrogen-tracking baseline, and that a rate near the closed-form total demand causes partial, not total, stress.
- **Leaching**: reuses the water balance's own daily drainage output (`redistribute()`'s return value, previously discarded, now captured) in a simple "well-mixed reservoir" model -- nitrate washes out in proportion to how much water leaves the soil profile and how concentrated the remaining N pool is. A disclosed, defensible proxy, not a verified Cycles formula, same spirit as this file's existing soil-evaporation and `fwc` approximations.

Soybean (LEGUME=1 in the real crop file) is deliberately exempt from N stress regardless of the slider -- real soybeans fix atmospheric nitrogen via rhizobia symbiosis and aren't normally nitrogen-fertilized, so a correct model has the knob do nothing for them. This isn't a modeled nodulation submodel (undisclosed, not needed for a v1 knob); it's a direct, correct consequence of the same LEGUME flag already read from the crop file elsewhere, and it's a real, useful lesson in its own right, not just an edge case to handle. Verified: soybean yield is identical at any fertilizer rate from 0 to 650 kg N/ha.

One real calibration surprise worth flagging: because this simplified model assumes *all* crop nitrogen comes from the fertilizer knob (no background soil-supplied nitrogen from organic matter mineralization, which the full six-pool system would include), the point at which the knob stops mattering for corn is much higher than a real-world fertilizer recommendation (which also draws on soil-supplied N) -- checked empirically across all 37 years of Rock Springs weather, 650 kg N/ha is needed to be non-limiting in every year, not the ~180-220 kg N/ha a real corn fertilizer recommendation would suggest. The demo's slider defaults to 650 (matching the pre-nitrogen validated baseline exactly on load) and the UI copy explains why the non-limiting point reads high, rather than silently picking a lower default that would misrepresent the validated numbers.

Known open items, in rough priority order: the pasture rotation's perennial forage mechanism (above) is the single biggest remaining gap and doesn't meet the classroom-workable bar — four separate trigger hypotheses are now ruled out with real evidence (see the "Follow-up session" paragraph above and QUESTIONS_FOR_DEVS.md item 4), most likely now needs dev input rather than more independent guessing; winter wheat's and silage corn's weak correlation survived multiple real diagnostic attempts each and don't meet the bar either — now diagnosed as two distinct problems (silage corn's is a water-balance timing/redistribution-physics gap, wheat's is a stranger anomaly where real reported stress is far larger than real soil moisture depletion would explain — see the "Follow-up diagnostic session" paragraph above and QUESTIONS_FOR_DEVS.md items 6-7 for the full evidence trail and what's already been ruled out); the `fwc` runoff moisture-adjustment formula and the bare-soil/residue evaporation formula are both undisclosed and low-priority since they don't block the bar for corn/soybean; the real soil-water redistribution physics (Eq. 1-2) needs Saxton-Rawls secondary parameters (pore-size exponent, air-entry potential, saturated hydraulic conductivity) that this sandbox's network policy blocked every attempt to verify from a public source this session — not paywalled, just unreachable here, worth retrying if network access changes; the guided-investigation tabs haven't been started (engine-demo.html is a bare validated-engine demo, not a guided unit, same pattern as `engineering/prototypes/truss_stiffness_validate.py` before `truss-designer.html` existed). All of the undisclosed-formula items are tracked in `agricultural/prototypes/QUESTIONS_FOR_DEVS.md`, not repeated here — check that file before re-deriving any of them from scratch.

WASM-Cycles investigation (2026-09-14 session, prompted by Matt asking whether the real 64-bit binary could somehow be made to run given v86's dead end): found and used an actually-viable fourth path, `jart/blink` (a mature x86-64-linux emulator that, unlike v86, supports 64-bit guests) compiled to WebAssembly via the existing open-source `x86-64-playground` project. Got the real, unmodified Cycles v1.4.4 binary loading and executing inside a headless-Chromium test harness, but it initially crashed partway through the dynamic linker with a Blink-internal assertion failure (`Munmap(...)  EINVAL`). Root-caused via Blink's own syscall tracing (temporarily re-enabled at the source level, since the WASM build ships with it compiled out) to two real, disclosed-quality bugs in Blink's own memory teardown logic (`memorymalloc.c`): when a `MAP_FIXED` mmap overwrites part of an existing mapping, the code computed how much of the old mapping to release from the *new* request's size instead of the page actually being freed, and separately, the code that creates a mapping for the tail page of a file (shorter than 4096 bytes, matching real mmap's own page-rounding behavior) wasn't matched by the teardown code that later assumes every such mapping is a full page — real Linux tolerates both mismatches silently, Emscripten's own mmap/munmap emulation does not. Both are one-line-ish, well-evidenced fixes (in this session's local clone only, not upstream). After that fix, a second unrelated blocker appeared — this sandbox's own glibc (Ubuntu 24.04) hard-requires Intel CET, a CPU feature Blink doesn't emulate — worked around by side-loading an older Ubuntu 20.04 glibc build instead (Cycles only needs GLIBC_2.14, so any post-2011 build works; the specific one matters only for the CET note, not symbol versions). A third blocker, an artificial "kill the guest after ~100M instructions" safety cap baked into `x86-64-playground` for its original use case (assembling tiny programs live in a browser), was simply raised. After all three fixes, the real Cycles binary ran a full simulated year end to end and produced `harvest.txt` output matching native execution to every displayed decimal place. The blocker that's left is pure speed: roughly 80-120 seconds of wall-clock time per simulated year (trimming Cycles' own daily-output logging saves about 30% of that; stripping unrelated debug instrumentation saved nothing), because Blink's WASM build has no JIT (`DISABLE_JIT`) and re-enabling one isn't a config flag — WebAssembly's sandbox doesn't allow generating and executing new machine code at runtime the way a native JIT does, so a real fix would mean rewriting Blink's JIT backend to emit WASM instead of x86-64, a different, much larger project. Matt's call, given a 37-year validation run would take roughly an hour: not worth building a classroom feature on top of, at least not in its current unoptimized form. Being kept instead as a concrete demo for the Armen conversation ("we already got your real binary running client-side with no backend") rather than pursued further right now. If this is ever revisited, the fixed `memorymalloc.c` and `blinkenlib.c` only exist in this session's `/tmp` scratch clones of `x86-64-playground`/`jart/blink`, not committed anywhere — re-derive or re-apply them (this file has enough detail to redo both in well under an hour) rather than assuming they're saved somewhere.

While regenerating reference output for this investigation, `/tmp/cycles-run/output/ContinuousCorn/harvest.txt` (a scratch-directory file, not part of this repo) got truncated/corrupted by an interrupted test run, and a from-scratch re-run of the real Cycles binary against the same committed input files consistently reproduces a *different* result (mean 10.56 Mg/ha, 0.540 correlation) than the one documented earlier in this file (mean 10.93, 0.771) — despite verified-identical binary, input files, and full determinism. This is the same unresolved mystery flagged in an earlier pass; still unresolved, still deprioritized (per Matt's direction to keep pushing on the WASM investigation instead), and worth real attention before quoting the 0.771/10.93 figures as freshly reproducible rather than historical record. Doesn't affect anything committed to this repo.

Also this session: `simulate_season()`'s new `record_history` option (see "Active build thread" above) powers a rebuilt `engine-demo.html` — no longer a bare single-result demo, now a working prototype of what the tool's interaction model could look like: a day-by-day season chart (canopy cover and water stress), a nitrogen-rate comparison (five fertilizer rates run back to back and charted), and a one-click sweep across all 37 years of real weather, all computed client-side in seconds. Built at Matt's explicit request for something to show Armen alongside the licensing/technical conversation, deliberately not gated on closing the wheat/silage-corn/pasture accuracy gaps first ("I don't care about the inaccuracy" for this purpose) — only corn and soybean are exposed, since those are the two that already clear the classroom-workable bar and the goal here is demonstrating the UI, not the science. Verified two ways given this sandbox can't reach the Pyodide CDN (a pre-existing, documented network restriction, same one that's blocked live-browser AI-feedback testing elsewhere in this project): extracted and ran the exact embedded Python against the exact embedded weather data with plain CPython (not Pyodide) to confirm the single-run, nitrogen-sweep, and 37-year-sweep code paths all produce correct numbers, and separately extracted and ran the exact embedded chart-drawing JS against synthetic data in headless Chromium to confirm rendering. One real bug caught this way before it shipped: `water_stress` is internally coded as a multiplier (1.0 = no stress, 0 = fully stressed, same convention as `n_stress` elsewhere in this engine) — charted directly, a "water stress" line would rise when things get *better*, backwards from what a reader would expect. Fixed by inverting only at chart-draw time, not touching the underlying validated field. The actual live Pyodide load-and-run still needs a real-browser check before relying on it, same caveat as this demo always carried.

A condensed, five-question version of `QUESTIONS_FOR_DEVS.md` was drafted for the Armen meeting, cut down from the full evidentiary write-up to what's actually sayable out loud (the `fwc` formula, bare-soil/residue evaporation, the pasture cutting trigger, wheat's water-stress anomaly, and the Saxton-Rawls secondary parameters for the real soil-water redistribution physics) — see chat history for the exact wording, not reproduced here since it's a distillation of what's already in that file, not new information. Business framing worth remembering: dropping the WASM-Cycles path means the Armen meeting is no longer "please bless what we already built" — it doesn't need his permission at all to keep building the from-scratch model, since that's independently derived from the published paper, not his code. What it's now about is asking specific technical questions that are actually blocking accuracy (the ones above), which needs no permission to ask or to use a plain factual answer to; separately, Matt's own N-cycle teaching idea (motivated by his prior work with Armen on cover crops and the Chesapeake Bay) is a content reason to have the meeting that has nothing to do with licensing at all.

Matt independently researched the Kemanian et al. 2024 paper's own stated intent for Cycles (from Penn State's own pages plus the paper's abstract/PURE listing, not the full PDF re-read by Claude) and found two things worth remembering: (1) the paper's own flagship demonstrated application is "Cycles-A," an autonomous crop-sequence builder run across the Chesapeake Bay watershed specifically to find double-cropping opportunities — the same watershed and nutrient-runoff problem as Matt's own prior work with Armen, which sharpens (not just supports) the N-cycle teaching angle: the classroom investigation could explicitly mirror the paper's own published headline use case rather than a generic conservation-of-matter framing; (2) the model's own stated philosophy ("a dynamic knowledge repository," meant to be "unplugged and coupled," "reused and tested against data") is in real tension with its actual CC BY-NC-ND license, which blocks exactly that kind of reuse — worth raising with Armen directly as a specific point, not just asking for permission in the abstract. Matt also sharpened his own read on the licensing conflict: "free to end users" does not resolve the NC restriction, since epiSTEMic itself is a for-profit, acquisition-track company regardless of what any given classroom is charged — don't let "it's a free resource" stand in as a resolution of the licensing question in future conversations.

New demo direction (in progress, not yet built): Matt wants a second demo recreating a more realistic use case than single-crop/single-year runs — a corn/soybean rotation with a winter cover crop between them, evaluated on outcomes beyond biomass, specifically nitrogen leaching (relevant to the anaerobic-digester/Chesapeake-Bay framing), with biomass still tracked since it matters for the digester context. Two versions were scoped: a "correct" one (three growth phases chained with real, continuous soil-state carryover across crop transitions, calendar-driven cover-crop termination) and a "cheap" one (three independent single-season runs, same soil-reset-to-field-capacity simplification the engine already uses today, stitched together for narrative purposes only). Matt chose the cheap version first.

Built so far toward the cheap version: `simulate_season()` gained a second additive option, `n_leached_kg_ha` in the returned dict whenever nitrogen tracking is active (i.e. `n_rate_kg_ha` is set and the crop isn't a legume) — accumulates the leaching loss already being computed internally (via `redistribute()`'s drainage output) but previously discarded, rather than adding a new mechanism. Purely additive like `record_history`: verified `run_validation.py` reproduces the exact same numbers as before (37 years, mean 10.96, correlation 0.540 against the currently-available reference data — see the still-unresolved reference-data mystery noted above), and a real-weather spot check (Rock Springs 2012, corn) shows sensible behavior — 14 kg N/ha leached at a limiting 100 kg N/ha rate vs. 104 kg N/ha at the non-limiting 650 rate. Done in both `cycles_engine_validate.py` and the embedded copy in `engine-demo.html`, kept in sync as usual.

Weather-data blocker (resolved, Matt chose full-year extension): `engine-demo.html`'s embedded weather data originally only covered DOY 90-320 (roughly early April through mid-November), trimmed to just the corn/soybean growing-season window since that's all the single-crop demo ever needed — too narrow for a real winter cover crop, whose whole point is holding nitrogen through the months that window excluded. Re-extracted the full 366-day record for all 37 years (1980-2016) directly from the real source file already used elsewhere in this project (`/tmp/cycles-run/input/RockSprings.weather`, not committed to this repo), matching the existing embedded data's own per-field rounding convention exactly (verified byte-for-byte against all 8,547 previously-embedded rows before swapping it in: pp/solar/wind rounded to 2 decimals, tx/tn/rhx/rhn to 1). Grew the embedded blob from about 398KB to about 603KB. Verified non-disruptive: the 2012 corn result and `run_validation.py`'s numbers are unchanged after the swap.

The corn/cover-crop/soybean rotation demo (the cheap version, as decided) is now built as a third panel in `engine-demo.html`, "Simulate a rotation." Three independent single-season runs, stitched together for narrative purposes only (soil still resets to field capacity at the start of each phase, the known simplification of the cheap version, stated plainly in the panel's own copy): corn in year Y with its own nitrogen slider; a winter cover crop planted at a fixed DOY 288 in the fall of year Y and terminated at a fixed DOY 100 the following spring (a new `build_cover_crop_rows()` glue function concatenating the tail of one embedded year with the head of the next, now possible because of the full-year data above) modeled with real winter wheat parameters (the only fall-planted, winter-surviving crop this engine has real data for — reused as-is from `run_validation_rotation2.py`'s already-vetted `WHEAT` dict, not re-derived, added to the embedded engine copy alongside `CORN`/`SOYBEAN`); then soybean in year Y+1. Since a cover crop's real purpose (scavenging a previous crop's leftover fertilizer) can't be shown without the state-carryover this version deliberately skips, "nitrogen available to the cover crop" is exposed as a direct student-set input standing in for that residual amount, not something the engine derives automatically — stated explicitly in the UI. The panel also runs a `bare_fallow_leaching()` counterfactual (new, small function: reuses `redistribute()`/`eto_fao56()`/`soil_evaporation()` for the same water balance but skips all crop-growth and uptake logic, since there's no crop) to show what the same available nitrogen would do sitting in bare soil over the same window — the actual point of the demo. A real test case (2012→2013, corn N=150, cover crop N=30) shows the cover crop cutting leaching roughly in half versus bare fallow (5.3 vs 10.5 kg N/ha), narrowing but not disappearing at higher available-N levels (15.6 vs 21.0 kg N/ha at N=60) — physically sensible, not tuned to produce that result. Verified the same way as the rest of this file's Pyodide-dependent features (this sandbox can't reach the Pyodide CDN): extracted and ran the exact embedded Python snippet the button handler sends, and separately confirmed the reused bar-chart function renders a two-bar comparison correctly.

Splash page and demo copy (2026-09-14, ahead of tomorrow's Armen meeting): `index.html` now links to `agricultural/prototypes/engine-demo.html` as a fourth unit card, "Crop & Nitrogen Cycle Modeling," badged "In Progress" like the engineering card — same pattern (routes straight to the sandbox tool, since no guided-investigation wrapper exists yet, matching how the engineering card worked before `investigation.html` existed). The card deliberately doesn't name CYCLES by brand, same convention the engineering card uses (task-named, not tool-named). Per Matt directly: `engine-demo.html`'s own copy was cut substantially, specifically removing all licensing commentary (the "independently implemented, not from CYCLES' code, see CLAUDE.md for the licensing rationale" framing, and the footer's CLAUDE.md pointer) — that's real, important context for this file's own internal record, not something to surface to the CYCLES scientists this demo is for. Kept, shortened: which crops are shown and why (corn/soybean validated, others still being refined), the nitrogen slider's no-background-soil-N simplification, and the rotation panel's no-real-state-carryover caveat — each now a single short line rather than a boxed multi-sentence note. If this page's copy is touched again, keep that balance: brief, honest caveats about what the model can't yet do are fine and expected, but don't let licensing/provenance argumentation creep back into a page meant for an external audience.

Nitrogen response curve fixed, economic tradeoff added (2026-09-14, same day, after Matt tried the live demo): Matt reported the rotation/N-sweep demo showed no diminishing returns at all -- yield just kept climbing until a hard wall at 650 kg N/ha -- and asked why, correctly guessing there should be diminishing returns in growth and an eventual runoff/cost tradeoff. Root cause, confirmed by direct testing: the day-by-day fertilizer pool was first-come-first-served -- once it hit exactly zero, `n_stress` locked at a permanent 0 for every remaining day (nothing ever refills it), so a higher N rate just delayed a mid-season collapse into a period of higher unconstrained growth, which is worth progressively MORE per added kg of N right up until the collapse was pushed past the end of the season entirely. That produces the opposite of diminishing returns (accelerating marginal yield, then a hard cliff to exactly zero marginal benefit) instead of a genuine concave response curve, and building an economic optimum on top of it would have been meaningless -- the "optimum" would just sit at the artificial cliff edge.

Fixed with a two-part change to `simulate_season()` (`cycles_engine_validate.py` and the embedded copy in `engine-demo.html`, kept in sync, both re-verified against `run_validation.py`/`run_validation_rotation2.py` after each step -- all four crops' correlations unchanged throughout, since none of this touches the `n_rate_kg_ha=None` code path):
1. A real, disclosed gap acknowledged since the nitrogen knob was first built ("no background soil-supplied nitrogen... the full six-pool system would include") is now actually modeled: `BACKGROUND_N_KG_HA_DAY = 0.5`, a flat per-active-day credit toward the fertilizer pool, in the middle of the real 60-120 kg N/ha-per-season range unfertilized ("check plot") corn typically draws from organic-matter mineralization. On its own this wasn't enough to fix the curve shape (confirmed by direct testing -- the accelerating-then-cliff pattern persisted for any fertilizer rate that still eventually ran the pool dry mid-season), but it's real and stayed in.
2. The actual structural fix: a new `_reference_n_demand()` helper deliberately duplicates the main loop's water/canopy physics (not a call to `simulate_season` itself, so it can be read and audited directly) with no nitrogen feedback at all, exploiting the fact that dGB_water_limited never depends on biomass or N status -- canopy cover is a function of thermal-time fraction alone, water-limited growth depends only on soil moisture. This precomputes the day-by-day nitrogen DEMAND of the fully unconstrained trajectory up front, letting `simulate_season()` apply nitrogen adequacy as a single WHOLE-SEASON fraction (total available N supply over total season demand) instead of a day-by-day pool that can hit a hard, uncorrectable zero. The fraction uses a quadratic-plateau shape (`f(x) = 2x - x^2` for `x` = supply/demand capped at 1) rather than a plain linear-plateau -- both are real, standard functional forms in the actual agronomic N-response literature (e.g. Cerrato & Blackmer 1990), but a straight linear-plateau has constant marginal return right up to a sharp corner, which would make the later economic-optimum calculation degenerate into a step function instead of a genuine interior maximum; the quadratic-plateau gives real, smoothly diminishing returns throughout. The day-by-day fertilizer pool is still tracked, but only to give leaching a real trajectory (surplus N left over after the season's rationed uptake washes out with drainage as before) -- it no longer drives growth stress directly. Verified: 2012 and 1993 corn now both show a smooth, genuinely concave yield-vs-N curve (e.g. 2012: marginal yield per 25 kg N/ha declining from +0.999 near N=25 down through +0.015 near N=400, then flat) instead of the old accelerating-then-cliff shape, with leaching still climbing steadily across the whole range as expected.
