A real second bug surfaced while re-testing the rotation demo with this fix, caught before shipping rather than after: `BACKGROUND_N_KG_HA_DAY` applied as a flat per-calendar-day rate (calibrated against a ~150-day corn/soybean summer growing season) badly overcorrects for the winter cover crop's much longer, mostly-dormant ~179-day window -- background alone (89.5 kg N/ha total) nearly matched the cover crop's entire season demand (82.8 kg N/ha) regardless of applied fertilizer, making the "nitrogen available to the cover crop" slider almost a no-op for growth, AND (since `bare_fallow_leaching()`, the counterfactual comparison function, had no background credit at all) made the cover crop appear to leach MORE than bare fallow at low N -- backwards from the whole point of the demo. Fixed two ways: background credit is now gated to biologically-active days only (`dGB_water_limited > 0`, i.e. not frozen/dormant -- a no-op for corn/soybean, which have zero dormant days in their tracked season, confirmed by re-running the corn N-sweep unchanged after this gate), and `bare_fallow_leaching()` now credits the same background mineralization (gated on above-freezing days, since there's no crop-growth signal available there) so the two sides of the comparison are apples-to-apples. Re-verified: cover crop now leaches LESS than bare fallow at every tested N level again (e.g. 2012->2013 at cover N=30: 15.97 vs. 20.75 kg N/ha), narrowing but not disappearing at higher N, matching the physically-sensible pattern already documented in the rotation-demo paragraph above.

Economic overlay added to the "How much does nitrogen matter?" panel in `engine-demo.html`, per Matt's explicit ask ("spending more money on fertilizer will not only pollute the bay, but it will cost more than it needs to"): two adjustable inputs (grain price $/Mg, default 190; fertilizer cost $/kg N, default 0.60, both labeled as rough illustrative figures, not a market quote), computed client-side with no new Pyodide calls (net revenue = grain value minus fertilizer cost, using the same N-sweep data already fetched). The N-sweep itself was widened from 5 points to 14 (0-650 step 50) for a smoother curve, and now also returns/charts nitrogen leached alongside yield. A new profit bar chart highlights the profit-maximizing rate in green among the rest in gray, with a text note naming the gap between that rate and the rate that actually first reaches peak yield (found as the first sample within 0.005 Mg/ha of the maximum, not just the last swept point -- an earlier version wrongly used the last sampled rate as "yield-maximizing," which overstated it whenever the curve plateaus well before the top of the sweep range). Verified computationally against the real embedded engine before writing any UI: at default prices, 2012 corn's profit peaks at 350 kg N/ha (net $1782/ha) even though yield doesn't fully plateau until 400 -- a genuine interior optimum, not a corner solution, confirming the quadratic-plateau shape does what it was built for. Also verified in headless Chromium (this sandbox still can't reach the Pyodide CDN, so the chart-drawing/profit-calc JS was extracted and driven directly with real precomputed sweep data, the same pattern used elsewhere in this project): the profit chart and highlighted-bar coloring render correctly, the note text is accurate at three different price/cost combinations (an interior optimum, a further-left optimum under high fertilizer cost, and the optimum coinciding with the yield-max point under near-zero fertilizer cost, which correctly triggers the alternate "no split" wording), and the soybean case correctly skips the whole panel (no profit calculation makes sense for a crop nitrogen doesn't affect).

Comparison figure regenerated at the fixed curve (2026-09-15, one-off deliverable, not committed to the repo, mirroring the earlier three-weighting-extremes cantilever figure's own send-as-PNG pattern): a standalone matplotlib script (`/tmp` scratch, not saved to this repo) re-ran the real `cycles_engine_validate.py` engine directly (not the JS port, not the browser) against real Rock Springs 2012 weather, at a finer 25 kg N/ha step than the live demo's 50 kg step, and rendered yield/leaching/profit as three small-multiple panels (one hue each, per this project's dataviz conventions -- no dual-axis, no rainbow). Confirms the same result as the live demo's own numbers: profit peaks at 350 kg N/ha ($1782/ha), genuinely below the rate needed to max out yield. That yield-max rate reads as 375 here vs. 400 in the live demo -- not a discrepancy, just grid resolution (the demo only samples every 50 kg N/ha, this script every 25, so it catches the plateau's actual start one sample earlier); the underlying curve and the profit-max point are identical. Sent directly to Matt as a PNG.

Year-range control added to the "years of real weather" sweep panel (2026-09-15, per Matt directly: "seems like there should be functionality to choose whatever years" rather than only single-year or the fixed full 37-year sweep): the panel's one button is now paired with From/To year dropdowns (1980-2016 each, defaulting to the full range) instead of a hardcoded `range(1980, 2017)`, and the embedded Python snippet takes `run_from_year`/`run_to_year` as Pyodide globals instead. Selecting a From past the current To (or a To before the current From) snaps the other end to match, rather than allowing a backwards range that would silently produce zero years. Button label and result note both reflect the actual span run (singular/plural handled, e.g. "1 year (2012-2012)" for a single-year selection). Verified in plain CPython that a 4-year range returns exactly those 4 years' results and that a single-year "range" (From=To=2012) reproduces the standalone single-season panel's own number exactly; verified the From/To clamp logic and option population (37 entries each) in headless Chromium with no JS errors. `run_validation.py` unaffected, as expected (this only touches the demo's own sweep glue, not the engine).

Multiple ag fields besides Rock Springs (requested 2026-09-15, genuinely blocked here, not a code task): confirmed by direct test that this sandbox's egress policy rejects even fully public, unauthenticated data sources for a new site -- both NASA POWER (weather) and USDA's Soil Data Access (gSSURGO soil) returned a 403 at the proxy level, the same category of block already documented for NASA Earthdata/Google Drive elsewhere in this file, not something to route around from here. Every site this unit uses (Rock Springs weather, CERES, ERA5) got into the repo the same way: Matt runs the Colab preprocessing pipeline himself (which has real internet access) and exports a small processed file for embedding. A second field needs the same treatment -- specifically: (1) real NLDAS-2 (or equivalent) daily weather for the new site's lat/lon, same fields/date range as `RockSprings.weather` (doy, pp, tx, tn, solar, rhx, rhn, wind), and (2) a real gSSURGO soil profile for that site, layered the same way as `SOIL_LAYERS_RAW` in `run_validation.py` (thickness, clay%, sand%, SOC% per layer) so `saxton_rawls()` can derive its hydraulic properties exactly as it already does for Rock Springs. Once Matt provides both for a specific second site, wiring it into `engine-demo.html` as a selectable field is a small, mechanical change (parametrize `ALL_WX`/`SOIL_LAYERS_RAW` by site key) -- deliberately not pre-built as an unused multi-site scaffold with only one real site to test it against.

Real "pick any field in the US" (Option 2 chosen 2026-09-15, superseding the curated-site-list option above for the long-term direction): Matt confirmed he wants literal KML/pin selection anywhere in the CONUS, not just a handful of curated sites, but also flagged wanting humidity data and couldn't find it in whatever NASA data-search interface he was browsing -- confirmed for him that "Atmospheric Water Vapor" is the correct GCMD Earth Science Keyword category (a real, standard NASA Earthdata/GES DISC taxonomy branch, distinct from Atmospheric Temperature/Radiation/Winds) that holds humidity variables, and that NLDAS-2 publishes specific humidity, not relative humidity directly -- `cycles-utils` (the same pipeline already used for Rock Springs) already handles that conversion to the rhx/rhn Cycles actually wants, so this isn't a new problem to solve.

Real math before any commitment, not a guess: a literal per-point CONUS bundle at NLDAS-2's native 1/8-degree resolution would be ~20GB raw for the full 37-year record (92,800 cells x 37yr x 365d x 8 fields x 2 bytes) -- completely incompatible with this platform's no-backend, bundle-everything-in-advance architecture. Coarsening to 1 degree still runs ~100-150MB gzipped, over git's 100MB single-file limit. Landed on a two-part design, deliberately simpler than meteorology's coarse+fine tiling: meteorology needs a coarse "always loaded" tier because it does genuine whole-globe scanning (trend maps, global time series); this feature only ever needs ONE point's data for ONE simulation, so there's no scanning task to justify a second, redundant coarse dataset -- fine tiles only. Chosen scheme: 5x5-degree tiles at 0.5-degree native resolution (~55km cells, real county-to-multi-region scale, an honest approximation of "your field," not literal field-scale precision -- disclosed the same way this unit discloses every other approximation), ~8.6MB gzipped per tile, ~58 tiles to cover the CONUS bounding box, ~500MB total across all of them, though a single field only ever needs to fetch 1 tile (occasionally up to ~4 at a tile-boundary edge). Going finer to 0.25 degrees would roughly quadruple total storage to ~2GB, in real tension with this repo's own already-documented size sensitivity (see "Repo size decision" near the top of this file) -- started at 0.5 degrees, revisit only if 0.5 genuinely proves too coarse in practice.

Hosting: Matt chose GitHub Releases over committing tiles to the repo directly (the CERES/ERA5 pattern) -- Releases allow much larger files than git's normal limits and avoid repeating the exact git-history-bloat mistake already documented in this file's "Repo size decision" and TECHNICAL-NOTES.md, at the cost of a new upload workflow for Matt (uploading release assets rather than committing via GitHub Desktop). Soil data (STATSGO2, the coarser nationally-summarized cousin of gSSURGO, built for exactly this regional-scale use rather than gSSURGO's field-scale precision) is expected to be small enough to bundle as a single file for the whole country, unlike weather -- not yet confirmed, since STATSGO2's actual export size depends on whether Matt's existing Colab/`cycles-utils` pipeline can even target it the way it already targets gSSURGO; that capability is unconfirmed and needs checking before he spends real time on the weather export.

What's built so far, all before any real tile data exists (deliberately, so the format is proven before Matt invests hours in a Colab export): `agricultural/prototypes/field_selector.js` -- `parseKML()` (handles both a Polygon placemark and a plain Point/pin-drop placemark), `polygonCentroid()` (a real area-weighted shoelace-formula centroid, not a naive vertex average -- verified against a hand-computed L-shaped test polygon where the two methods give genuinely different answers, confirming this isn't accidentally equivalent to the simpler wrong approach), `tileForPoint()` (floor-aligned to absolute integer-degree lines, no CONUS-specific origin offset needed since CONUS lat/lon never wrap the way global antimeridian handling does), and `loadManifest()`/`loadTile()`/`resolveFieldTile()` (fetch + `DecompressionStream("gzip")`, the exact same pattern `energy-balance-sandbox.html` already uses for its CERES/ERA5 fine tiles, just pointed at a configurable base URL instead of a repo-relative path). `TILE_MANIFEST_SHAPE` in that file documents the exact manifest.json contract Matt's export needs to produce (tile_deg, resolution_deg, years, fields in `RockSprings.weather`'s own order, a per-field int16 quantization scale mirroring ERA5's per-variable-scale approach, and a tiles map keyed by `"{lat0}_{lon0}"`). Verified in headless Chromium with a real cross-implementation round-trip (Node's zlib compresses a synthetic tile, the browser's native `DecompressionStream` decompresses it, no shared library) that the full parse-to-fetch pipeline resolves the correct tile for known coordinates (Rock Springs correctly resolves to tile `40_-80`) and correctly returns null rather than crashing for a point outside tiled coverage.

A working preview panel ("Preview: pick a field anywhere in the US (KML)") is now live in `engine-demo.html` itself, ahead of any real weather data -- lets Matt upload a real KML file exported from Google Earth today and see the actual computed centroid and which tile key it would need, so the KML round-trip is validated against his own real files before he spends Colab time on the export. Verified end-to-end in headless Chromium against the real page (a real KML file upload correctly computes and displays centroid `42.0400, -93.6100` and tile `40_-95` for a known test rectangle; a malformed file shows a graceful error instead of crashing). Not yet connected to any simulation -- this only proves the client-side geometry/tiling math, nothing more.

STATSGO2 reachability confirmed (2026-09-15): `cycles_utils` itself has no STATSGO2 support (a fresh Colab runtime without it installed surfaced that first, unrelated to the actual question) -- but direct queries against USDA's public Soil Data Access (SDA) tabular endpoint work fine from Colab (this sandbox's own attempt at the same endpoint got a 403 at the proxy, the same network restriction already documented elsewhere in this file; Colab has real, unrestricted internet access). Real debugging round trip worth recording since it corrects a schema assumption: the first query (`WHERE areasymbol = 'US'` directly on `mapunit`) failed with "Invalid column name 'areasymbol'" -- `areasymbol` actually lives on the `legend` table, not `mapunit`, which only carries a foreign key (`lkey`) into it. Corrected query (`mapunit` joined to `legend` on `lkey`, filtered `legend.areasymbol = 'US'`, which is STATSGO2's real national-coverage area symbol) returned real rows (HTTP 200, actual STATSGO2 map units like "Zanesville-Vandalia-Guernsey-Berks (s6149)"). This confirms a custom SDA export script is the path forward, independent of whatever `cycles_utils` does or doesn't wrap -- not blocked, just needs writing.

Correction to the cell-count figure used above and earlier in this section: "~1450 CONUS grid cells" was wrong -- that number came from an abandoned 1-degree-resolution calculation, carried forward by mistake after the real decision (0.5-degree resolution) was made. The real count is ~5800-6100 candidate cells nationally at 0.5 degrees (confirmed directly: 6136 candidate cell centers over the CONUS bounding box, 3280 actually inside STATSGO2's real CONUS coverage, the rest correctly falling on ocean/Great Lakes/Canada-Mexico slivers within the rectangular bounding box). This does NOT change the tiling architecture or the ~500MB total / ~8.6MB-per-tile size estimates, both of which were computed per-tile (100 cells/tile at 5-degree tiles and 0.5-degree resolution) rather than from the wrong total -- only the "total cell count" figure itself was mis-stated.

STATSGO2 soil export completed end to end for a real single point, then scaled to the full CONUS grid (2026-09-15): the single-point test (Rock Springs' own coordinates) correctly resolved to real STATSGO2 mapunit `670947` (musym `s6558`), whose dominant component by comppct_r is "Hazleton." Its chorizon layers gave clay 10-12.5%, sand 45.7-67.9%, SOC 0.145-1.74% across 3 layers to 1.42m -- a real, informative divergence from Rock Springs' existing SSURGO-based `SOIL_LAYERS_RAW` (clay 21-55%, sand 4-21%, SOC 0.17-1.74% across 9 layers to 1.4m), explained by real soil science rather than a bug: Hazleton is a documented sandstone-derived ridgetop soil series in PA's Ridge-and-Valley province, while Rock Springs' actual SSURGO-mapped field sits in a valley with finer-textured shale/limestone-derived soil -- exactly the kind of scale-mismatch tradeoff already disclosed for this feature, now seen concretely for the first time. The top-layer SOC values matching almost exactly (1.744 vs. real 1.74) was a good independent cross-check that the extraction pipeline itself is correct, distinct from the (expected) texture difference. Two real format surprises hit along the way, both resolved with evidence rather than guessing further: (1) the bundled `soildb_US_2003.mdb` turned out to be a schema-only template (real column names, zero data rows) -- NRCS's actual data lives in the accompanying headerless pipe-delimited `tabular/*.txt` files, but the `.mdb`'s column headers were still exactly what was needed to parse those files correctly, so nothing was wasted; (2) the zip's internal entry names used literal Windows-style backslashes as path separators, which `zipfile.extractall()` does not translate into real subdirectories on Linux, requiring a small extraction fix to normalize them before the nested `spatial`/`tabular` folder structure would actually exist. Scaled via a real `geopandas.sjoin()` spatial join (not a naive per-point loop) against the real `spatial/gsmsoilmu_a_us.shp` polygon layer (already in plain EPSG:4326, no reprojection needed) across all ~6136 candidate 0.5-degree grid cells: 3280 fell inside real STATSGO2 coverage, 2246 unique mapunits among them, 126 of those mapunits had no usable chorizon data (water/rock outcrop/urban land, a real and expected small fraction, not a bug) and were dropped, leaving 3044 grid cells with a complete real soil profile -- exported to `statsgo2_soil_grid.json`, sent back for embedding.

Soil side now fully wired end to end (2026-09-15): Matt sent the real `statsgo2_soil_grid.json` (869KB raw, a flat list of 3044 cells, each `{lat, lon, mukey, compname, layers: [{thick, clay, sand, soc}]}` -- exactly `SOIL_LAYERS_RAW`'s own shape, so no translation layer is needed beyond a lookup). Real data quirk confirmed and handled: 25.7% of horizon layers have a null `soc` (missing organic-matter measurement), but this is heavily concentrated in deeper layers (2.2% null at the topmost layer vs. 16-39% null below it) -- a genuine, expected SSURGO/STATSGO2 characteristic (topsoil OM gets measured far more often than subsoil OM does), not a data-quality problem to chase further. Minified and gzipped to `agricultural/prototypes/statsgo2_soil_grid.json.gz` (66KB, small enough to commit directly to the repo like CERES/ERA5's embedded data -- no GitHub Release needed for this piece, only the much larger weather tiles need that). `field_selector.js` gained `nearestSoilCell()` (linear nearest-neighbor scan over the 3044 points -- fine, this runs once per field selection, not a hot loop), `soilLayersForCell()` (fills any null `soc` with `DEFAULT_SUBSOIL_SOC_PCT = 0.2`, a disclosed stand-in chosen to sit in the same 0.17-0.27% range Rock Springs' own real deep layers already use elsewhere in this codebase, not derived from this dataset), `loadSoilGrid()`, and `resolveFieldSoil()` (the full KML-to-layers pipeline, mirroring `resolveFieldTile()`'s shape). The same logic is inlined into `engine-demo.html` and wired into the KML preview panel's own result table: it now shows the real nearest STATSGO2 series name, mukey, distance in degrees, and topsoil clay/sand/SOC for whatever field a student uploads, with a `distanceDeg > 0.75` check (grid spacing is 0.5 degrees) surfacing an explicit "likely ocean/Great Lake/border, don't trust this profile" note rather than silently handing back a distant, wrong soil. Verified in Node against real coordinates (Rock Springs correctly resolves to the same Hazleton/mukey 670947 cell already validated above; a mid-Pacific test point correctly comes back 16.6 degrees away, flagging out-of-coverage) and in headless Chromium served over a real local HTTP server (file:// blocks `fetch()` of the gzip, so this needed a real server, unlike the KML-only test from the previous round) -- both the in-coverage Iowa test rectangle (Canisteo series, mukey 667247) and the out-of-coverage ocean point render the correct table/note text.

Weather export completed and hosting decision reversed (2026-09-16): Matt found the real weather source wasn't a fresh NASA/GES DISC download at all -- it was a pre-built, per-grid-cell Cycles-ready weather database already sitting in the "Cycles global simulation database" Shared Drive (`weather/NLDAS_CONUS_1979-2024_3.6.7z`, 10.15GB compressed, 52,476 files at NLDAS-2's real native 0.125-degree resolution, one `.weather` file per cell in the exact same format as `RockSprings.weather`). Verified directly before trusting it: extracted Rock Springs' own native cell from the archive and it matched the real bundled `RockSprings.weather` sample to displayed precision, a real confirmation, not an assumption. Downsampled to the planned 0.5-degree/5x5-degree-tile scheme (every 4th native point), packed into int16-quantized, gzip-compressed tiles via a Colab script, producing 50 real tiles (some of the originally-estimated ~58 fell entirely outside real NLDAS land coverage, expected) totaling ~480MB gzipped, matching the original size estimate closely.

Real, disclosed imprecision worth knowing about: the manifest's per-tile `cells` list records the idealized 0.5-degree target grid coordinates, not the exact native NLDAS coordinates actually packed there (the real native grid is offset by 0.0625 degrees from clean lines) -- so a cell's recorded lat/lon can be off by up to ~0.09 degrees (~10km) from where its data truly originates. The weather values themselves are completely real; only the coordinate label is an approximation on top of the already-disclosed 0.5-degree/~55km-cell resolution tradeoff, not a new correctness problem.

Getting the tiles from Colab into a hosted, fetchable location took several real wrong turns worth remembering: (1) GitHub's website upload widget (both the ordinary file-upload page and, it turns out, a Release's own drag-and-drop box) caps every browser-based upload at 25MB regardless of context -- the previously-documented "2GB per Release asset" limit is real but only applies to uploads done via the API, not the website UI; (2) unzipping the tile bundle locally via macOS's Archive Utility silently auto-decompressed the nested `.gz` files and stripped the extension, the same class of OS-helpfully-touching-a-compressed-file mistake already documented in this file for the CERES upload, now recurring with a different dataset -- fixed by uploading the still-correctly-gzipped files directly from Colab via the GitHub REST API (`requests.post` to the release's `upload_url` with a personal access token) instead of routing them through a local machine at all; (3) the first API upload attempt failed with a 403 from an under-scoped token (a fine-grained token needs both the specific repo selected under Repository access AND Contents set to Read and write, easy to miss the second one) -- fixed by correcting the token's permissions, not by changing anything about the upload code itself.

Then a bigger, architecture-level problem surfaced only after all 50 tiles were correctly uploaded to a GitHub Release: confirmed directly with a real headless-Chromium browser (not curl, which doesn't enforce this) that GitHub's Release asset delivery (`objects.githubusercontent.com`) sends no `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` header at all, so a client-side `fetch()` from `engine-demo.html` running on `mmj125.github.io` is blocked outright by the browser's CORS policy -- a hard security boundary only the server side can opt into, not a workaround-able client quirk. This meant the entire "host tiles on a Release, fetch client-side" plan from earlier in this session could not work as designed, regardless of how correctly the tiles themselves were packaged; that architecture choice should have been checked before the upload effort, not after. Reversed to the same pattern every other large dataset in this project already uses successfully -- CERES, ERA5, and the STATSGO2 soil grid are all committed directly into the repo and served same-origin via GitHub Pages, which has no CORS issue at all. All 50 tiles plus `manifest.json` (459MB, downloaded back from the now-superseded `ag-weather-tiles-v2` release and verified byte-identical via a local re-decode of Rock Springs' own cell before committing) now live at `agricultural/prototypes/weather-tiles/`. This pushes the repo meaningfully closer to the "~1GB soft guidance" already flagged in this file's "Repo size decision" section (previously ~578MB, now roughly 1.05-1.15GB) -- a real, accepted tradeoff for this session, not yet revisited; worth keeping an eye on if more large datasets get added to this unit later (coarsening resolution further, or a Cloudflare-Worker CORS proxy in front of a Release, are the two live alternatives if it becomes a real problem).

`field_selector.js` gained the actual byte decoder (deliberately not written until real tile bytes existed, per this file's own earlier note): `decodeCellWeather()` reads a tile's raw bytes for one cell (cells in the manifest's own listed order, days in chronological order including real leap days, fields in `manifest.fields` order, little-endian int16, scaled per `manifest.scale`) into `{year: {doy: [field...]}}` -- exactly the shape `engine-demo.html`'s own embedded `ALL_WX` already uses, so a resolved field's weather is a drop-in substitute for one of `ALL_WX`'s years, no translation layer needed. Verified two ways: a synthetic round-trip test with known values (including a real leap-year boundary, confirming day-to-(year,doy) indexing and cell-offset math are both correct) and a live decode against the real committed tiles, confirming sane Rock-Springs-area winter weather comes out the other end (not an exact match to the original `RockSprings.weather` sample, expected and correct, since the nearest real 0.5-degree cell is a different, nearby point, not the exact original coordinate).

Feature completed end to end, including a two-field comparison (2026-09-16, per Matt's explicit ask): the old preview-only KML panel in `engine-demo.html` was replaced with "Pick a field and run a simulation" -- two independent field slots (A required, B optional), each with its own KML/pin upload, real STATSGO2 soil resolution, and real weather resolution against the now-committed `agricultural/prototypes/weather-tiles/` (same-origin, no CORS issue). A shared "Run" button runs the real validated corn model (fixed at 150 kg N/ha so any difference reflects real soil/location, not a different fertilizer input) for whichever slots are ready, at a student-adjustable weather year, and renders a side-by-side table plus three small-multiple bar charts (yield, aboveground biomass, nitrogen leached) using the same `drawBarChart()` helper already in this file. Deliberately does NOT mutate the shared global `CORN`/`SOIL_LAYERS_RAW` the Rock Springs panel above uses -- each field run builds a call-scoped `dict(CORN)` copy with its own `make_layers()` closure over that field's own soil, so running a field comparison can never leak into (or be leaked into by) the fixed-site panel regardless of click order, a real cross-contamination risk that would have been easy to miss.

Verified two ways, matching this file's own established split for anything Pyodide-dependent (a JS-only mock can't catch a real Python bug, learned the hard way in the engineering unit's history): (1) the actual embedded `ENGINE_SOURCE`/`DEMO_GLUE_SOURCE` Python, extracted verbatim, run in plain CPython against real decoded weather and real STATSGO2 soil for two genuinely different locations (Rock Springs, PA and central Iowa) at two different years -- confirms not just that it runs, but that the result is physically sensible and non-trivial: 2012 (a real Midwest drought year) hits Iowa harder than Rock Springs, while 1993 (wetter) flips the pattern, with nitrogen leaching tracking the wetter year in each case. (2) The JS wiring itself verified in headless Chromium with a mocked Pyodide: uploading Field A and B resolves each independently with correctly-differing soil and weather payloads (checked directly, not assumed), the run button's label and enabled state track which slots are ready, two distinct Python calls fire with two distinct sets of globals, the comparison table and charts render correctly, and uploading an out-of-coverage point (open ocean) to Field B correctly reverts the button to Field-A-only rather than silently including bad data.

Not yet done: `simulate_season()`'s existing nitrogen-rate/economic-overlay UI (built earlier this session for the fixed Rock Springs site) hasn't been extended to this comparison panel -- nitrogen is a fixed 150 kg N/ha here, deliberately, to isolate soil/location as the variable being compared; letting a student also vary nitrogen per-field would be a reasonable next step but wasn't asked for. The actual live Pyodide load-and-run in a real browser (not the mock) still needs a real-browser check before trusting it in front of anyone, same standing caveat as every other Pyodide-dependent feature in this file.

Restructuring conversation and six new engine mechanisms (2026-09-17): Matt flagged directly that the demo now "looks like it was built by Dr. Frankenstein" -- a fair diagnosis, since every panel in `engine-demo.html` was built in response to an isolated ask in a single sitting, not from a learning-progression design, unlike anthropology and meteorology, which both independently converged on the same real shape (build vocabulary, verify against something known, guided application, open investigation, argue a claim). Discussed backward design as the applicable framework and NGSS anchors (HS-LS2-7, HS-ESS3-4) for this content, then Matt redirected: the primary audience is intro undergrad plant/soil science, with a second, distinct planned surface for a middle-school teacher currently blocked from using real CYCLES by Colab being blocked at their school. Matt's own framing for the eventual investigation: three stakeholder perspectives sharing one model (a farmer maximizing profit, a Chesapeake-Bay-water-quality advocate minimizing runoff, a policymaker balancing both) -- a real, usable structure, flagged back that "minimize runoff" alone has a trivial answer (zero fertilizer) unless paired with a yield floor or a second lever like rotation, and that the real tool capability this needs is a joint profit-vs-leaching tradeoff frontier (already-computed sweep data, just never plotted against itself) rather than the two separate charts that exist today. Matt explicitly deferred the learning-progression/tab-sequencing question to focus on tool capability first.

That led to a direct question: what does the current engine actually take as input and return as output. Answered by reading the real code rather than the CLAUDE.md summary (weather, soil, crop selection among corn/soybean/wheat-as-cover-only, a single seasonal N rate, weather year(s), field location via the KML pip, a cover-crop-availability slider, economics; returns biomass/grain/leaching/planting day/optional history) -- and named what wasn't a lever at all: irrigation, split/timed nitrogen, planting date as a choice, tillage, soil amendments, cover crop species beyond wheat. Matt asked to add exactly those six, "as close to what CYCLES really works like without outkicking our coverage."

First pass (before the real paper was available) sorted the six by risk from memory/inference. Matt then supplied the real Kemanian et al. 2024 paper and its Supplemental Information, which changed several verdicts with real evidence instead of guesses:
- **Irrigation**: confirmed real and disclosed as operation-file-driven (Sec. 2.8: operations "can be set to a fixed date or be conditional to soil temperature, soil moisture, and crop phenology thresholds") -- no universal trigger formula exists to discover, because Cycles itself treats this as scenario-specific configuration, not a computed rule. Safe to build as a student-configurable trigger.
- **Nitrogen application timing**: doubly confirmed with two real, disclosed examples -- winter wheat fertilized 80/60 kg N/ha split fall/spring (Fig. 6), and the Iowa statewide run at 220 kg N/ha for maize with a **real 60 kg N/ha credit when maize follows soybean**, manured fields split 50/50 fall/spring (SI Sec. IX). The previous-crop credit is a genuine mechanism worth having, not something inferred.
- **Manure as an amendment**: better than expected -- the same Iowa case discloses manure nitrogen credited at exactly **0.5 the availability of mineral fertilizer**, a real, citable multiplier, not an invented discount.
- **Cover crop species**: SI Table SI.2 gives real, citable radiation- and transpiration-use efficiency for exactly the right candidates (C3 Grasses: barley/oats/ryegrass/wheat/winter rye, rue=1.7/wue=6.5; C3 Legumes: lentils/soybean/red clover/white clover/hairy vetch, rue=1.1/wue=4.5) -- but only 2 of the ~10 parameters a species needs; the rest (thermal time, temperature bounds, canopy shape, N-dilution) live in `GenericCrops.crop`, still not available this session (Matt's contact hasn't replied yet).
- **Tillage and manure's real mechanism**: confirmed genuinely disclosed at the conceptual level (Sec. 2.6: tillage mixes soil layers by an implement-specific coefficient and temporarily speeds organic-matter decomposition via a `fT >= 1` factor in the six-pool Cs/Cm saturation differential equations; SI Sec. V gives the actual decomposition/saturation equations, embedded as native Word equations the available tools couldn't render -- LibreOffice's docx import failed outright on this file, and OMML lives outside plain-text/table extraction, so `fwc` and the other open equation-shaped questions in `QUESTIONS_FOR_DEVS.md` remain unresolved, not for lack of trying this round). Confirmed this really is coupled to the full six-pool soil carbon/nitrogen system explicitly out of v1 scope, not just assumed to be. Lime/pH: absent from Cycles entirely, no pH state variable exists in either document -- not a disclosure gap, genuinely outside the model's scope.

Matt's call, given his contact's reply is pending: build now with clearly-flagged approximations where real data isn't available yet, tighten later -- explicitly the same build-now-disclose-the-gap-refine-later pattern already used throughout this engine (the `fwc` proxy, bare-soil evaporation, the subsoil SOC default), not a new standard. All six added to `simulate_season()` in both `cycles_engine_validate.py` and the embedded copy in `engine-demo.html`, kept in sync as usual, every new parameter defaulting to off/None so existing validated behavior is byte-identical when unused (verified directly: a 2012 corn run at n_rate=150 with no new parameters touched reproduces the exact pre-change result, 7.4348 Mg/ha grain, 27.4323 kg/ha leached, to full float precision, both before and after porting into the embedded copy):

- **Planting date** (`build_season_rows(..., plant_doy_override=None)`): a real choice now, not just a computed value -- when given, skips the soil-temperature trigger entirely. No new physics, just exposing an existing computed quantity.
- **Nitrogen application timing** (`n_applications=[(doy, amount), ...]`): replaces the single day-0 lump when given; the whole-season adequacy fraction (which actually drives yield) is computed from the correct season TOTAL either way, so timing changes leaching but not yield for the same total -- verified directly (a 140 kg/ha lump and an 80+60 split produce identical yield but different leaching, confirming the season-total logic is right and timing only affects the trajectory, as it should).
- **Previous-crop nitrogen credit** (`n_credit_kg_ha`) and **manure amendment** (`manure_n_kg_ha`, `manure_availability=0.5`): both add to the season's total nitrogen supply at day 0; verified 200 kg/ha of manure at the real 0.5 availability produces an identical result to 100 kg/ha of mineral fertilizer, exactly as the disclosed multiplier requires.
- **Irrigation** (`irrigation_trigger_frac`, `irrigation_amount_mm`): triggers off the same root-zone available-water fraction water stress is already computed from, adds water to that day's input before infiltration. No default trigger is set in the engine itself, since none is disclosed -- a caller has to choose one. Verified it measurably increases yield in a real drought year (2012) when enabled.
- **Tillage** (`tillage_doy`, `tillage_boost_days`, `tillage_boost_factor`): a temporary multiplier on `BACKGROUND_N_KG_HA_DAY`, explicitly NOT the real six-pool mechanism, standing in for its practical classroom-relevant effect. Real bug caught during verification, not a hypothetical: the first version only added the boost to the day-by-day pool, and yield came out completely unchanged, because yield is governed by the whole-season adequacy fraction computed *before* the day loop runs from a season-total that didn't yet know about the boost -- boosted nitrogen just leached out as unused surplus without ever reaching the plant. Fixed by computing the season-total background contribution with the boost correctly folded in up front. Re-verified: tillage now measurably raises yield at a nitrogen-limiting rate (50 kg N/ha: 5.141 -> 6.035 Mg/ha) and correctly has ~no effect at a non-limiting rate (650 kg N/ha: identical to four decimal places) -- the sanity check a real, working mechanism should pass.
- **Cover crop species** (`WINTER_RYE`, `HAIRY_VETCH` in the embedded engine copy): real RUE/WUE from the SI table, every other parameter borrowed from the closest already-validated crop as a disclosed placeholder (WHEAT for the grass, SOYBEAN for the legume's nitrogen-specific behavior) pending `GenericCrops.crop`. Real bug caught by testing, not assumed away: an initial version borrowed soybean's temperature response wholesale for hairy vetch, and a winter-planted cover crop using a warm-season crop's base temperature (5°C) barely grew at all (0.02 Mg/ha aboveground over a real fall-to-spring window), since soybean's thermal-time function returns ~zero through most of that cold window. Real hairy vetch is winter-hardy; fixed by borrowing WHEAT's cold-tolerant temperature parameters instead while keeping soybean's real legume RUE/WUE and nitrogen behavior -- corrected result, 0.518 Mg/ha, a physically plausible order of magnitude. Winter rye over the same real window (build via the existing `build_cover_crop_rows()`) gives 1.05 Mg/ha aboveground, also plausible.

Deliberately not done in this pass: no UI wiring for any of these six (no new sliders, no new panel controls) -- Matt's own Frankenstein observation two messages earlier is exactly why. Adding six more raw controls to the existing panels now would compound the problem being diagnosed, not fix it. The engine is now capable of all six mechanisms and verified; wiring them into a coherent interface should wait for the restructuring/tool-design conversation this session started, not happen ad hoc. `GenericCrops.crop` is still needed to move cover crop species and the borrowed placeholders from "disclosed and defensible" to "verified"; the exact `fwc`/tillage/decomposition equations are still sitting in the SI's native Word equations, unresolved by this session's tools -- worth a real attempt at parsing the raw OMML XML directly if those specific formulas become blocking, not attempted this round since it wasn't what was holding anything up.

Profit-vs-leaching frontier chart added (2026-09-17), the first concrete piece of the stakeholder-perspective idea above rather than more discussion of it: Matt asked what the proposed "frontier chart" actually was, then asked to wire it up before any further restructuring/UI work. Added to the "How much does nitrogen matter?" panel in `engine-demo.html`, directly below the existing profit bar chart, reusing the same nitrogen sweep data already computed there (no new Pyodide calls, no engine changes) -- a new `drawFrontierChart()` function plots profit ($/ha) against nitrogen leached (kg N/ha) as a connected scatter, one dot per swept nitrogen rate, in ascending-N order, with the profit-maximizing point highlighted in green (same color convention the existing profit bar chart already uses for its best bar) and labeled with its N rate. Copy explains the actual point directly: every point on the curve is already the most profit achievable at that leaching level, so a farmer (maximize profit, move up), a water-quality advocate (minimize leaching, move left), and a policymaker (pick a point on the curve) are arguing about where to land on one shared curve, not each entitled to an answer the others can ignore. Hidden by default and only shown/hidden alongside the existing profit chart (including the same soybean no-op path, since nitrogen doesn't affect soybean profit at all) so there's no new state to keep in sync. Verified in headless Chromium (this sandbox still can't reach the Pyodide CDN, same standing caveat as every other Pyodide-dependent feature in this file): extracted the real `drawFrontierChart`/`renderProfitChart` functions verbatim and drove them with a synthetic concave-yield/rising-leaching dataset shaped like real sweep output, confirming the chart actually draws (non-blank canvas, screenshot-checked for label collisions), the note text is arithmetically correct, and the soybean hide path correctly suppresses all five new elements (heading, intro paragraph, axis-label line, canvas, note) rather than just the canvas. Real engine numbers for this exact tradeoff were already computed and documented earlier in this file (2012 corn: profit peaks at 350 kg N/ha even though yield keeps climbing to 400) -- this chart is what makes that relationship visible directly rather than requiring a reader to cross-reference two separate bar charts by eye. UI/restructuring work continues next, per Matt's own sequencing ("wire that up first, then work on the UI").

Three-perspective UI built on top of the frontier chart (2026-09-17, same day, per Matt's "Start with the stakeholder framing"): the single profit-max dot became three marked positions on the same curve, plus a comparison table underneath, so a farmer, a water-quality advocate, and a policymaker are now genuinely distinguishable numbers on the same run rather than three names in a paragraph. `drawFrontierChart()`'s signature changed from a single `highlightIdx` to a `highlights` array of `{idx, color, label}` -- when two roles land on the exact same swept nitrogen rate (a real case, hit immediately in testing), their dots merge into one marker with a combined label ("Water advocate & Policymaker") instead of drawing on top of each other, and label position alternates above/below by draw order to avoid collisions when the three land close together. Definitions, chosen to directly close the gap flagged when the frontier chart idea was first proposed ("minimize runoff alone has a trivial answer, zero fertilizer, unless paired with a yield floor"):
- **Farmer** = the already-existing profit-maximizing point (`bestIdx`), unchanged.
- **Water-quality advocate** = minimum leaching among swept rates where yield is at least 80% of this run's own max yield -- a real, disclosed floor standing in for "a real advocate argues for less fertilizer, not none." If no sampled rate clears 80% (a real crop/site/year combination could fail this), falls back to the single highest-yield rate and the note says so explicitly rather than silently returning a misleading number.
- **Policymaker** = the point that best balances profit and leaching, each normalized to this sweep's own min/max and weighted equally, searched only across the range between the farmer's and the water advocate's own rates (inclusive) -- NOT a free global search. A free search was the first version, and testing caught it landing outside that range entirely (to the left of the water advocate's own position in one synthetic case), which would have read as a real inconsistency to a student ("the policymaker leaches less than the advocate but the advocate wants more nitrogen than the policymaker settles on") rather than the intended "compromise sits between the two extremes." Restricting the candidate set to `[min(farmerIdx, waterIdx), max(farmerIdx, waterIdx)]` guarantees the policymaker's pick is genuinely between the two named positions regardless of which one sits at higher nitrogen.

Verified in headless Chromium with the same extract-and-drive-with-synthetic-data pattern as the frontier chart itself: a normal concave-yield case (three genuinely distinct positions, one merged-label collision between the water advocate and a nearby policymaker point, screenshot-checked for legibility); a sharp-plateau/near-zero-fertilizer-cost case where all three collapse onto the same single point (confirmed the combined "Farmer & Water advocate & Policymaker" label and the correct "all three land on the same point" note text, not three overlapping unlabeled dots); and the soybean hide path (confirmed the new legend and table elements, not just the chart, are included in the suppression list -- an omission that would have left a stale table visible after switching from corn to soybean). The comparison table's swatch colors are the three roles' own canonical colors regardless of chart collisions (so a merged red dot on the chart still shows blue next to "Policymaker" in the table) -- deliberate, since the chart's combined text label already carries the identity in that case, and giving every role a stable, always-correct color in the table matters more than forcing table and chart to visually agree in a rare edge case.

Not yet done: the 0.8 yield-floor threshold is hardcoded, not a student/instructor-adjustable input -- deliberately, to avoid adding another slider to a panel Matt flagged as already overloaded; revisit if the fixed threshold proves wrong for a real classroom scenario. This only lives in the fixed Rock Springs "How much does nitrogen matter?" panel, not the field-comparison or rotation panels -- extending it there wasn't asked for and would need its own sweep data first. No role-selection affordance ("I am a farmer") exists -- all three positions render simultaneously by design, since the point is for students in different assigned roles to argue from one shared view, not to hide the other two perspectives from whoever is looking.

Four preset sites added to the "Pick a field and run a simulation" panel (2026-09-17, per Matt: "you need to select your site first no matter where... maybe there should be four clickable areas... if you don't want to pick your field"). Read closely, this is a statement about the eventual whole-tool architecture (site selection as a universal first step, ahead of whatever lesson content follows, with Rock Springs staying valid for some tabs per Matt's own "we can use Rock Springs in some of the lesson tabs") -- bigger than one panel. Scoped this pass to the one panel that already does site-picking (KML upload, real STATSGO2 soil, real NLDAS-2 weather) rather than rewiring the whole page: the "Run a season" / nitrogen-sweep-and-frontier / years-sweep / rotation panels above it stay on the embedded exact Rock Springs data, unchanged and unaffected. Flagged this scoping choice back rather than silently deciding it -- unifying every panel behind one shared site-selection step is the bigger version of this idea and hasn't been built.

Four real sites, chosen and verified before any UI was written (a direct Node check against the real committed `weather-tiles/manifest.json` and `statsgo2_soil_grid.json.gz`, not picked blind): Rock Springs PA (40.7093, -77.9478, Hazleton series, sandy Ridge-and-Valley ridgetop), Central Iowa (42.0, -93.6, Canisteo series, deep clayey prairie soil -- the same coordinate already used and validated in the earlier two-field comparison work), Western Kansas (37.97, -100.87, Manter series, sandy semi-arid High Plains), and the Maryland Eastern Shore (38.5, -75.8, Othello series, silty Chesapeake Bay coastal plain -- deliberately the one site that ties directly to this unit's own N-runoff/Bay narrative, real poultry-and-nutrient-runoff country). All four confirmed to resolve to real, in-coverage weather tiles and soil cells with real, genuinely distinct series (checked via a standalone Node script against the actual files before writing any HTML).

Implementation: refactored the existing KML-upload handler (`wireFieldSlot`) to extract its resolve-soil/resolve-weather/build-table/set-`fieldState` body into a shared `applyResolvedSite(slot, centroid, placemarkDesc)` function, then added a `wirePresetRow(slot)` that renders four buttons per field slot from a `PRESET_SITES` array and calls the same `applyResolvedSite()` with a fixed lat/lng instead of a parsed KML centroid -- no duplicated resolution logic between the KML path and the preset path, only the input source differs. Rock Springs is deliberately NOT special-cased to use the exact embedded `ALL_WX`/`SOIL_LAYERS_RAW` data the panels above use -- it resolves through the same nearest-tile/nearest-soil-cell approximation as the other three presets, so all four are on equal footing with each other inside this panel; the panel's own copy now states plainly that its Rock Springs preset won't exactly match the hand-validated numbers used elsewhere on the page, so a mismatch reads as expected, not as a bug.

Verified two ways, same split this file always uses for anything Pyodide-dependent: (1) JS wiring, headless Chromium served over a real local HTTP server (fetching the real committed gzip files, not mocked) -- confirmed 4 buttons render per slot with the right labels, clicking Central Iowa for Field A and Western Kansas for Field B resolves real, correctly-differing soil (Canisteo vs. Manter) and weather (matching the standalone Node check's numbers exactly), the run button's label tracks both slots becoming ready, and switching a slot to a different preset fully replaces its state rather than merging with the old one. A real timing lesson from this round: the first test pass used a fixed 800ms wait after each click and read blank tables/notes, which looked like a bug (and briefly like slot B not working at all) -- it was purely that the first real fetch of the manifest/tile/soil-grid data takes longer than 800ms end to end; polling for `fieldState[slot] !== null` instead of guessing a fixed delay resolved it, and this fixed-timeout trap is worth remembering for future Pyodide/fetch-dependent Playwright tests in this file. (2) The real embedded `ENGINE_SOURCE`/`DEMO_GLUE_SOURCE` Python, extracted verbatim and run in plain CPython against the real decoded Iowa and Kansas weather/soil for two different years (2012 drought, 1993 wetter): Iowa's deep prairie soil consistently outyields Kansas's sandy semi-arid soil in both years (5.11 vs 2.45 Mg/ha in 2012, 7.97 vs 5.37 in 1993), and Kansas shows near-zero nitrogen leaching in both years (0.0 and 0.2 kg N/ha) against Iowa's 1.9 and 35.4 -- physically sensible (a sandy, semi-arid, low-drainage profile doesn't flush nitrate the way a wetter prairie soil does), not tuned to produce that result.

Frontier/three-perspectives panel rewired to run against a chosen site (2026-09-17, same day, per Matt's direct "rewire the frontier panel to run against a chosen site"): the "How much does nitrogen matter?" panel (nitrogen sweep, profit chart, frontier chart, three-perspectives comparison, all in one card) previously always ran the selected crop/year against the embedded exact Rock Springs `ALL_WX`/`SOIL_LAYERS_RAW` data with no way to change that. It now has its own site picker (same four presets, same KML upload) feeding directly into the actual Pyodide call.

Rock Springs is deliberately special-cased here, unlike the field-comparison panel below it: selecting the Rock Springs preset (the default on load) keeps using the exact embedded validated data, not the nearest-tile/nearest-soil-cell approximation the other three presets and any uploaded KML go through. This is a real, disclosed asymmetry with the field-comparison panel's own choice (documented just above) to treat all four presets identically there -- the two panels have different jobs. The field-comparison panel exists to do a fair A/B comparison between two places, where using exact data for one preset and approximate data for the other three would make the comparison itself uneven. This panel's primary job is still "the validated Rock Springs demo," now extended to other places rather than replaced by them, so keeping its default path exactly as accurate as it's always been mattered more than internal consistency across its own four preset buttons. The panel's site-status line states plainly which mode is active and that non-Rock-Springs sites are approximate, same STATSGO2/NLDAS-2 lookup as the field-comparison panel.

Implementation: extracted a new pure `resolveSiteAt(centroid)` function (soil + weather lookup, no DOM or state side effects) that both the field-comparison panel's existing `applyResolvedSite()` and this panel's new site picker now call, rather than a third copy of the same STATSGO2/tile resolution logic. A new `nSweepSite` state object (`{kind: "rocksprings"}` or `{kind: "resolved", ...}`) drives both the status line and the actual Pyodide call: when `kind` is `"resolved"`, the embedded Python builds a call-scoped `dict(crop_obj)` with its own `make_layers()` closure over the resolved site's soil (the exact same pattern `runFieldSlot()` already used for the field-comparison panel) and reads weather from the resolved site's own `{year: {...}}` object instead of the global `ALL_WX`; when `kind` is `"rocksprings"` (the default), the Python is textually identical to the pre-rewire code path, not just numerically equivalent.

Verified three ways: (1) confirmed the embedded `ENGINE_SOURCE`/`DEMO_GLUE_SOURCE` template literals are byte-identical before and after this change (this rewire only touched the outer page's own JS glue, never the embedded engine strings) -- diffed the extracted source both before and after touching any code. (2) Ran the real default (Rock Springs) code path in plain CPython against the real embedded weather data and reproduced the exact previously-documented reference numbers to the dollar (2012 corn profit-maximizing at 350 kg N/ha, net $1782/ha) -- confirms the rewire didn't silently change the panel's own headline demo. (3) Ran the real resolved-site code path in plain CPython against real Central Iowa weather/soil (same site validated earlier in this session) and got a genuinely different, physically sensible concave curve -- profit peaks at 250 kg N/ha (not 350), at a lower absolute yield than Rock Springs, consistent with applying Rock-Springs-calibrated crop parameters to a different location's real weather and soil (an already-disclosed limitation of this whole feature, not new here). Separately verified the JS wiring itself with a lightweight mocked `pyodide` (capturing which globals get set and which code branch runs, rather than re-executing the physics, which the CPython checks above already covered): selecting Central Iowa sets `use_resolved_site=true` with `site_wx_json`/`site_soil_json` matching `nSweepSite`'s own data exactly and the Python string taking the `_site_make_layers` branch; switching back to Rock Springs sets `use_resolved_site=false` with neither site JSON global present at all, not just stale leftovers from the previous selection. One real mock-setup mistake caught immediately by the test failing loudly rather than silently: `window.pyodide = {...}` inside `page.evaluate` doesn't actually override the page's own `pyodide`, since it's declared `let pyodide` at top-level script scope, not attached to `window` -- fixed by assigning `pyodide = {...}` directly in the injected page context.

Not yet done: the year-sweep panel ("Multiple years of real weather, computed instantly") and the rotation panel both still run only against the embedded exact Rock Springs data, same as before this change -- Matt's ask was specifically the frontier panel, and extending site selection to those two would need the same treatment (a site picker, a resolved-site Python branch) repeated for each, not assumed to come along for free. The two-field comparison panel and the new frontier-panel site picker are two separate site-selection UIs on the same page now, each calling the same shared `resolveSiteAt()` -- not yet unified into one page-wide "pick your site once" step, which is the larger version of Matt's original framing ("you need to select your site first no matter where") still not built.

Two site pickers unified into one shared selection (2026-09-17, same day, per Matt's direct "unify the two site pickers into one shared selection"): the nitrogen-sweep/frontier panel's own picker (built earlier the same day) and the field-comparison panel's Field A picker were two separate, redundant UIs both feeding the same underlying `resolveSiteAt()`. Replaced with one new "Select your site" panel (four presets + KML upload, positioned near the top of the page, right after "Run a season") that both consumers now read from. Field B keeps its own independent picker, unchanged -- its whole job is being a deliberate second site to compare against, not a duplicate of the primary choice, so it staying separate isn't the redundancy Matt was pointing at.

The Rock-Springs-exact-vs-approximate asymmetry from earlier the same day forced a real design decision here, not a simple rename: the nitrogen-sweep panel wants an exact-data option for Rock Springs specifically (every number ever quoted for that panel came from it), while Field A needs every site including Rock Springs to resolve through the *same* approximate STATSGO2/NLDAS-2 lookup as Field B, or the A/B comparison stops being fair the moment "Rock Springs" is selected as the shared site. Resolved by keeping the exact-vs-approximate distinction as a property of *which panel consumes* the shared `currentSite` object, not of the site selection itself: `currentSite` always carries a real centroid (even in `kind: "rocksprings"` mode) plus a `kind` flag. The nitrogen-sweep panel's Python branch reads `currentSite.kind` directly (exact when `"rocksprings"`, same as before); Field A ignores that flag entirely and always calls `resolveSiteAt(currentSite.centroid)` (via the existing `applyResolvedSite()`, unchanged), regardless of what `currentSite.kind` says. Net effect, and now stated plainly in both panels' own copy: selecting "Rock Springs" as your site gives the nitrogen-sweep panel the exact hand-validated numbers, while Field A shows the approximate Hazleton-series numbers for the same nominal location -- a real, disclosed subtlety rather than a hidden inconsistency, and the honest answer to "should one site mean two different things to two different panels" (yes, when one panel's job requires internal fairness across two fields and the other's doesn't).

Implementation: renamed the nitrogen-panel-only `nSweepSite`/`setNSweepSite`/`describeNSweepSite`/`wireNSweepPresetRow` machinery to the shared `currentSite`/`setCurrentSite`/`describeCurrentSite`/`wireCurrentSitePresetRow`, now driving three DOM targets on every change: the shared panel's own status line, a short read-only mirror line inside the nitrogen-sweep panel (`nSweepSiteMirror` -- no picker of its own anymore, just a pointer to "chosen above"), and a new `syncFieldAFromCurrentSite()` that calls the existing `applyResolvedSite("A", currentSite.centroid, ...)` on every shared-site change and once on page load. Field A's own preset row and KML input (`presetRowA`, `kmlInputA`) were removed from the HTML entirely, along with the JS that wired them (`wireFieldSlot("A")`, `wirePresetRow("A")`) -- only Field B keeps that machinery now. `fieldRunBtn`'s disabled-state fallback text changed from "Upload Field A to enable" to "Resolving your selected site..." since Field A populates itself automatically now rather than waiting on a manual upload.

Verified in headless Chromium against the real committed data (weather-tiles/, statsgo2_soil_grid.json.gz) over a local server: on page load, Field A auto-resolves to the real Hazleton profile via the approximate path with no click needed, while `currentSite` itself starts in exact `"rocksprings"` mode -- confirming the intentional asymmetry works from the very first render, not just after an interaction. Clicking Central Iowa in the shared picker updates both `currentSite` and Field A together (both correctly show Canisteo/Iowa). Field B independently selecting Western Kansas leaves `currentSite` and Field A on Iowa, untouched -- confirming the two remaining pickers (shared, and Field B's own) are genuinely independent, not accidentally cross-wired. Switching the shared site back to Rock Springs correctly flips `currentSite.kind` to `"rocksprings"` while Field A stays on the approximate Hazleton lookup, not exact data -- the core design decision, directly verified rather than assumed. Re-ran the existing mocked-`pyodide` wiring check (renamed variable references only) and confirmed the nitrogen-sweep panel's Python branch selection is unaffected by the rename. A screenshot confirmed the page reads cleanly: one preset row for the shared site, one for Field B, no visual leftovers from the removed Field A picker.

Not yet done, same as flagged before this pass: "Run a season," the year-sweep panel, and the rotation panel are still Rock-Springs-only, not reading from `currentSite` -- this was a unification of the two pickers that already existed, not an expansion to new consumers. If those three panels get wired to `currentSite` later, each needs its own decision about which side of the exact-vs-approximate asymmetry it wants, the same way the nitrogen-sweep panel and Field A each needed one here -- don't assume one answer applies to all of them.

"Run a season" wired to the shared site (2026-09-17, same day, per Matt's direct "Wire 'Run a season' to the shared site too"): the very first panel on the page -- crop/year select, nitrogen slider, single-run season chart -- previously always ran against the embedded exact `ALL_WX`/`CORN`/`SOYBEAN` defaults with no site awareness at all. Now branches on `currentSite.kind` exactly like the nitrogen-sweep panel's own Pyodide call: exact embedded data when Rock Springs (the default), a call-scoped `dict(crop_obj)` with a `make_layers()` closure over the resolved site's soil otherwise. Decided to treat this panel the same as the nitrogen-sweep panel, not the same as Field A -- both "Run a season" and the nitrogen-sweep panel are single-site analysis, so keeping their Rock Springs numbers exact matters the way it doesn't for Field A, which specifically needs internal fairness against Field B. One real generalization this panel needed that the others didn't: it lets a student pick soybean as well as corn, so the resolved-site branch applies `make_layers` to whichever crop was actually selected (`dict(crop_obj)`, not hardcoded to `dict(CORN)` the way Field A's corn-only branch is) -- verified directly by running the resolved-site Python path for both corn and soybean against real Central Iowa data and confirming both produce sensible, non-crashing, non-identical results.

Since this panel now depends on `currentSite`, the "Select your site" panel was moved from after "Run a season" to before it -- the very first panel on the page now, matching Matt's original framing that site selection should come first, no matter where a student ends up. This is a pure reorder (no logic change to either panel), verified via Playwright reading the actual rendered panel-header order off the live page rather than assumed from the HTML edit. A new `runSeasonSiteMirror` status line was added to the "Run a season" panel (mirroring the nitrogen-sweep panel's own `nSweepSiteMirror`), and `describeCurrentSite()` now updates both mirror elements from one shared text string on every site change, rather than duplicating the mirror-text logic a second time.

Verified the same three ways as the nitrogen-sweep panel's own rewire: (1) confirmed `ENGINE_SOURCE`/`DEMO_GLUE_SOURCE` are still byte-identical after this change (diffed against the previous round's saved copies). (2) Ran the real default-path Python directly in CPython against the real embedded weather data (2012 corn @ 650 kg N/ha: grain 10.4987 Mg/ha) -- consistent with the already-validated N-sweep table's own N=650 plateau value for the same year, confirming the default path is unaffected. Ran the resolved-site branch directly against real Central Iowa data for both corn (5.819 Mg/ha) and soybean (2.267 Mg/ha) at the same site, confirming the crop-agnostic `make_layers` generalization actually works for both crops, not just corn. (3) Headless Chromium with a lightweight mocked `pyodide` confirmed the panel order (site picker now first), the mirror line's text in both states, and that `runBtn`'s click handler sets the correct globals and takes the correct code branch for both the default and a resolved site -- the same globals-and-branch-capture technique used for the nitrogen-sweep panel's own wiring check.

Remaining consumers still on Rock-Springs-only: the year-sweep panel and the rotation panel. Three of five site-consuming surfaces on this page (Run a season, the nitrogen-sweep/frontier panel, Field A) now read `currentSite`; two (year-sweep, rotation) don't yet.

Architecture conversation continued (2026-09-18): Matt sketched the eventual final tool -- pick a field, then set every condition (year range or single year, drought/rainy preset, crop, nitrogen, rotation), then a place to record outcomes -- and asked whether location/outcomes/perspective is the right set of things to vary. Pushed back on two points before building anything. First, "outcomes of interest" and "perspective" looked like the same axis restated (a farmer's outcome of interest just is profit) unless he meant something else. He clarified they're genuinely different: outcomes are the full vector of what a run produced, perspective is a rule for picking a preferred point out of that vector -- which actually generalizes the frontier chart's own mechanism beyond nitrogen specifically. Any single swept condition produces an outcome table; perspective is a lens over whichever table exists, nitrogen is just the one that's built, not a structural requirement. Second, "drought vs. rainy" as a condition only has real meaning at Rock Springs right now (2012/1993 are checked, real drought/wet years there) -- the other three preset sites have never had their own years ranked, so this needs a real per-site precipitation-ranking computation before it can be offered as a condition anywhere but Rock Springs, not just a label change on the existing year dropdown.

Second exchange: Matt argued a model needs complete, accurate inputs to replicate reality, and asked where that reasoning was off. Answer: it isn't wrong, it's answering a different question than interface design. The engine needing every real mechanism it can get is a completeness bar on the engine's internal state (exactly why the six mechanisms went in with zero UI already) -- it doesn't mean a student must actively choose all of them before a run counts. Forcing every condition to be a live, required choice both recreates the Frankenstein interface and breaks a student's ability to attribute an outcome to one cause if they changed five things at once. The resolution: every run is always fully specified under the hood (real defaults for everything, nothing silently missing), only the one or two conditions relevant to a given exercise are exposed as active; the full configuration, defaults included, belongs in the results log Matt asked for, so nothing is hidden even when not everything was a live choice. Also flagged a real ceiling independent of this: even a fully-specified run can't get CYCLES-accurate on pieces this engine doesn't have real formulas for yet (`fwc`, decomposition, tillage's real mechanism) -- complete inputs don't buy past that.

Matt's follow-up, agreeing with the sequencing bet ("we can set the UI prior to being accurate"): confirmed this is true for most open items, but split them into two real categories rather than treating "get better numbers later" as one uniform story. Pure internal-formula swaps (the `fwc` proxy, bare-soil evaporation, the real sub-daily water redistribution physics, wheat/silage-corn calibration) take the same inputs and produce the same shaped output regardless of accuracy -- genuinely zero UI impact when corrected. Cover crop species only stays zero-impact if the crop-picker UI is built to read from a registry rather than hardcode "corn"/"soybean" as special strings -- otherwise plugging in real `GenericCrops.crop` parameters later means hunting down every place that assumed only two crop names exist. Tillage is the one real exception flagged plainly: what's built isn't an inaccurate version of the real six-pool mechanism, it's a stand-in for a mechanism that doesn't exist in this engine at all, so getting real data means building a subsystem, not correcting a number. Injection fertilization (Matt: "some are also testing things like injection fertilization to limit volitization") was flagged the same way as tillage, not as an easy later fix: this engine has no ammonia volatilization loss pathway at all right now (only leaching), so "injected vs. broadcast" has no existing mechanism to modulate -- adding it means a new loss term first, and whether Cycles' own paper discloses a real volatilization formula (the way it disclosed the manure/leaching numbers) hasn't been checked yet. Not researched this session since Matt didn't ask for the build yet, only flagged.

Species registry built (2026-09-18), Matt's direct ask ("I want the dict of available species now"): `SPECIES_REGISTRY` added to `engine-demo.html`, one object covering every species this project has looked at all session, not just the two buttons currently live -- corn, soybean, wheat, winter rye, hairy vetch (all five have real Python crop dicts and are `implementedInDemo: true`), plus corn silage, orchardgrass, white clover, and Lotus corniculatus (`implementedInDemo: false`, no Python dict exists for any of these three pasture species or for silage corn in this interactive engine, only in the standalone validation harnesses). Each entry carries `dataStatus` (validated / partial / validated-not-workable / unresolved), real correlation numbers where they exist, `workableAsCash` gated on the classroom-workable bar, and a `notes` string stating plainly what's real vs. disclosed-placeholder vs. missing -- pulled directly from this file's own documented history for each species, not re-derived from memory. `pyKey` matches a key in a new Python-side `CROPS` dict added to the embedded `ENGINE_SOURCE` (`{"corn": CORN, "soybean": SOYBEAN, "wheat": WHEAT, "winter_rye": WINTER_RYE, "hairy_vetch": HAIRY_VETCH}`), and all three existing `crop_obj = CORN if run_crop == "corn" else SOYBEAN` call sites (Run a season, the nitrogen-sweep panel, the year-sweep panel) were refactored to `crop_obj = CROPS[run_crop]` -- this is the concrete payoff of the registry, not just documentation: adding a species later means adding one entry to `CROPS` and one to `SPECIES_REGISTRY`, not finding and editing every conditional. Verified this refactor is behavior-preserving, not just numerically close: confirmed `CROPS["corn"] is CORN` and `CROPS["soybean"] is SOYBEAN` (object identity, not copies) directly in CPython, and re-ran the real embedded engine through the new lookup path to reproduce the exact previously-verified reference number (2012 corn @ 650 kg N/ha, 10.4987 Mg/ha, unchanged). Verified `SPECIES_REGISTRY` itself loads correctly and has the expected shape for all 9 entries in a live browser.

Not yet done: no picker UI reads from `SPECIES_REGISTRY` yet -- the existing crop dropdowns (`cropSelect` in "Run a season") deliberately still only offer corn/soybean, since exposing wheat/winter rye/hairy vetch there would let a student run them outside their real fall-planted cover-crop context (nonsensical) or as an unvalidated cash-crop choice (misleading). The registry exists as the data layer a real conditions/rotation UI can build on next, not a UI change itself. Rotations (a sequence of species plus timing) are explicitly out of scope for this registry -- a separate structure, not built.

Why corn silage/orchardgrass/etc. aren't live, and what Cycles really supports (research, 2026-09-18, Matt asked directly before any more building): corrected an imprecise framing given in conversation -- corn silage (`CORN_SILAGE` in `run_validation_rotation2.py`) is genuinely a committed, real-parameter validation script, unlike orchardgrass/white clover/Lotus corniculatus, which exist in no committed script at all (that pasture work happened in an uncommitted scratch effort per this file's own "Deliberately not committed to the repo in this state" note above) -- not four equivalent cases. All four are blocked by diagnosed, unresolved *model-physics* gaps (wheat/silage-corn's weak correlation, the pasture cutting trigger), a different problem from winter rye/hairy vetch's genuine *missing-data* gap (real RUE/WUE exists, the other ~8 parameters don't, pending the devs). Confirmed directly (not from memory) that Cycles supports far more than these: `/tmp/cycles-run/input/GenericCrops.crop` (Matt's own local Cycles v1.4.4 sample files, same category as `RockSprings.weather` -- not committed to this repo) has 39 real named species entries with full real parameters, so adding a new crop to this engine is a data-availability question per species, not a structural limit of Cycles itself or of this engine's design (the `CROPS`/`SPECIES_REGISTRY` pattern just built exists specifically so a 40th entry is cheap).

Tillage and injection-fertilization research, same session: found real, disclosed data this file previously flagged as unavailable. `/tmp/cycles-run/input/till.txt` has 86 real named tillage implements, each with a real `DEPTH` (m) and `MIXING_EFFICIENCY` (0-0.8) -- exactly the "implement-specific coefficient" Kemanian et al. 2024 Sec. 2.6 references only in words, now with actual numbers. A real, useful surprise: Cycles represents fertilizer *placement* (injected vs. broadcast, Matt's own stated interest, "some are also testing things like injection fertilization to limit volitization") through this *same* tillage-implement mechanism, not a separate flag -- `Fert_applic_anhyd_knife_12_in` (mixing_efficiency=0.265919), `Fert_applic_deep_plcmt_hvy_shnk` (0.371806), and `Manure_injector` (0.447042) all mix soil on application, while `Fert_applic_surface_broadcast` and `Manure_spreader` both sit at 0 -- confirmed against a real operation file (`CornSilageSoyWheat.operation`) that only "Broadcast" appears as an actual `METHOD` value across the bundled sample rotations (`grep -h "^METHOD" *.operation | sort -u`), so no real injected example exists in the samples on hand, only the implement catalog's own data. What tillage/injection do NOT have, confirmed by two independent sources: a real briefing document already sitting in this container from an earlier session (`nitrogen_fate_questions.pdf`, prepared for the same upcoming devs meeting, footer states "not published on the public epiSTEMic site or committed to its repo") shows real Cycles `annualN.txt` output tracking a full nitrogen fate ledger every year (leaching, denitrification, NH3 volatilized, N2O emitted, N in harvest) but states plainly that neither the main paper nor the SI discloses the actual equations for nitrification, denitrification, ammonia volatilization, or nitrate leaching transport (only the mineralization/organic-matter side, SI Sec. V Eq. SI.10-14, is disclosed) -- independently corroborated this session by grepping the extracted SI text for "volatiliz" and finding zero matches. So injection fertilization's real point (less volatilization loss) has no formula to implement yet in either Cycles' own published sources or this engine; tillage's real soil-mixing effect does now (see below), but tillage's other real effect (temporarily faster organic-matter decomposition, the actual six-pool mechanism) remains exactly as undisclosed as ammonia volatilization is.

Recommendations given ahead of Matt's devs meeting next week: (1) near-term, ship the six mechanisms (and now tillage's real mixing effect) with their disclosed-placeholder pieces stated plainly in code/UI, not blocked on the meeting -- this is the same build-now-disclose-the-gap-refine-later pattern already used throughout this engine, not a new standard; (2) prepare for the meeting's answers by keeping every "we'll get better numbers eventually" item in whichever of the three categories it actually belongs to (pure formula swap / registry-dependent / genuine missing subsystem, see the exchange above), since only the middle category needed UI work done now to stay zero-impact later; (3) bring a short, specific, evidence-backed question list rather than a general "how does nitrogen work" ask -- the five-question list already condensed for the Armen meeting (see the WASM-Cycles investigation section above) plus `QUESTIONS_FOR_DEVS.md` cover the engine-accuracy gaps; nitrification/denitrification/volatilization equations (confirmed undisclosed above) and the real tillage decomposition-boost formula (SI Sec. V, still blocked on OMML extraction -- worth one real attempt before the meeting, not yet made) are two more specific, well-evidenced items worth adding to that list now that they're confirmed gaps rather than suspected ones; (4) do not build a rotation/operations registry yet -- wait until real tillage/fertilization operation data (timing, sequencing rules) is actually wired in, to avoid building the wrong shape twice.

Tillage soil-mixing wired into the engine (2026-09-18, same day, per Matt's direct "Start on the tillage soil-mixing wiring now" -- engine only, no UI, matching the pattern already used for the six mechanisms above): replaced the old arbitrary `tillage_boost_factor=2.0` parameter with real per-implement data. `TILLAGE_IMPLEMENTS`, a new 86-entry dict (`depth_m`, `mixing_efficiency`) parsed directly from the real `till.txt` discovered above, added to both `cycles_engine_validate.py` (canonical) and the embedded `ENGINE_SOURCE` copy in `engine-demo.html`. A new `mix_tilled_layers(layers, depth_m, mixing_efficiency)` function homogenizes soil moisture (not the C/N pools Cycles itself mixes with this coefficient, since this engine has no per-layer C/N pools to mix -- a disclosed reinterpretation of a real number for a different, but also real, physical effect of the same tillage pass) within the tilled depth, using the same fractional-layer-overlap handling already established in `root_zone_availability`/`extract_transpiration` (a tillage depth ending partway through a layer only mixes that fraction of it). Soil hydraulic properties (fc/pwp/sat) are left untouched -- blending those too would be a further, undisclosed extrapolation beyond what's defensible here.
