Verified three ways: (1) the full four-crop validation suite reproduces byte-identical correlations to three decimals (corn 0.546, soybean 0.857, wheat 0.223, silage corn 0.523) confirming the predicted null result at Rock Springs held; (2) a synthetic artificially-tiny cap (2mm/day) roughly halves corn's yield, confirming the mechanism actually does something when it binds, not silently inert; (3) the standing tillage sanity check (moldboard plow: real gain at a nitrogen-limiting rate, zero at a non-limiting rate) still passes. Added the real per-crop values (corn/silage corn 10, soybean/wheat 8) to both validation harnesses' crop dicts and both embedded `ENGINE_SOURCE` copies (`CORN_LONG_SEASON` inherits corn's value automatically via `dict(CORN)`; `WINTER_RYE`/`HAIRY_VETCH` deliberately left without a real value, same "disclosed placeholder, not real data" treatment those two species already carry for other parameters, since no real crop-file entry exists for either). Confirmed the ported engine reproduces the canonical script's exact reference number (2012 corn @ N=150: 8.031091501200546 Mg/ha) and stays byte-identical between both HTML files (44430 bytes each). Kept despite the null result at Rock Springs specifically because it's real, disclosed, cheap, and could plausibly matter at a hotter/drier site already used elsewhere in this project (Kansas) -- the same standard already applied to every other water-balance refinement today that moved little or nothing at this particular site's climate.

Cold damage investigated, ruled out as an implementation this round; `TTf50` refit from real data, kept (2026-09-23, continuing the "move onto the next fix" thread): checked what other real, disclosed `GenericCrops.crop` data this engine still wasn't using. Found `MIN_TEMPERATURE_FOR_COLD_DAMAGE`/`THRESHOLD_TEMPERATURE_FOR_COLD_DAMAGE`, dramatically differentiated by crop (corn/soybean threshold ~2-3 deg C; winter wheat -10 deg C, min -25 deg C) -- but the main paper's own text (Sec. 2.4) describes cold damage as "reducing [canopy interception] and [thermal-time fraction], rejuvenating the canopy," the exact same category of effect as haying/grazing/cutting, not a simple rate penalty. That's the same unresolved mechanism this file already spent real effort on for the pasture cutting trigger (QUESTIONS_FOR_DEVS.md item 4, four hypotheses ruled out) -- a real "reset," not a growth-rate multiplier, whose exact magnitude has never been recoverable from any source tried.

Checked triggering frequency before deciding whether to chase this further: corn's real threshold is crossed 246 days across the full 37-year Rock Springs record, wheat's 610 days across its real seasons -- not a rare edge case for either crop, unlike `TRANSPIRATION_MAX` earlier the same day. Searched general crop-modeling literature (not just Cycles' own sources) for a portable formula: confirmed linear/trapezoidal threshold-to-minimum response functions are the standard shape elsewhere (STICS, WOFOST), and found a real "Frost Damage Index" concept specifically for wheat canopy loss with a base temperature of -9 deg C -- strikingly close to Cycles' own -10 deg C wheat threshold, a real corroborating cross-check that the threshold itself is physically sensible. But no source (general literature included) gives an implementable formula for the actual rejuvenation magnitude. Logged as new item 8 in `QUESTIONS_FOR_DEVS.md` and item 14 in `model-validation.html`'s gap list rather than guessed at -- consistent with this project's standing discipline (this is the same call already made for the pasture cutting trigger and for tillage's real decomposition mechanism before a citable formula was found).

Pivoted to a genuinely tractable item instead: `TTf50` (item 3, the shoot/root partitioning half-max thermal-time fraction, previously just "0.5, the literal reading of the parameter's own name"). Real per-day Cycles output (already sitting in this container's `/tmp/cycles-run/output/`) turns out to make this directly derivable, not just guessable: the paper's own equation implies the day's *instantaneous* (marginal) shoot fraction of new growth equals `shoot_fraction(ttf)` exactly, since `dAG_biomass = dGrowth * shoot_fraction(ttf)` and `dTotal_biomass = dGrowth` -- so day-to-day differences in real AG BIOMASS/BIOMASS output are direct (ttf, marginal-fraction) measurements of the real curve, not an inference through aggregate yield. Pulled 5174 such points from four independent real daily-output files (two separate corn seasons -- ContinuousCorn and CornSoyWheatPasture's own corn phase -- plus soybean and wheat from CornSoyWheatPasture) and fit `TTf50` against each independently: every one converged tightly to 0.315-0.335 (corn 0.320 and 0.317, soybean 0.316, wheat 0.335), an ~11x reduction in sum-of-squared-error versus 0.5 across the pooled fit (0.3205, mean absolute error 0.113->0.025). This tight, cross-species agreement is a strong, direct measurement of a real quantity, categorically different from the reverted power-law water-stress fit earlier this session (which was fit through an inferred functional-form search, not a direct algebraic read of real output).

Implemented as `TTF50_SHOOT_PARTITION = 0.32` (rounded from the precise 0.3205 pooled fit). This shifts more of each day's new growth toward shoot earlier in the season for every crop, which meaningfully raises raw (uncalibrated) aboveground/total biomass -- so every validated crop's `calibration_factor` was re-derived to keep mean yield matching real output exactly, the same residual-scale-correction step this project has taken after every prior shoot/root or canopy fix (correlation is scale-invariant under it, so nothing is hidden by doing this). New values: corn 0.6913 (was 0.841), soybean 0.9643 (was 1.116), wheat 0.6241 (was 0.716), silage corn 0.6735 (was 0.806).

Honest, real result: wheat's correlation improved meaningfully (0.223 -> 0.276, the single largest movement from any water/canopy mechanism this entire session), while corn (0.546->0.544), soybean (0.857->0.846), and silage corn (0.523->0.510) each moved slightly down. A genuinely mixed result with the negative spread on the majority of crops, more like the reverted power-law fit's shape than the "3-up-1-down" pattern of most of today's other fixes -- but kept anyway, deliberately, because this is a direct real-data measurement (not an inferred shape), the improvement it gives wheat is the largest seen from any single change today, and the value it replaces was never anything but an untested placeholder. Re-verified the standing tillage sanity check still passes (real gain at a nitrogen-limiting rate, ~0 at a non-limiting one). Ported into both embedded `ENGINE_SOURCE` copies (`TTF50_SHOOT_PARTITION` plus the three crops' new `calibration_factor` values -- `CORN_LONG_SEASON` inherits corn's automatically via `dict(CORN)`; no silage-corn crop dict exists in the interactive engine to update), confirmed byte-identical to each other afterward (44528 bytes each) and reproducing the canonical script's exact reference number (2012 corn @ N=150: 7.707290265158505 Mg/ha, matched to full float precision).

`QUESTIONS_FOR_DEVS.md`'s item 3 moved to "Resolved without asking"; item 7 (the redistribution-physics question) rewritten to reflect that it's now largely resolved by earlier work this session (real Saxton-Rawls secondary parameters, the closed-form Eq. 1 derivation, substepping), not left stale claiming an unverified source; a new item 8 added for cold damage. `model-validation.html`'s gap list gained items 13 (TTf50, resolved) and 14 (cold damage, investigated/not implemented) -- and, since today's TTf50 fix directly and substantially changed the page's own "Beyond final yield" multi-variable comparison (aboveground biomass's error roughly tripled, from 11.3% to 30.3%, converging toward total biomass's own error rather than sitting meaningfully below it, since a lower TTf50 means less of the season's growth is ever attributed to root at all), that whole table and its surrounding prose were refreshed with freshly re-run numbers rather than left to drift, with a dedicated note explaining why aboveground biomass stopped looking better than total biomass. Verified via headless Chromium that both HTML files still load cleanly (panel count 13 for `engine-demo.html`, gap-list count correctly up to 14 for `model-validation.html`, only the standing CDN-unreachable console errors).

Real per-crop root depth wired in -- confirmed correct, a null result everywhere tested so far (2026-09-23, continuing the "move onto the next fix" thread): systematically cross-referenced `GenericCrops.crop`'s full field list against what this engine actually reads, since the last two fixes both came from that same exercise. Found `MAXIMUM_ROOTING_DEPTH` -- corn/wheat 2.0m, soybean 1.5m, silage corn 1.55m -- sitting completely unused: `root_max_m` had been a single hardcoded 1.4m shared by every crop across both validation harnesses and both embedded engines, with no per-crop override anywhere.

Wired in via `crop.get("root_max_m", root_max_m)` in both `simulate_season()` and `_reference_n_demand()`, right after `layers = crop["make_layers"]()` -- backward compatible, no call site anywhere needed to change. Ran the full validation suite expecting some movement given root depth directly affects how much soil moisture a crop can access (exactly the mechanism already implicated in the wheat/silage-corn timing investigations) -- got byte-for-byte the same correlations and means as before, to three decimals. Didn't stop at "no effect," traced the actual cause: this project's own hand-curated Rock Springs soil profile (`SOIL_LAYERS_RAW` in `run_validation.py`) sums to exactly 1.4m across its 9 layers -- almost certainly why 1.4m was chosen as the old shared default in the first place, since a crop can never draw water from soil layers the profile doesn't model regardless of how deep its own real root system could go. Checked whether this was a Rock-Springs-specific quirk: it isn't -- the real STATSGO2-resolved cell nearest Rock Springs (Hazleton series) is 1.42m, and even Iowa's own real Canisteo cell (a deep prairie soil already used elsewhere in this project) only reaches 1.52m. Every real soil profile this project currently has access to, for any site, caps out well short of any validated crop's real 1.5-2.0m root depth.

Confirmed the mechanism itself is correctly implemented, not just correctly inert, with a synthetic test: Iowa's own real 1.52m STATSGO2 profile (deeper than Rock Springs' 1.4m, so root_max_m=2.0 can actually reach further than 1.4m there) driven with Rock Springs' real 2012 drought-year weather -- root_max_m=2.0 measurably outyields root_max_m=1.4 (7.79 vs 7.66 Mg/ha, +0.14), confirming the code path genuinely does something once soil data deep enough to expose it exists. Re-verified the standing tillage sanity check still passes. Ported into both embedded `ENGINE_SOURCE` copies (the override plus corn/soybean/wheat's real values; `CORN_LONG_SEASON` inherits automatically, no silage-corn crop dict exists in the interactive engine to update), confirmed byte-identical to each other afterward (45118 bytes each) and reproducing the canonical script's own new reference number exactly (2012 corn @ N=150: 7.706756265781028 Mg/ha -- a tiny, expected floating-point-boundary shift from the pre-fix 7.707290265158505, since root_depth now lands exactly at vs. fractionally past the profile's own 1.4m edge depending on floating-point rounding of `root_max_m * min(1.0, ttf/0.5)`, not a real behavioral change).

Kept the real values despite the confirmed null result, same standard as `TRANSPIRATION_MAX` earlier the same day -- real, disclosed, correctly implemented, and would matter immediately at a genuinely deep-soil site or if either validation harness's own soil profile is ever extended below 1.4m with real texture data (which we don't currently have for Rock Springs). `QUESTIONS_FOR_DEVS.md` gained a new "Resolved without asking" entry (no open question here, since the mechanism works correctly -- it's a data-availability limitation, not an ambiguity); `model-validation.html`'s gap list gained item 15 with the same account, condensed. Verified via headless Chromium that both HTML files still load cleanly (panel count 13 for `engine-demo.html` unchanged, gap-list count correctly up to 15 for `model-validation.html`, only the standing CDN-unreachable console errors).

Real per-crop emergence delay wired in -- confirmed correct via real Cycles output, a tiny effect (2026-09-23, continuing the "move onto the next fix" thread, the third real-unused-parameter find in a row from the same systematic `GenericCrops.crop` field-list cross-reference): checked `THERMAL_TIME_TO_EMERGENCE` (corn/silage corn 65, soybean 70, wheat 100 degree-days), completely unused anywhere in this engine. Verified directly against real Cycles' own daily crop output before implementing anything (not assumed from the parameter's name alone): `CornRM.90.txt`'s STAGE column shows an explicit `PRE_EMERGENCE` phase with BIOMASS exactly 0.000000 from planting through thermal time 64.47, then 0.001000 the very next day at 69.997 -- a real, hard cutoff at essentially the crop file's own disclosed value (65), not a smoothly-ramping small number. This engine's own canopy-cover formula, by contrast, gives a small but nonzero value even at thermal time 0 (eie~0.0025 for corn) -- a genuine, if tiny, discrepancy from real Cycles' exact behavior.

Fixed by gating canopy interception (`eie`) to exactly 0.0 whenever `tt_cum < crop.get("tt_emergence", 0.0)`, at both call sites (`_reference_n_demand()` and `simulate_season()`'s main loop). This is a clean fix, not an approximation: both radiation-limited growth (`GR = rue*eie*solar`) and transpiration-limited growth (`TRp` contains a bare `eie` factor) are literally multiplied by `eie`, so zeroing it zeroes both growth pathways exactly, matching real Cycles' own exact-zero pre-emergence biomass precisely.

Tested before committing to keep it, same discipline as every fix today: ran the full four-crop validation suite and got no change to three decimals in any correlation (0.544/0.846/0.276/0.510-0.511, all unchanged) -- the pre-emergence growth window really is as small as the raw `eie~0.0025-0.005` values suggested it would be, on the order of 0.01-0.02 Mg/ha out of 10+ Mg/ha yields. Means shifted by about 0.01 Mg/ha at most, small enough that each crop's `calibration_factor` only needed a 4th-decimal-place nudge (corn 0.6913->0.6914, soybean 0.9643->0.9644, wheat 0.6241->0.6243, silage corn 0.6735->0.6736) to keep matching real output exactly. Re-verified the standing tillage sanity check still passes (real gain at a nitrogen-limiting rate, shrunk somewhat from the emergence gate's interaction with early-season growth timing but still clearly positive; ~0 at a non-limiting rate, unchanged).

Kept regardless of the negligible effect, the same standard applied to `TRANSPIRATION_MAX` and root depth earlier the same day -- real, disclosed, verified correct against actual Cycles output, and removes a genuine (if small) discrepancy. Ported into both embedded `ENGINE_SOURCE` copies (the gate plus each crop's new `tt_emergence`/`calibration_factor` values; `CORN_LONG_SEASON` inherits automatically), confirmed byte-identical to each other afterward (45722 bytes each) and reproducing the canonical script's own reference number exactly (2012 corn @ N=150: 7.55031486345136 Mg/ha, matched to full float precision). `QUESTIONS_FOR_DEVS.md` and `model-validation.html`'s gap list (item 16) both gained a new "resolved, tiny effect" entry documenting this. Verified via headless Chromium that both HTML files still load cleanly (panel count 13 for `engine-demo.html`, gap-list count correctly up to 16 for `model-validation.html`, only the standing CDN-unreachable console errors).

This closes out the systematic `GenericCrops.crop` field-list review started with `TRANSPIRATION_MAX` -- the remaining unused fields (`N_MAX_CONCENTRATION_GRAIN`/`STRAW`, `N_MIN_CONCENTRATION_GRAIN`/`STRAW`, `N_PARTITIONING_FACTOR`) belong to a more detailed grain/straw-split nitrogen model this engine deliberately doesn't implement (out of v1 scope, already documented), and `MAXIMUM_SOIL_COVERAGE` is confirmed always 100% across every crop checked (a real, verified no-op, not worth wiring in). No further easy wins expected from this specific list without expanding scope.

Wheat's water-stress anomaly was a column-misread, not a mystery -- and the real chase it kicked off found this session's biggest single fix (2026-09-23, same day, per Matt's "keep digging... you and I can recreate CYCLES to be close enough for classroom work"): re-checked the "wheat's real reported water stress is far larger than its real soil moisture would explain" anomaly documented in the "Follow-up diagnostic session" paragraph above and `QUESTIONS_FOR_DEVS.md`, and found the whole premise was wrong. Precisely re-parsed real Cycles' own `WinterWheat.txt` header and the exact flagged row (2012-03-16) field-by-field: `N STRESS = 59.5373`, `WATER STRESS = 0.0` -- the "59.5% water stress" this file and QUESTIONS_FOR_DEVS.md have quoted since 2026-09-10 was actually N stress, misattributed. Confirmed with a real cross-check: correlating each harvest year's yield against real Cycles' own max-per-season N stress gives -0.894, matching the previously-documented "-0.89" almost exactly; against max water stress it's only 0.359. Wheat's real driver is nitrogen, not water -- the entire prior diagnostic thread was chasing the wrong mechanism.

Found wheat's real fertilizer input in `/tmp/cycles-run/input/CornSilageSoyWheat.operation` (a real Cycles sample file, not committed to this repo): a single 90 kg N/ha UAN broadcast at DOY 75, the only fertilization event tied to wheat in the whole rotation (wheat follows soybean). Applying it (via the already-implemented `n_applications` parameter) made the validated wheat correlation WORSE, not better (0.276 -> -0.145) -- rather than fit a credit to paper over that, traced why: our engine's UNCONSTRAINED (n_rate=None) total biomass for wheat runs 1.3-2.1x too high vs. real Cycles across all 9 validated years (checked every one, not just an anecdote), while real total N content at maturity is remarkably tight (159-174 kg N/ha) across the same years. Feeding the constrained comparison the real N input isn't a fair test until the underlying biomass overestimate itself is understood -- so that became the actual target, matching Matt's own direct instruction to keep digging rather than patch around it.

Ruled out the leading hypothesis with a direct primary-source quote before touching any code: suspected a PAR-vs-total-solar-radiation unit mismatch (radiation-use-efficiency values are usually calibrated per unit PAR, ~45-50% of total solar radiation, and this engine multiplies RUE directly by total solar `St`). Checked Kemanian et al. 2024's own Eq. 4 text directly: `GR(eie,St) = eR*eie*St`, with `St` explicitly total incoming solar radiation and `eR` "based on solar radiation" per Table SI.2 -- this engine already matches the paper's own equation exactly. Not a units bug.

Ruled out canopy cover next, with a decisive substitution test rather than a plausibility check: fed the engine real Cycles' own exact day-by-day `FRAC INTERCEP` trajectory (from `CornRM.90.txt`, 1993) in place of its own computed canopy cover, holding everything else the same. Total biomass STILL ran 25-40% high all season (28.35 vs. real 22.69 by harvest) -- canopy shape wasn't the cause, even though canopy cover alone (checked separately) tracks real `FRAC INTERCEP` within about 4-5% at peak, consistent with the already-documented "~5% high at peak" finding.

Found the real cause by backing out a day's ACTUAL radiation-use efficiency directly from real Cycles' own daily BIOMASS output -- on any day with zero N/water stress and canopy cover in a clean 0.15-0.85 range, `dB[Mg/ha]*100 / (FRAC_INTERCEP * solar_MJ)` gives the implied g/MJ actually used that day, no back-solving through yield needed. Across 658 such days in the full 29-year `ContinuousCorn` record, real Cycles' own implied RUE averages 1.626 g/MJ against the crop file's stated 2.2 -- a real, tight (~11% coefficient of variation), 29-year-reproducible ratio of 0.74, not noise. `GenericCrops.crop`'s own `RADIATION_USE_EFFICIENCY` field is explicitly labeled "Maximum eR" in the paper's own Table SI.2 heading -- neither source discloses what reduces it from that maximum to a day's actual value.

Binning the same implied-RUE-ratio data by each day's own temperature found a second, additive effect: a clear, strong relationship (pooled correlation 0.69) between the ratio and each crop's own EXISTING `transpiration_temp_factor` (`tr_min_t`/`tr_threshold_t`, already computed for water uptake, not radiation) -- much stronger than a thermal-time-style temperature factor (corr 0.53). Wheat's raw, temperature-unadjusted implied RUE (its cold fall-to-spring season makes this the crop most exposed to the effect) averages under 0.35 of nominal; dividing the cold-day data by `transpiration_temp_factor` brings it back up near the warm-day baseline (median 0.88 vs. 0.74), confirming the factor explains most, not quite all, of the cold-season reduction.

Tested both pieces separately before shipping either, exactly the kind of check this session's whole "keep digging" instruction was about: applying BOTH the flat ~0.74 warm-day baseline correction AND the temperature factor together made every crop's correlation WORSE despite fixing the mean (corn 0.549->0.535, soybean 0.857->0.805, wheat 0.216->0.258, silage corn 0.530->0.446). Applying ONLY the temperature factor (no flat baseline reduction at all) was a clear, unambiguous win: corn 0.547, soybean 0.858, wheat 0.397 (up from 0.276, the single largest correlation jump this project has seen from any one fix), silage corn 0.512 -- and the mean-level match improved dramatically for all four crops even before recalibrating (e.g. corn's mean landed at 10.54 vs. real 10.56 using the OLD calibration_factor). Implemented by reusing `transpiration_temp_factor` (already computed for `TRp`) as a second multiplier on `GR` in both `_reference_n_demand()` and `simulate_season()`'s main loop -- no new per-crop thresholds invented. Re-derived each crop's `calibration_factor` afterward (corn 0.6922, soybean 0.9669, wheat 0.6357, silage corn 0.6744) to keep means matching real output exactly, the same residual-scale-correction step this project always takes after a growth-rate fix. Re-verified the standing tillage sanity check still passes (real gain at a nitrogen-limiting rate, +0.53 Mg/ha at N=50; ~zero at N=650, +0.0025 Mg/ha) -- the new mechanism composes correctly with tillage's own.

The flat ~0.74 warm-day baseline gap remains real and measured (also checked for silage corn 0.754 and wheat 0.736, both matching corn's own cluster; soybean sits distinctly lower at 0.640, a real, consistent ~15-point step, not noise) but was deliberately NOT implemented -- it's genuinely open, not a simple constant the way the cold-season piece turned out to be (`QUESTIONS_FOR_DEVS.md` item 9, `model-validation.html` gap-list item 17). Checked honestly rather than declared a full win: re-running the "beyond final yield" multi-variable comparison for corn afterward showed the fix did NOT close, and in this one check even slightly widened, the gap in UNCALIBRATED total biomass (24.0 real vs. 31.5 model, 32% error, correlation actually down slightly from 0.608 to 0.550) -- a real, disclosed reminder that this fix improved the CALIBRATED, reported grain number substantially without fixing the underlying biomass physics it's built from, consistent with (not contradicting) this project's own standing finding that a good grain match can mask a worse upstream one.

Ported into both embedded `ENGINE_SOURCE` copies identically (confirmed byte-identical to each other afterward, 45818 bytes each, reproducing the canonical script's own reference numbers to full float precision) along with each crop's new `calibration_factor`. `model-validation.html`'s main validation table, its gap list (new item 17), and `engine-demo.html`'s `SPECIES_REGISTRY` notes for corn/soybean/wheat/corn_silage were all updated to the new numbers. Verified via headless Chromium that both HTML files still load cleanly (panel count 13 for `engine-demo.html`, gap-list count correctly up to 17 for `model-validation.html`, zero console errors beyond the standing CDN-unreachable ones). The actual live Pyodide load-and-run in a real browser still needs checking before trusting it in front of students, the same standing caveat as every Pyodide-dependent feature in this file.

Warm-day radiation-use-efficiency gap resolved as a post-limitation growth-conversion loss, not a discount on GR (2026-09-23, same day, per Matt's direct "keep digging on the flat warm-day RUE gap"): re-diagnosed the ~25% warm-day gap flagged as deliberately-not-implemented in the fix just above, and found the original measurement itself was partly an artifact. The implied-RUE calculation there assumed every sampled day was purely radiation-limited, but a real check found many weren't: binning the same implied-RUE-ratio data by this engine's own GT/GR ratio (computed with nominal, uncorrected RUE) showed the ratio rising smoothly and monotonically from 0.62 (GT/GR 0.6-0.9, water-limited-leaning) to 0.80 (GT/GR > 1.33, unambiguously radiation-limited) -- most, but not all, of the apparent ~25% gap was really water-limited days being misattributed to the radiation formula. Restricting to the cleanest, most unambiguous days (GT/GR > 1.5, n=23, stdev 0.039 -- tight and reproducible despite the small n) gives a real, residual gap of 0.785, smaller than the original ~0.74 but still real. Also ruled out a reporting-lag/differencing artifact directly: re-running the same implied-RUE calculation over 1/3/5/7-day windows instead of single days gave an essentially flat ratio (0.762-0.768) regardless of window size -- a lag artifact would have converged toward 1.0 as the window widened, so this confirmed the gap is real, not a measurement quirk.

Confirmed, with a direct synthetic test rather than just reasoning about it, exactly why the earlier fix was right to decline implementing this as a flat discount on GR at any magnitude: applying a flat 0.74 correction to GR before the min(GR, GT) choice made radiation the binding constraint on 81-85% of days in a synthetic corn test (three separate years), up from a real 38-46% baseline -- because a smaller GR is more often the smaller of the two terms. Since real year-to-year yield variation is overwhelmingly water-driven (precipitation varies far more than solar radiation does), making radiation almost always the limiting factor instead destroys the model's sensitivity to the actual real signal -- the precise mechanism behind the correlation damage documented earlier, not just an observed side effect.

The fix that works: apply the 0.785 fraction to `min(GR, GT)` (a new `NET_GROWTH_FRACTION` constant) AFTER the day's limiting choice is made, not to `GR` beforehand. The key insight making this safe: nothing else in this engine's day loop depends on cumulative biomass -- canopy cover and root depth are both pure functions of thermal time, not of accumulated biomass, and soil-moisture extraction depends only on realized transpiration, not on how much biomass that transpiration produced. Scaling the daily growth increment by a flat fraction after the min() choice is therefore mathematically equivalent to a uniform rescaling of the whole season's biomass trajectory -- it cannot change which of GR or GT wins on any given day, unlike scaling GR beforehand. Verified directly, not just derived: applying it reproduces every validated crop's grain correlation to three decimals, completely unchanged (corn 0.547, soybean 0.858, wheat 0.397, silage corn 0.512, all identical to the pre-fix values) -- only the mean moved, absorbed by re-deriving each crop's `calibration_factor` (corn 0.6922->0.8818, soybean 0.9669->1.2318, wheat 0.6357->0.8097, silage corn 0.6744->0.8591), the same residual-scale-correction step this project always takes after a pure level change.

This directly closes a real, previously-disclosed-but-unfixed gap: `model-validation.html`'s "Beyond final yield" multi-variable comparison has repeatedly flagged that this engine's UNCALIBRATED total/aboveground biomass runs 26-32% higher than real Cycles even when the CALIBRATED grain number matches well. Re-ran that exact comparison after this fix: corn's total biomass error dropped from 32.2% to 7.0% (correlation unchanged at 0.550, exactly as predicted since this is a pure rescale), aboveground biomass from 32.7% to 7.6% (correlation unchanged at 0.531). Re-verified the standing tillage sanity check still passes (real gain at a nitrogen-limiting rate, +0.573 Mg/ha at N=50; ~zero at N=650, +0.0025 Mg/ha) and the nitrogen mass-balance identity still closes exactly.

What this fix does NOT do: identify the real cause of the gap. `QUESTIONS_FOR_DEVS.md` item 9 and `model-validation.html`'s gap-list item 18 both keep the actual question open -- what real mechanism (a CO2-reference scaling? an undisclosed conversion?) turns GenericCrops.crop's own "Maximum eR" into a day's actual value, and does real Cycles apply it before or after the radiation/water co-limitation choice (the distinction that made this workaround succeed where the naive one failed). This is a verified fix to the model's output, correcting a real, honestly-disclosed discrepancy with a mathematically-guaranteed-safe method, not a claim to have found Cycles' own real formula.

Ported into both embedded `ENGINE_SOURCE` copies identically (confirmed byte-identical to each other afterward, 45991 bytes each, reproducing the canonical script's own reference numbers) along with each crop's new `calibration_factor`. `model-validation.html`'s "Beyond final yield" table and gap list (new item 18) and its own explanatory notes were updated to the new, dramatically-improved biomass numbers. Verified via headless Chromium that both HTML files still load cleanly (panel count 13 for `engine-demo.html`, zero console errors beyond the standing CDN-unreachable ones). The actual live Pyodide load-and-run in a real browser still needs checking before trusting it in front of students, the same standing caveat as every Pyodide-dependent feature in this file.

Wheat needed its own radiation-temperature response, separate from the shared NET_GROWTH_FRACTION -- a modest, real refinement (2026-09-24, per Matt's "move onto the next fix"): checked whether the shared 0.785 fraction actually held for all four crops before assuming it did. Ran the same "clean, GT/GR > 1.5" measurement used to derive it for soybean and silage corn: soybean 0.786 (n=5), silage corn 0.806 (n=19) -- both match corn's own 0.785 almost exactly, a real, reassuring cross-validation of the shared constant. Wheat did not match at all: n=117 clean days gave mean 0.471, median 0.502, stdev 0.256 -- roughly half the other three crops' value, and far noisier.

Traced why rather than just accepting a lower wheat-specific constant. Every one of wheat's 117 "clean" (unambiguously radiation-limited) days fell in November through April -- wheat's real fall-to-early-summer growing season never reaches the genuinely warm conditions corn/soybean/silage corn's own clean samples came from, since by the time real summer heat arrives wheat has already been harvested. Within that cold-season subset, the implied-RUE ratio still correlates strongly with temperature (corr 0.85) even after the existing cold-temperature fix (transpiration_temp_factor reused on GR, documented above) is already applied -- meaning that fix's own threshold (reused from wheat's transpiration parameters, 0-12°C) doesn't fully capture radiation's own real temperature response. A larger, cleaner sample (n=401, using the RAW implied ratio with no temp correction pre-applied) regresses linearly against daily mean temperature as `ratio = 0.202 + 0.0375*tmean`, reaching the shared 0.785 plateau at ~15.55°C, not at wheat's own 12°C transpiration threshold -- a real, distinct value that had never been independently validated for radiation, only reused from transpiration for convenience. The nonzero floor at 0°C (0.202, about a quarter of the 0.785 plateau) is real too: the existing formula predicts exactly zero growth at freezing, but real Cycles' own wheat output keeps growing at roughly 26% of its eventual rate even that cold.

Implemented as a second, separate temperature-response curve, `radiation_temp_factor(tmean, min_t, floor, plateau_t)`, applied only to radiation-limited growth (GR) -- transpiration keeps using its own existing, unchanged `transpiration_temp_factor` for GT. Defaults to reproducing the shared response exactly (floor=0, plateau=the crop's own transpiration threshold) when a crop doesn't set `rad_temp_floor`/`rad_temp_plateau_t`, making this a genuine no-op for corn/soybean/silage corn -- verified directly, not just by inspection: corn's reference number (10.42557318788082 Mg/ha at N=650) reproduces byte-for-byte unchanged, and the standing tillage sanity check still passes identically. Wheat's own real values (floor=0.257, plateau=15.55°C) gave a real, if modest, improvement: correlation 0.397 -> 0.399, and the total-biomass level bias (already substantially cut by the fix just above) improved further, 1.233x -> 1.204x of real output. Smaller than the cleanly-fit regression made it look like it should be -- worth being honest about that gap between how good a fit looked and how much it actually moved the numbers that matter -- but real, sourced, correctly verified, and kept for the same reason `TRANSPIRATION_MAX`/root depth/emergence delay were kept despite modest effects.

Re-derived wheat's `calibration_factor` (0.8097 -> 0.8290) to keep the mean matching real output exactly. Ported into both embedded `ENGINE_SOURCE` copies identically (confirmed byte-identical to each other afterward, 46868 bytes each). `model-validation.html`'s main table (wheat's correlation) and gap list (new item 19) and `QUESTIONS_FOR_DEVS.md` were updated to match. Verified via headless Chromium that both HTML files still load cleanly (panel count 13 for `engine-demo.html`, zero console errors beyond the standing CDN-unreachable ones).

Multi-site validation groundwork and a real engine feature to come out of it (2026-09-24): Matt asked what multi-site validation would actually buy given the model's Rock-Springs-only track record, correctly skeptical it would just confirm "still bad" at harder sites. Diagnosed the actual mechanism behind this engine's known overshoot at a semi-arid site (Western Kansas, real STATSGO2/NLDAS-2 data, corn N=150, real Cycles reference 0.137 Mg/ha for 2012): `root_zone_availability()` credits a soil layer at its full current moisture the instant `root_depth` reaches it, with zero regard for whether the root has had time to draw it down -- confirmed directly, day by day, that an apparent "recovery" around doy 128-145 was root growth discovering untouched, still-at-field-capacity subsoil, not rain (precip that week was 0.1-0.7mm). Two candidate fixes for this specific mechanism were tested and discarded: Borg & Grimes (1986)'s real root-growth-vs-time sigmoid (coefficients search-corroborated only, every primary-source host tried -- arxiv, researchgate, eurekamag, core.ac.uk -- blocked by this sandbox's network egress) regressed soybean at Rock Springs (0.858 -> 0.825) and didn't move Kansas; a new root-residence-time weighting mechanism had zero effect at Kansas even layered on working carryover. Full evidence trail for both in `QUESTIONS_FOR_DEVS.md` item 6.

A real bug in this session's own Kansas test harness (not the engine) was caught while building the residence-time test: `simulate_season()` calls `make_layers()` twice internally when nitrogen tracking is active (once for `_reference_n_demand()`'s independent precompute pass, once for the real main loop), and a shared-object trick let those two calls corrupt each other within a single call. This produced a wrong fresh-start baseline (5.39x over real, looking like real progress) and a wrong "carryover does nothing" conclusion, both reported to Matt before being caught and corrected. The real numbers: fresh-start baseline is 18.44x over real Cycles (matching the pre-session finding almost exactly -- none of this session's water-balance work has actually touched Kansas), and a real 3-year chained run (2010-2012, real crop-driven depletion carried forward, not bare fallow) cuts that to 7.65x -- more than halving the gap, the largest effect any single mechanism has had on this comparison.

Built as a real engine feature the same day, not left as a scratch test: `simulate_season()` gained an `initial_layers` parameter (`cycles_engine_validate.py` and both embedded `ENGINE_SOURCE` copies, verified byte-identical to each other afterward, 48607 bytes each). When given a prior call's own returned `layers` state, a season starts from that real ending moisture instead of always resetting to field capacity; `None` (default) reproduces the exact old behavior byte-for-byte, confirmed via the unchanged full 4-crop validation suite. `_reference_n_demand()`'s own internal precompute pass now always gets an independent `copy.deepcopy()` of whatever's passed in, built into the function itself rather than left to a caller's discipline -- exactly what would have prevented the test-harness bug above. When used, the result dict also carries `final_layers`, so chaining seasons needs no extraction step. Verified three ways: the caller's own passed-in object is never mutated; two calls with the same untouched input are fully deterministic; the real public API (no dispatch hacks) reproduces the exact chained Kansas result already found by hand (1.047 Mg/ha, 7.65x). Not yet done at that point: no UI wiring anywhere (engine-only, matching the "engine first, UI later" pattern from the six-mechanisms session); Rock Springs' own 37-year record hadn't been re-checked against a chained run; the broader multi-site validation effort this thread grew out of hadn't been run -- one site, one crop, one 3-year window, not a systematic check yet.

Validation-first follow-up, per Matt's direct instruction (2026-09-24: "let's use it to improve our model and not yet worry about UI until we make it more accurate"): ran the two natural next tests, both engine-only, no UI. (1) Rock Springs' full 37-year corn record, chained with real carried soil state between every consecutive year (bridged by each year's own real Jan-1-to-planting weather): **no meaningful change** -- correlation 0.554 fresh vs. 0.555 chained. Diagnosed, not left unexplained: Rock Springs gets enough real winter/spring rain that the profile is back near field capacity by planting time regardless of the prior year's ending state, the same "real mechanism, wrong climate to expose it" pattern already seen this session for runoff, spin-up, and the Ksat rate cap. (2) Since Rock Springs had nothing more to say and the one real Kansas point (2012) wasn't enough to know if the earlier 7.65x finding was a fluke, generated 5 more real Cycles reference points at Kansas using the native `Cycles` binary already on this machine (real STATSGO2 soil, same operation file, same one-year-lead-in-spin methodology as the original 2012 reference): 1988/1993/2005/2008/2016, real yields spanning 1.126-4.369 Mg/ha (drought to wet). Our own model, run the same way (fresh-start vs. one-year-lead-in chained) across all six years: carryover improved every single year, not just 2012 -- mean overshoot 5.05x -> 2.16x, mean absolute error 2.30 -> 0.64 Mg/ha, correlation essentially unchanged both ways (0.983/0.977, already high, confirming the model ranks good/bad years correctly even without carryover -- carryover fixes magnitude, not ranking). Real, systematic, out-of-sample evidence, not a one-year coincidence. A 3-year lead-in variant barely improved on the 1-year version (2.16x -> 2.13x, MAE 0.636 -> 0.592) -- most of the benefit comes from just the immediately preceding season; deep multi-year history has sharply diminishing returns, a useful, cheap-to-know fact if this is ever built into a real feature. Honest residual: even chained, the worst case (2012) still overshoots 6.19x -- carryover cuts the Kansas gap substantially but doesn't close it, and more lead-in years barely move that specific number (6.19x -> 6.13x at 3 years), pointing at the still-unresolved "root discovery" artifact (documented above) as a separate, additive problem rather than a soil-history-depth one. Full numbers and methodology in `QUESTIONS_FOR_DEVS.md` item 6. Still not done: no UI wiring (per Matt's explicit instruction); no test yet at Iowa or Maryland; no check of whether carryover helps wheat/soybean/silage corn the way it helps corn.

Root-discovery fix, directly pursued next (2026-09-24, per Matt's "go after the root-discovery fix next"): re-confirmed the anomaly is real by instrumenting Kansas 2012's daily history -- `water_stress` reads a perfect 1.0 (zero stress) for a 20-day span (doy 125-145) during which total real rainfall was 12.5mm, no physical justification. Traced the exact mechanism: by that point the shallow 0.33m layer is nearly at wilting point, but the two deeper layers (0.31m and 0.88m, far more total capacity) are still untouched at field capacity since root_depth just reached them -- the POOLED ratio across all three lands around 0.55-0.60 even with the shallow layer essentially dead, and `water_stress_response()`'s threshold (0.45 for corn) clips any ratio above that straight to zero stress. The bug isn't a brief inflated credit that fades -- a single lumped root-zone reservoir structurally can't tell "some deep water technically exists" from "the crop isn't thirsty."

Tried four further real, distinct mechanisms on top of the two already discarded in the original diagnostic session, each tested against both the Rock Springs 4-crop suite (must not regress) and the new 6-year Kansas benchmark (should actually move the number): a quarter-weighted (FAO-56 40-30-20-10) blend gated by the gap between it and the flat ratio (safe at Rock Springs, unchanged to three decimals, but too weak to matter at Kansas since the threshold-clip swallows small corrections); a hard exclusion of any layer until it measurably depletes below field capacity (dramatic apparent Kansas improvement, 5.05x -> 2.44x, but it roughly HALVED Rock Springs' validated yields across all four crops -- shrinking the effective capacity denominator broadly, not a targeted fix, discarded outright); an elapsed-days step-function delay before a layer counts at all (made BOTH sites steadily worse as the delay grew, since extraction still cascades into "not yet counted" layers regardless, decoupling the feedback -- discarded); and the pure FAO-56 quarter-weighted ratio applied unconditionally (re-confirmed the known Rock Springs regression AND, contrary to a hand-calculation that motivated trying it, made Kansas worse too, 5.05x -> 5.30x -- a real reminder that a plausible one-day estimate doesn't predict a whole season's integrated effect).

One more real, disclosed FAO-56 mechanism (Eq. 8-4, the ETc-adjustment to the depletion fraction -- crops tolerate less depletion under high evaporative demand) was the only one of the six that moved the chained Kansas number in the right direction at all (2012: 6.19x -> 5.98x), but cost real correlation at Rock Springs on three of four crops (corn 0.547 -> 0.528, soybean 0.858 -> 0.843, wheat 0.399 -> 0.377) for a ~1.4% aggregate Kansas gain -- not a trade worth making at its current unconditional strength.

Net standing: six real, mechanistically-motivated fixes tried, tested against two independent benchmarks, none a clean win. The anomaly is real and precisely diagnosed but resists correction via any tested formula adjustment -- the single-lumped-reservoir FAO-56 Ks approach may simply not be expressive enough to separate "water exists somewhere in the root zone" from "the crop can actually use it," especially where capacity sits in a few large, discretely-reached layers rather than distributed smoothly. Carryover remains the one thing that has robustly helped, not by fixing this bug directly but by changing the starting state of the very layers it misjudges. No engine or repo files were changed by this investigation -- all six tests were run against scratch copies; nothing shipped. Full evidence trail in `QUESTIONS_FOR_DEVS.md` item 6.

Real hidden lever found (2026-09-24, per Matt's direct "I want to find the hidden lever(s) that are causing us to be off"), by asking a different question than the previous six attempts: rather than vary the availability formula again, pulled real Cycles' own daily `water.txt` output at Kansas directly (something not yet done for this site) and found layer 3 (the big 0.88m layer) sits at essentially the same value, 0.058-0.061 m3/m3, from the literal first day of the whole 2011-2012 simulation record through to harvest -- including before the 2012 crop is even planted. Checked the ratio precisely against our own computed pwp/fc for this soil: `(theta_initial-pwp)/(fc-pwp)` = 0.463, 0.497, 0.503 for layers 1-3 at the record's actual start (2011-01-01) -- two of three within 0.7% of exactly 0.50. Real Cycles starts a simulation at **half of plant-available water, not field capacity**. Every make_layers()-equivalent in this project (both canonical validation scripts, both embedded engine copies) had always initialized `theta=fc`, a known, disclosed simplification never checked against a real Cycles run whose output began at time zero, because no prior comparison had one.

This explains why six formula-level fixes to `root_zone_availability()` all missed it, and why it's invisible at Rock Springs but dominant at Kansas: the real Jan-1-to-planting bare-fallow spinup every validated run already does gets enough real rain there to erase a 100%-vs-50%-of-available starting difference within weeks, well before planting (confirmed: the full 4-crop suite is byte-identical to three decimals with the fix applied). At Kansas, deep layers never get meaningfully recharged once depleted, so starting at "full" instead of "half" carries phantom water for the entire season, not a transient window. Tested against the same 6-year Kansas benchmark: fresh-start mean overshoot dropped from 5.05x to 3.63x (2012: 18.44x -> 12.49x), MAE 2.30 -> 1.45 Mg/ha, zero cost anywhere -- the first of seven mechanisms tried against this benchmark that's an unambiguous win with no tradeoff to weigh. Doesn't stack much further with one year of carryover (chained ratio unchanged, 2.16x/6.19x) since the spin year's own initial condition gets substantially overwritten by a full season of its own simulated dynamics by the time it ends.

Shipped as `INITIAL_MOISTURE_FRACTION = 0.5` in `cycles_engine_validate.py`, `run_validation.py`, `run_validation_rotation2.py`, and both embedded `ENGINE_SOURCE` copies (confirmed byte-identical to each other afterward, 48919 bytes each; full validation suite reproduces the exact same correlations as before, corn 0.547/soybean 0.858/wheat 0.399/silage corn 0.512). Honest limit: this is a real, substantial hidden lever, not the whole answer -- 2012 still overshoots 12.49x fresh-start, far above the classroom-workable bar, and the separately-diagnosed root-discovery symptom (documented just above) is still unresolved on top of it. What changed is the diagnosis itself: Kansas's overshoot looks less like a within-season dynamics bug and more like a wrong initial condition a humid-climate validation suite could never have surfaced.

Checked at Iowa and Maryland next, per Matt's direct follow-up: real Cycles' own initial SMC confirms the exact same ~50%-of-available pattern at both sites, not a Kansas coincidence -- Iowa's four layers give 0.449/0.474/0.480/0.474, and a fresh native Cycles run built for Maryland (no prior run existed there; built from the already-resolved Othello-series STATSGO2 soil and the committed NLDAS-2 tiles) gives 0.466/0.476/0.486/0.509. Three independent sites, twelve layers, all in a tight band around exactly 0.50. Applying the fix helped at both: Iowa (2 real years) mean overshoot 1.37x -> 1.21x, MAE 1.35 -> 0.75 Mg/ha; Maryland (6 real years, generated fresh for this check) mean overshoot 1.66x -> 1.60x, MAE 2.96 -> 2.80 Mg/ha, a smaller but real gain.

A genuine methodological lesson surfaced along the way, not just a result: the first Maryland pass (4 years) found a striking -0.966 correlation between our model and real Cycles -- looked like a serious, distinct backwards-ranking bug. Checked one hypothesis (vapor pressure deficit inflating growth in humid years) and it pointed the wrong direction, then generated 2 more real Maryland years via the same native-Cycles pipeline rather than trust 4 points -- the correlation collapsed to -0.02/-0.06 (essentially zero) at n=6. The dramatic inverse correlation was a small-sample artifact, not a real signal. What does survive the larger sample: our model's Maryland output barely responds to which year it's given (stdev 0.73 Mg/ha vs. real Cycles' 1.44, less than half the real spread) -- not ranking years backwards, just comparatively flat. Plausible, unconfirmed explanation: Maryland's humid coastal-plain climate rarely lets water become the limiting factor the way Kansas's does every year, so a water-balance-only engine has structurally less to say about what actually drives its real year-to-year variability -- consistent with this engine's explanatory power tracking how water-limited a site's climate is (strongest at Kansas, pre-fix correlation already 0.98+; middling at Rock Springs, 0.55; weak at Maryland). Not something the initial-moisture fix, or likely any water-balance fix, would be expected to close -- flagged as a distinct, probably-structural limitation, not another lever to chase. Full numbers and methodology in `QUESTIONS_FOR_DEVS.md` item 6.

Real, disclosed consequence for `engine-demo.html` specifically: unlike the two canonical validation scripts, its own interactive panels (Run a season, the nitrogen-sweep/frontier panel, the field-comparison panel, the rotation panel) call `simulate_season()` with no `spinup_rows` at all (confirmed by grepping every call site), so their starting condition genuinely changes with this fix -- a direct check found Rock Springs 2012 corn @ N=150 moving from 8.2919 to 8.1832 Mg/ha, a small, sensible ~1.3% reduction, not a red flag. Every specific number already written into this file's history for those panels (the Tab 3 crossover point, the profit-maximizing nitrogen rate, the tillage-compare spreads, the season-risk 37-year averages, and more) was computed before this fix and won't reproduce exactly if re-run now -- the same category of disclosure already made for the FAO-56 depletion-fraction and NET_GROWTH_FRACTION ports; re-verify a given panel's numbers when it's next touched, not before. `model-validation.html` is unaffected in this specific way since its own form exposes `spinup_rows` as a real parameter rather than omitting it. Full evidence trail in `QUESTIONS_FOR_DEVS.md` item 6.

Full paper + SI audit and Eq. 7 built (2026-09-24), per Matt's direct "let's step back... return to the Kemanian paper and SI and make sure we have everything they say," after several sessions of chasing small numerical levers with diminishing returns. Two real methodological upgrades made this possible for the first time: (1) the SI docx's 22 equations are real, parseable OMML XML, not images -- wrote an actual OMML-to-text converter (recursively walking `m:oMath`/`m:f`/`m:sSup`/`m:d`/`m:nary`/etc.) and got a complete, faithful transcript of the whole SI, not the garbled/empty extractions prior sessions produced; (2) re-extracted the main paper's own equations with `pdftotext -layout` instead of reading a rendered page image visually -- layout-preserving extraction keeps exponents correctly attached to their base terms in a way a small rendered image can miss.

This confirmed, directly from the primary source rather than by inference, every sign/exponent error this project had previously suspected -- worth stating plainly since Matt separately recalled Armen mentioning a typo in one of the equations that the devs later fixed, and asked if it's the same thing. Three real, confirmed typos exist: main paper Eq. 1 (the central water-redistribution equation) prints the conductivity exponent as `(2+3b)`, which collapses conductivity to ~0 almost everywhere with realistic soil b-values -- read as `2+3/b` (a lost division slash) it exactly reproduces Campbell's (1974) own well-known K(theta)=Ksat*(theta/theta_sat)^(2b+3), already found and fixed in `campbell_khe()` before this audit. SI Eq. SI.2 (runoff) prints `Win-0.8S` in the denominator, giving negative runoff outright; the standard SCS-CN derivation needs `Win+0.8S`, already fixed in `runoff_mm()`. SI Eq. SI.6 (wet curve number) prints a denominator that goes negative for any CN above ~67 (breaking most real agricultural soils, not an edge case); the corrected `0.4+0.006*CNb` gives sensible values throughout -- already fixed once, then later superseded by a different SWAT+-sourced formula family, which is now flagged as worth testing head-to-head against the SI's own newly-precisely-read structure (CN_dry/CN_wet plus a linearly-interpolated fwc), not reverted this pass. Eq. 1's exponent is the strongest single candidate for what Armen described: it's in the flagship equation of the methods section, dramatic enough that anyone running it would hit it immediately, and has the exact signature of one lost character in typesetting.

Cross-referenced everything else relevant to this engine against both documents and confirmed as already correctly built: the min(GR,GT) growth minimum (Eq. 3-5), the canopy-cover double sigmoid and its exact hardcoded defaults (Eq. 6), potential transpiration (SI Eq. SI.1), shoot partitioning (SI Eq. SI.8), and harvest index (SI Eq. SI.9). Found one genuine, complete, fully-disclosed gap never built: Eq. 7, a plant-density adjustment to canopy cover. Implemented it the same day: `effective_canopy_cover(ei, pdf) = 1-exp(ln(1-ei)*sqrt(pdf))`, applied to `canopy_cover()`'s own output via a new `plant_density_factor` crop parameter (default 1.0, confirmed an exact no-op to full float precision, so every validated crop is unaffected -- full 4-crop suite reproduces byte-identical correlations). Verified the mechanism does something real: sweeping plant_density_factor 0.7-1.6 at Rock Springs 2012 corn (N=650) gives a monotonic, diminishing-returns yield curve (10.02 -> 10.86 Mg/ha), the correct real agronomic pattern, and it composes correctly with tillage. Ported into both embedded `ENGINE_SOURCE` copies (confirmed byte-identical to each other, 49665 bytes each, reproducing the canonical script's exact numbers). No UI wiring -- engine-only, matching the established pattern. Full evidence trail, including the two lower-priority findings (Eq. 2's exact adaptive time-step formula, and a ~20%-in-diffuse-light RUE bonus with no usable input data), in `QUESTIONS_FOR_DEVS.md`'s "Resolved without asking" section.

SI's own CN_dry/CN_wet tested head-to-head against the SWAT-sourced substitute (2026-09-24), per Matt's direct follow-up. Built the SI's literal Eq. SI.5-SI.7 path as a scratch A/B test (both curve numbers now correctly sign-read, plus this project's own pre-SWAT depth-weighted f_wc interpretation, recovered from git history) and ran it against both established benchmarks. Rock Springs: tiny, mixed movements in both directions, none over 0.007 (corn 0.547->0.550, soybean 0.858->0.856, wheat 0.399->0.393, silage corn 0.512->0.519). Kansas (6-year benchmark): essentially identical -- fresh-start mean overshoot 3.63x vs. 3.67x, chained 2.16x both ways. A genuine wash, not a close win either direction. Kept the current SWAT-sourced implementation: with accuracy tied, the deciding factor is that `cn_dry()`/`cn_wet()`/`retention_param_mm()` are ALL fully-disclosed, cited formulas with zero guessed components, while the SI's own Eq. SI.5-SI.7 still needs `f_wc`, which neither source gives an exact formula for. Switching would trade fully-disclosed formulas for a mix of disclosed formulas plus a still-guessed weighting scheme, for no accuracy gain -- not the faithfulness improvement it initially looked like. Nothing shipped; the SI path exists only as a scratch test. Full numbers in `QUESTIONS_FOR_DEVS.md` item 6.

Real attempt to find f_wc's own exact formula (2026-09-24), per Matt's "or we go find f_wc ourselves." Tried eight distinct sources: CropSyst's own manual page on its curve-number method (the strongest lead, since the main paper states Cycles shares biophysical fundamentals with CropSyst -- this water balance was very plausibly inherited near-verbatim); both of Kemanian's own prior papers already sitting locally in this repo (checked directly via pdftotext -- neither describes the water balance beyond naming "runoff" as an output); Williams et al. (2012) itself, already cited in the SI for the slope factor and a strong second candidate for the whole moisture-adjustment mechanism given its title ("...application to continuous runoff simulation"); and Wikipedia's CropSyst page. Every direct fetch came back EGRESS_BLOCKED -- including en.wikipedia.org and ars.usda.gov, neither previously flagged blocked anywhere in this project's history, suggesting this session's network policy is tighter than usual rather than the search being careless.

WebSearch itself did surface one real, modest, useful fact: the continuous-CN literature draws a clear line between a "Revised Soil Moisture Index" method (driven by accumulated ET/climate history) and an "SMCII"-style method (driven directly by actual current soil water content). The SI's own f_wc description ("saturated to a depth of 0.6m... weighted based on depth") is unambiguously the second kind -- confirming the currently-implemented retention_param_mm() (continuous, driven by actual profile SW) is in the right conceptual family, even without the exact depth-weighted formula. Not enough to justify a change on its own. f_wc's exact formula remains a genuine, disclosed-only-in-words gap -- worth retrying if this sandbox's network policy ever loosens, particularly for the CropSyst manual page specifically. Full account in `QUESTIONS_FOR_DEVS.md` item 6.

Instructor view/dashboard: deferred to v1.5. CSV export is interim solution. LMS integration (LTI 1.3) deferred to v1.5. A lesson plan with learning objectives now exists (anthropology/lesson-plan.pdf), written for an intro undergraduate biological anthropology course and framed against AAAS Vision and Change core competencies rather than NGSS (NGSS is K-12 only and doesn't fit that audience). If a K-12 version of this or another unit is built later, it would need its own NGSS-aligned pass. Accounts/login: deferred, but the splash page now has a placeholder for it. This is a real, intended feature, not something Matt has decided against — individual student logins, a teacher-side view, and grade passback into Canvas/Brightspace/Google Classroom are all wanted eventually. LTI 1.3 is the standard mechanism that covers all three LMSs with one integration rather than three separate ones. But per the "Current stage" note at the top of this file, none of that gets built during this proof-of-concept phase — it's explicitly earmarked for the future hired software engineer, not for Claude/Matt to build now. Do not start scaffolding auth, a database, or a backend for this reason; keep new units static and no-login like anthropology and meteorology currently are. Meteorology unit: the core research sandbox and a 5-tab guided investigation are both built and linked live from the splash page (see the dedicated section above) — but an instructor resource page, an end-of-unit assessment, and a poster template (optional end deliverable, explicitly not pulling from logged notebook entries) are all still deferred — see "Planned but not started" in the meteorology section above.

CAMEL folder (external reference material — not an epiSTEMic-built unit)

Added 2026-09-18. `CAMEL/` is a top-level folder holding archived notebooks and planning documents for a separate collaboration, not a guided investigation built on this platform's Pyodide architecture the way the other four units are. Treat it differently from the rest of this file: nothing in it was validated against a real model or built through this project's usual build-then-verify discipline, and it shouldn't be read as a precedent for how a "unit" gets built here.

What it is: Kathy is co-organizing a Data Literacy Summit breakout session ("Modeling the Messy"), built against her own session deck by two external collaborators, Wes and Becca, using a notebook catalog from a separate GitHub repository, `reinhart-group/camel-2dcc` (not part of this codebase — every Colab link in this folder points there, not here). The real data underlying the notebooks is Penn State's 2D Crystal Consortium (2DCC) LiST sample database: 1,005 real, uncleaned records of atomically-thin crystal films grown as candidates for future electronics, covering growth recipe (material, substrate, growth method, time, temperature, pressure) and, for 894 of them, a surface-roughness measurement in nanometres.

Contents of `CAMEL/`:
- `README.md` / `CAMEL/index.html` — the catalog menu: four phone-friendly notebooks needing no Google account or runtime (`warmup.ipynb` "Notice and wonder," 8 items; `mess.ipynb` "Find the mess," 12; `model.ipynb` "Model it," 16; `dials.ipynb` "Turn the dials," 4) plus two that do need a runtime (`surface3d.ipynb`, real microscope scans a viewer can spin — only one live 3D view can exist on the page at once, a Safari/Colab-iframe limit stated in the notebook itself, not a bug; and `python_demo.ipynb`, not archived locally, which rebuilds several catalog widgets in a dozen lines of Python each). `index.html` was built this session as a real static landing page (same visual language as the rest of the site) because `README.md` doesn't render usefully on GitHub Pages; it was briefly linked from the splash page as its own unit card, then unlinked the same day per Kathy's direct request that visitors see only "Modeling the Messy" for now — `CAMEL/index.html` itself is untouched and still reachable by direct URL, just no longer linked from `index.html`.
- `SOURCES.md` — citations and caveats for the curated context tables used across the catalog (chip transistor counts, 2D-material layer/lattice/gap data from Manzeli et al. 2017, superconductor critical temperatures, IEA data-centre electricity figures) — rounded, classroom-appropriate figures, explicitly not LiST data itself.
- `modeling_the_messy.ipynb` — the actual built summit session page: one link, one page, Kathy's four rounds in her own stated order and timings (5/10/12/8 minutes).
- `facilitator-crib.md` — round-by-round facilitator notes: real findings baked into the session (e.g. a single corrupted AFM scan line inflates one sample's recorded roughness from 0.80 nm to 6.10 nm; requiring both a growth-time and a roughness field to be present keeps 740/772 MOCVD samples but only 14/233 hybrid-MBE samples, a missing-data trap) and three specific claims to correct if a team reaches for them (2DCC samples aren't confirmed to be in any shipped chip; "smoother is better" isn't supported by these records; the growth-time/roughness relationship is a tiny, non-causal association).
- `dataset-b-card.md` — corrected replacement text for a summit slide that had promised grain-area data the collection doesn't actually have for most samples (real grain-area measurements exist for only 2 wafers); the measurement that works across the whole collection is surface roughness.
- `2026-09-16-menu-review-round1.md` — an operator's item-by-item review of the warmup/mess notebooks: confirmed causes and fixes for axis-scaling and mislabeled/broken items, plus a couple of real process notes worth not repeating (including a caution about blanket `git add -A` in a working tree shared with running agents).
- `draft-reply-to-kathy.md` — an unsent draft email from Wes and Becca to Kathy. Two real questions are still open inside it: the summit date and deck deadline, and whether they may replace the Dataset B slide card as drafted. Worth actually resolving (e.g. sending the reply) rather than leaving them sitting in an archived file, which is easy to lose track of.

Why this doesn't fit the "unit" pattern used elsewhere in this file: it isn't Pyodide-based (the notebooks run on Colab, not in-browser), wasn't built or validated by Claude working in this repo, and the underlying content (chip/materials-science data literacy) isn't one of Matt's ~6 previously-taught units mentioned in the business/strategy thread above. It's here purely as file storage and visibility for Kathy's own work.

Materials Science / Data Literacy unit ("Modeling the Messy") — new, built this session, not yet presented

Added 2026-09-18, directly after the CAMEL folder above. Unlike CAMEL, this one *is* a real epiSTEMic-pattern guided investigation, at `materials-science/investigation.html` — a fifth top-level unit folder, badged "In Progress" on the splash page (not yet used with a real audience, same convention as engineering/agricultural before their first classroom use). This is now the only materials-science-related card on the splash page: the separate CAMEL Notebook Catalog card was removed the same day per Kathy's request (see the "CAMEL folder" section above), so this investigation is what visitors actually see. Kathy asked for a set of investigations, in the format of `CAMEL/modeling_the_messy.ipynb`, to serve as the interactive portion of a talk she's giving to math educators and the PA Department of Education — introducing data science through real materials-science data, not a coding task about materials science for its own sake.

Two real scoping decisions were made with Kathy directly before any code was written (the same "scope before building" discipline this file's business/strategy and assessment sections both insist on): (1) content = port the six activities already built and fact-checked in `modeling_the_messy.ipynb`, not the broader 40-item catalog and not a new dataset from `SOURCES.md`'s context tables; (2) structure = a multi-tab guided investigation matching anthropology/meteorology's navigation pattern, not `modeling_the_messy.ipynb`'s own single continuous scrolling page.

What was actually done: the six widgets (three-scan 3D warm-up, W-01 unlabelled AFM image, W-02 unlabelled roughness histogram, M-05 material-spelling cleanup, M-02 the growth-method-deleting filter, M-11 extreme-value triage, G-01 growth-time-vs-roughness regression, G-15 claim-check-and-rewrite, G-06 sampling-variability demo, D-01 the three complexity dials — ten widgets total, since Round 1's warm-up scan viewer isn't counted as one of the "six activities" in Kathy's own framing) were extracted verbatim from `modeling_the_messy.ipynb`'s saved cell outputs (real, hand-rolled JS with real embedded 2DCC LiST data baked in — no Pyodide, no external library beyond a lazy-loaded Plotly CDN call for the 3D scans, which already degrades gracefully to a static image when unreachable, exactly like this sandbox's network policy every time it was tested here) and embedded as-is into six tabs (Warm Up / Notice & Wonder / Find the Mess / Model It / Turn the Dials / Reflect) inside new page chrome cloned from meteorology/investigation.html's exact CSS pattern (same fonts, same component classes: `.panel`, `.panel-header`, `.tabs`, etc.) with a new violet/crystal accent palette distinct from every other unit's. Deliberately did not rewrite or "improve" the widgets themselves — that content was already built and iteratively fact-checked against real 2DCC LiST records (see `CAMEL/facilitator-crib.md` and `CAMEL/2026-09-16-menu-review-round1.md` for the verification trail), so porting verbatim carries far less risk than re-deriving it. Only the framing prose per tab is new, written from the notebook's own real markdown cells rather than invented.

One real, necessary change to the ported content: the M-11 widget's table (66 flagged rows) ran unbounded on a full page in a way it never did inside a narrow Colab cell output, stretching that tab to over 6000px tall. Fixed with one additive CSS rule (`.panel-body .cw-scroll{max-height:380px;overflow-y:auto}`) that caps every widget's own table-scroll wrapper — safe because a check confirmed `.cw-scroll` is used only to wrap `<table>` elements across all ten widgets, never a chart, so nothing else could be clipped by it.

Verified in headless Chromium (this sandbox's standing network restriction meant the Plotly CDN call for the 3D warm-up predictably failed here, which was itself the useful test): all six tabs render and switch with zero uncaught JS errors despite ten separate widget scripts each redeclaring the shared `CAMEL` widget-core helper object on one page (safe, since each widget's own IIFE consumes its freshly-redeclared `CAMEL` synchronously before the next widget's script runs and redeclares it again); the 3D warm-up's CDN-unreachable fallback correctly shows the static image with its own built-in "could not reach the 3D library" message rather than crashing; representative interactive elements across different widget types all work (a toggle button in M-02, a reveal button in W-01, a claim dropdown in G-15); the splash page's new unit card links through correctly end to end. Div-tag balance was checked before and after every edit (a real discipline worth keeping for a page this size, assembled by script rather than hand-typed).

Known open items: the badge ("In Progress") and folder name (`materials-science/`, chosen for subject-based naming consistent with anthropology/meteorology/engineering/agricultural, not confirmed by Kathy) are both my calls, not hers — flag before assuming either is final. Whether to eventually expand beyond the six ported activities into the fuller 40-item catalog, and whether this should get its own instructor-facing page the way anthropology and (eventually) meteorology do, are both open and not asked about yet. The actual Gemini/AI-feedback pattern other units use was deliberately not added here — this investigation has no argument-building or AI-graded step, matching what was actually in `modeling_the_messy.ipynb`, not a gap to fill later unless Kathy asks for one.

Overlapping text fixed (2026-09-18, same day, after Kathy reviewed the live page): she reported text rendering on top of other text in several places. Root-caused properly rather than guessed at — an actual overlap detector (a small Playwright script pairwise-checking every `<text>` element's real `getBoundingClientRect()` against every other, since SVG `getBBox()` doesn't account for `transform="rotate(...)"` and gives false readings for rotated labels) run across all six tabs at three viewport widths (390/820/1280px, since the widgets were originally sized for a narrow ~560px-max Colab cell and needed checking at both narrower and wider real page widths). Found real, reproducible collisions on two of the ten widgets, all inside the shared "CAMEL widget core" JS every widget carries its own copy of (`frame()`, `bars()`, `box()`), not the page chrome:
- `box()` (used by M-11 and G-15) never passed `xticks: []` to `frame()` the way `bars()` already did, so `frame()`'s default numeric x-axis ticks (meaningless for a categorical box-plot axis — values like 0, 0.5, 1, 1.5 auto-generated from the group-count domain) rendered directly on top of the real category labels. This is a pre-existing bug in the source notebook's own widget code, invisible inside Colab's narrow cell output, not something the port introduced.
- `box()`'s category labels (e.g. "after your removals (894)") had no rotation or truncation logic at all, unlike `bars()`, which already rotated long/many labels. Gave it the same treatment, steepened further to -90deg (full vertical) since box charts have only 2-4 narrow columns close to the y-axis, where `bars()`'s original -38deg still left enough horizontal reach to hit the axis; added name truncation past 12 characters and extra bottom padding (`frame()` gained an optional `padB` parameter, additive/backward-compatible) to fit the now-vertical text.
- `bars()` itself (M-05's 15-category chart) still let its two longest labels touch even after rotation, since the existing rotate-when-long heuristic didn't scale the angle or font size down for a genuinely dense axis. Steepened to -80deg and dropped to font-9 specifically when item count exceeds 8.
- `frame()`'s offscale note text had no wrapping at all — M-11's note is 63 characters, wider than the chart's own 340px width, so it always overlapped the topmost y-axis tick on a narrow chart regardless of any label-rotation fix. Added word-wrapping with dynamic top padding, general to every chart type that passes a `note`, not just M-11's.

All three fixes are single find-and-replace edits applied identically across the widgets' 9 duplicate copies of the shared core (confirmed byte-identical via hashing before touching anything), not per-widget patches. Re-verified with the same overlap detector after each change: zero collisions remain across every chart in every tab at all three widths, down from 6-11 real pairwise collisions on the affected charts beforehand. Also re-confirmed zero uncaught JS errors and correct div/script/style tag balance. Worth remembering the shape of this bug for any future port of this kind: a widget built and tested only inside a narrow, fixed-width host (a Colab cell, an iframe) can carry latent layout bugs that a full-width real page will expose, and `getBBox()` is the wrong tool to check for it once any rotation is involved — use `getBoundingClientRect()`.

Immediate outstanding issues

FEEDBACK_ENDPOINT in anthropology/investigation.html (Tab 5) and now also engineering/investigation.html (Tab 7) both point at the same deployed Cloudflare Worker (https://epistemic-feedback.mmjohnson0505.workers.dev/, source in cloudflare-worker/worker.js) — it's fully generic ({systemPrompt, userPrompt} → Gemini), no worker changes were needed to add the engineering unit as a third consumer. Confirm the GEMINI_API_KEY secret is set on that worker if feedback stops working in either unit.

Meteorology unit specifics: there is no exposed API key to fix in the original notebook (see the "Important distinction" note in the meteorology section above) — whenever an AI feedback loop does get built for this unit's future open-ended research phase, it will need the same Cloudflare Worker treatment the anthropology unit already has, but that's new-feature work, not a fix. Fine-resolution tiles are global at a fixed 30°×30° granularity generated from the real CERES data; if finer tiles or a different tiling scheme are wanted later, regenerate via the same manifest+gzip pattern (documented in the meteorology section above), not by hand. GitHub's web upload UI has repeatedly proven unreliable for this project's larger binary assets (mis-files them into the wrong folder when uploaded in separate batches, silently drops files over roughly 25MB, and its "create new file" text editor will silently corrupt binary content if it's ever used by mistake instead of the file-upload flow) — GitHub Desktop is the reliable path for anything of meaningful size going forward.

How Matt works

Lead with what is wrong or missing before affirming anything. Provide comparables and explicit tradeoffs. Assume the project succeeds. Do not echo his framing. Stress-test ideas rather than validate them. Never use em dashes. Never create titles in the format "Phrase: longer phrase."

Calibration note (2026-08-31, from direct feedback, apply going forward): the instruction above to lead with what's wrong was landing as antagonistic, not honest. The fix isn't to soften the critique, Matt explicitly still wants the pushback and no glossing. It's to not stop at the critique: every problem or gap identified should be followed, soon after, by a concrete path forward, an actual recommendation, a next step, an option with a real tradeoff to choose between, not just a list of what's wrong left standing on its own. Stress-testing an idea is in service of landing somewhere useful, not an end in itself. Think "honest co-worker who helps find solutions," not "critic."
